Princess Kate Cancer Documentary ‘Heal’ Sets BBC, NBC and International Premiere Dates

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

“Heal,” a documentary from Princess Kate on her cancer diagnosis and living life in the face of serious illness, will debut in the U.K. on Nov. 29, with international premieres to follow.

The documentary will premiere on the BBC in the U.K. on Nov. 29, via NBCUniversal in the United States on Nov. 30, Channel 9 in Australia on Dec. 1 and CTV in Canada on Dec. 7.

The Princess of Wales was diagnosed with cancer in early 2024 and announced she was in remission in January 2025 after undergoing chemotherapy. Related Stories 'Searching for SmarterChild' Filmmakers Investigate Why We Insult Chatbots IDFA Industry Head Says Docs Like ‘Musk’ and ‘NAZA’ Signal That Audiences Want ‘Timely’ Films: They Offer 'Something They Feel They Have to Know’

The documentary follows Princess Kate as she undertakes the National Three Peaks Challenge, climbing the highest mountains in Scotland, England and Wales within 24 hours in support of The Royal Marsden Cancer Charity. During her challenge attempt, she reflects on her experience alongside others navigating their health journeys, exploring the impact that illness has on a person and the people, places and experiences that can aid in healing.

The documentary shines a light on navigating diagnosis, recovery and life beyond cancer. Princess Kate aims to transform access to holistic cancer care that supports resilience and quality of life during and after diagnosis. All funds received during the documentary’s distribution have been directed to The Royal Marsden Cancer Charity to support those living with and beyond a cancer diagnosis.

In the U.S., “Heal” will be available on Nov. 30 at 8 p.m. ET/PT on NBC and at 11:30 p.m. ET/PT on Bravo. It will also be available to stream next day on Peacock.

“Heal” was produced by Detail Films and directed by Benji Jackson.

Kate Middleton Documentary ‘Heal’ Set At BBC & NBC; Special About Princess’ Three Peaks Challenge & Cancer Journey Gets Release Date & Trailer

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Kate Middleton is starring in her first documentary, and it’s a personal one. Heal, which will premiere on the BBC in the UK on November 29 and NBC in the U.S. on November 30, follows the Princess of Wales as she undertakes the National Three Peaks Challenge and reflects on her life as a cancer survivor.

In June, Middleton completed the National Three Peaks Challenge, climbing the highest mountains in Scotland, England and Wales within 24 hours to support The Royal Marsden Cancer Charity. She had a crew filming her for the documentary, the existence of which had not been revealed until now.

In Heal, set against the backdrop of the challenge, Middleton reflects on her cancer experience alongside others who have faced major health issues, discussing the impact serious illness can have on the afflicted person and those around them as well as places and experiences that can help in the healing process while exploring life beyond diagnosis and recovery. Watch on Deadline

You can watch a trailer below. It features Middleton’s brother James, who accompanied her on a climb, and her husband, Prince William, and children George, Charlotte and Louis embracing her at the end of the challenge.

In the U.S., the documentary will run at 8:00 p.m. on NBC and 11:30 p.m. on Bravo on November 30, streaming on Peacock next day. In the UK, it will air on BBC at 6:15 p.m. GMT on November 29. Additionally, Heal is set for release via NBCUniversal on Channel 9 in Australia on December 1 and CTV in Canada on December 7.

Produced by Detail Films, Heal is directed by Benji Jackson. All proceeds from the distribution of the documentary are going to the Royal Marsden Cancer Charity.

This is Middleton’s first time headlining a documentary. Prince William has done it a couple of times, tackling subjects he is passionate about with projects such as Prince William: We Can End Homelessness. He and his brother, Prince Harry, partnered on Diane, Our Mother: Her Life and Legacy almost a decade ago.

Harry and his wife, Meghan Markle, have starred in a number of docuseries under their Netflix deal, most notably Harry & Meghan.

Bayern Munich considering Jamal Musiala sale after health problems

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Musiala was diagnosed with a “neurological dysfunction” after suffering an on-pitch collapse twice in a matter of days during pre-season

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Bayern Munich are considering selling Jamal Musiala over concerns that his ongoing health problems will prevent him from returning to his best form.

Musiala, 23, was diagnosed with a “neurological dysfunction” after suffering an on-pitch collapse twice in a matter of days during pre-season friendlies against Heidenheim and RB Leipzig.

The Germany international said he was “very optimistic... there is no additional health risk beyond this” and indicated the condition would not hamper his highly promising career.

However, Bayern chief Karl-Heinz Rummenigge has now admitted that the club are assessing his future amid a downturn in performances, publicly questioning whether the club can now justify his lofty salary.

Rummenigge told Sky Germany: “He has a health-related issue. It's also well known that he has a contract involving very substantial sums of money. As a club, we have to consider how to deal with that situation. I think that's responsible and perfectly normal.

“What we know unfortunately suggests it will be difficult for him to get back to the level he has often shown here in this stadium.”

Musiala was reportedly Bayern’s best-paid player until Ballon d’Or winner Harry Kane penned a contract extension earlier this month.

He has been limited to just four appearances in the Bundesliga this term due to a muscle injury, scoring one goal and notching one assist, with fitness problems continuing to plague him after spending over half of last season out with a broken leg.

Rummenigge says “a decision will have to be made” over his Musiala if he fails to show signs of improvement, but admitted that the former Chelsea academy product’s health issues would make a sale difficult.

Rummenigge added: “We need to talk to the family, the player, and the agency, because then these sums of money, sorry, with all due respect, are no longer feasible.

“I cannot imagine that Jamal wants to leave the club or that any other club would buy him under these conditions.

“We are all interested in his return, ideally in top form. But what happens if that isn’t the case? Then we still have to think about the finances.

“The discussion hasn’t taken place yet. These are just ideas at this stage.”

Two-Thirds of Dementia Content On TikTok Is Misleading, Researchers Say

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A new survey suggests that about two-thirds of dementia advice videos on TikTok are misleading

Of the videos examined, the analysis shows that nearly half contained inaccurate and potentially damaging information.

The researchers say that approximately 44% of TikTok creators who share dementia content lack credibility and appropriate qualifications.

Although misleading dementia content may appear on other social media platforms, this research examined only TikTok content, warranting further investigation.

The survey was commissioned by Homewatch Caregivers and has not been published in a peer-reviewed scientific journal.

A spokesperson for TikTok told Healthline that if health-related content is potentially harmful, it is flagged for removal. The spokesperson added that 99.6% of misinformation deemed violative was proactively removed from the platform in 2026.

The spokesperson added that TikTok relies on fact-checking and guidance from public health authorities to help assess the accuracy of content. The platform may also apply warning labels to content that cannot be verified as accurate.

Looking to social media for health information is not uncommon. A recent poll reports that 31% of adults in the United States use social media at least once a month to look for health information and advice.

Experts advise seeking information about dementia from trusted sources, such as a primary care doctor and organizations like the National Institute on Aging (NIA) and the Alzheimer’s Association, rather than social media, where content creators who share advice often lack expertise.

“It’s normal to want answers quickly when you’re caring for someone living with dementia, and a short video from someone who seems to know what they’re talking about can be really convincing,” said lead researcher Jennifer Winarski, senior manager of care operations and quality for Homewatch CareGivers.

“Despite there being some genuinely helpful information out there, you don’t always know whether it’s backed by medical evidence, so we wanted to bring our own expertise to uncover whether popular TikToks can be trusted,” she told Healthline. “We hope our report will encourage people to be a bit more careful about what they act on.”

For the survey, researchers selected a sample of 500 of the most-watched dementia-related videos on TikTok with hashtags such as:

Researchers then transcribed the video data to assess its accuracy.

The researchers found that two-thirds (66.4%) of the popular TikTok videos contained misleading information. Of those videos, about half (47.6%) contained potentially damaging misleading dementia information.

“It’s clear that many of the creators mean well, but much of what we saw was personal opinion rather than proven information, and in a few cases, general good health tips were presented as if they were specifically helpful for individuals living with dementia,” Winarski said.

“If families act on that, they could end up spending time and money on things that won’t help — and in the worst of cases, it could delay getting the support their loved one really needs,” she added.

Dung Trinh, MD, an internist for MemorialCare Medical Group and chief medical officer of the Healthy Brain Clinic in Irvine, CA, said it’s important to be aware of the volume of misleading dementia information on social media. Trinh was not involved in this survey.

Some people may be making decisions about their health based on incomplete, misleading, or incorrect information, he said.

“For example, someone might see a video claiming that a particular supplement can reverse Alzheimer’s disease or that certain memory problems automatically mean someone has dementia,” Trinh said.

“These messages can lead to unnecessary fear, false reassurance, wasted money, or delays in getting a proper diagnosis.”

“We are entering an exciting era in Alzheimer’s care, with advances in blood-based biomarkers, earlier detection, and treatments that may slow disease progression in appropriately selected patients,” Trinh continued.

“Getting an accurate diagnosis and receiving timely medical guidance are becoming increasingly important.”

The researchers examined the content creators behind the TikTok videos included in their analysis. They found that 44% of video creators behind the misleading videos had no relevant qualifications.

Rehan Aziz, MD, vice chair of education in the Department of Psychiatry at Hackensack Meridian Jersey Shore University Medical Center and associate professor of psychiatry and neurology at the Hackensack Meridian School of Medicine in New Jersey, said that content creators offering dementia advice should have relevant qualifications. Aziz wasn’t involved in the survey.

“Start by looking at the creator’s profile for a full name and a specific credential, such as MD, DO, PhD, or RN, along with a specialty,” Aziz told Healthline.

“For dementia, the most relevant experts include neurologists, geriatric psychiatrists, geriatricians, neuropsychologists, and nurses, including advanced practice nurses, or social workers who specialize in dementia care.”

“You can confirm a doctor’s license through your state’s licensing board website and check board certification at certificationmatters.org,” Aziz said.

As of 2020, more than 55 million people worldwide were living with dementia — a neurological condition that negatively affects the brain. Researchers predict this number will hit 78 million by 2030.

The increase in the number of people seeking dementia content online is a reflection of this trend.

When looking for accurate dementia information and guidance, Nestor Beltre, MD, a neurologist with Baptist Health Kenneth C. Griffin Neuroscience Institute, part of Baptist Health South Florida, said a great first step is to speak with their primary care physician. Beltre wasn’t involved in the survey.

“Memory changes can occur for many different reasons, and some causes, including vitamin deficiencies, depression, and certain medical conditions, may be treatable,” he told Healthline.

“Early evaluation can help identify what may be contributing to these changes and determine the most appropriate next steps. Depending on the individual’s symptoms, a neurologist, psychiatrist, or neuropsychologist can provide further evaluation and guidance,” Belter continued.

Beltre advised turning to trusted resources such as the Alzheimer’s Association and the National Institute on Aging, which can increase public understanding of memory-related conditions and available treatment options.

“The most important message is that patients and families have resources available to help them better understand and manage cognitive health,” he said.

Who can England face in Nations League quarter-finals? Opponents confirmed

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

England are gunning for glory in the Nations League having qualified for the quarter-finals as group winners ahead of world champions Spain.

Including its inaugural season in 2018/19, the Three Lions have competed in every single Nations League campaign, experiencing some highs and the lowest of lows.

From topping their group and qualifying for the Nations League finals and finishing third to suffering a shock relegation in 2022/23 and then immediate promotion, England have done almost everything except win the competition.

They will hope that changes next summer, and a remarkable win away in Spain last week will, potentially, secure, an easier quarter-final tie next March. The draw is this morning in Nyon.

The four group winners will face the four runners-up in a two-legged Nations League quarter-final.

The group winners will all be seeded and will have home advantage in their respective second leg ties. Teams from the same group cannot be paired together in the knockout stage, therefore England and Spain - for example - will be kept apart.

As a result of finishing top in Group A3, England’s confirmed potential Nations League quarter-final opponent are Belgium and the Netherlands after they were pipped to top spot in Group A1 and A2 by France and Germany respectively.

England’s remaining possible quarter-final opponents was revealed on Tuesday night as Norway produced a stunning comeback from 2-0 down pip Denmark to the runners-up spot behind Portugal in Group A4 by virtue of a superior head-to-head record.

The draw for the 2026/27 Nations League quarter-final will take place on Friday, November 20 with the ties scheduled to take place in March 2027.

When is Nations League draw? Start time today and confirmed pots as England learn quarter-final fate

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The draw for the 2026-27 Nations League quarter-finals is upon us, with England joined by seven other nations in the pot.

Thomas Tuchel’s side enjoying a near perfect group phase, which culminated with a stunning 3-2 win in Spain to top Group A3 at their host’s expense, and that means they are likely to avoid another European heavyweight in the quarter-finals.

Elsewhere, France sealed top spot in Group A1 with a win over Turkey, while Belgium thumped Italy 4-1 to finish as runners-up.

In A2, Germany topped the table with a 5-0 thrashing of the Netherlands, who slipped to second on goal difference after such a swing.

Portugal’s position was Group A4 was confirmed after their win over Wales on Tuesday, and it is Norway joining them in the knockout phases following a remarkable comeback win against Denmark.

The Nations League draw for the 2026/27 quarter-finals will take place today, Friday, November 20.

As well as the quarter-final draw, the promotion/relegation play-offs draw will also be conducted.

Coverage of the draw is due to start at 11am GMT, with the draw itself expected to begin shortly afterwards.

The Nations League draw will take place in Nyon, Switzerland.

TV channel: In the UK, the draw will be televised live UEFA TV.

Live stream: You can also stream the draw for free online via the UEFA YouTube page.

Live blog: You can follow the draw as it happens on the day through Standard Sport’s live blog.

The four group winners will face the four runners-up in a two-legged Nations League quarter-final.

The group winners will all be seeded and will have home advantage in their respective second leg ties. Teams from the same group cannot be paired together in the knockout stage, therefore England and Spain - for example - will be kept apart.

The four group winners will be seeded in Pot 1, with the four runners-up will be unseeded in Pot 2.

Pot 1: France, England, Germany, Portugal

Pot 2: Belgium, Spain, Netherlands, Norway

The Nations League quarter-finals, as well as the League A/B and League B/C promotion/relegation play-offs, will be played in March 2027.

The first legs are scheduled for March 25-27, with the return legs scheduled for March 28-30.

The finals tournament is scheduled for June 2027.

STAT+: UnitedHealth sues to reverse downgraded Medicare Advantage quality scores

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We ranked the 25 most valuable publicly traded Chinese drugmakers

Bob covers the money in health care, focusing on health insurance and hospitals. His stories delve into Medicare Advantage, opaque prescription drug benefits, and how much executives actually make. He is also the author of the Health Care Inc. newsletter. You can reach Bob on Signal at bobjherman.09.

UnitedHealth Group has sued the federal government over its lowered Medicare Advantage star ratings for next year, further plunging the program’s ratings system into disarray.

UnitedHealth marks at least the 15th Medicare Advantage plan to file a lawsuit against the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services for how the agency calculated 2027 star ratings. UnitedHealth is also the largest Medicare Advantage insurer, covering more than 9 million people. Medicare’s annual enrollment period ends Dec. 7.

The government rates Medicare Advantage plans on a scale from one to five stars, based on health screens, customer satisfaction, and other measures that get mixed into a complicated formula. Those with at least four stars earn bonus money. The insurers argue in their lawsuits that the government’s measurement is inconsistent and is inappropriately dropping their scores below the bonus thresholds.

Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: Why One Big Meal Isn’t the Real Holiday Health Problem

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

In the classic Thanksgiving comedy Planes, Trains and Automobiles, hapless hero Del Griffith (played by the late, great John Candy) delivers an emotional riposte to Steve Martin’s uptight ad man Neal Page that stands as a beautifully dignified defense of kindness, empathy and self-love in the face of his own perceived shortcomings.

“You want to hurt me? Go right ahead if it makes you feel any better…I could be a cold, hard cynic like you. But I don’t like to hurt people’s feelings. You think what you want about me, I’m not changing. I… I like me. My wife likes me…because I’m the real article. What you see is what you get.”

For many of us around the holidays, particularly after a night of excess, Del and Neal’s exchange could read as our internal monologue – your kinder inner voice battling the internal bully who judges your every overindulgence. The jury’s out on whether the kind voice is ever as potent as Del’s is in that moment. But it should be, because when it comes to your health around the holidays, the bully has usually got it wrong.

The truth is, for most of us, one day of immoderation during Thanksgiving is unlikely to matter in the grand scheme. As such, you would do well to avoid the litany of holiday season health myths out there, many of which seem hell-bent on punishing you for taking a day off:

Like all misinformation, these kinds of medical myths are loud and tend to die hard, but they almost always trade on quick fixes that target the wrong thing.

A 2020 review of 23 observational studies found that most showed significant weight gain during festive periods, averaging roughly a pound and a half. One of the original studies, a landmark NIH study from 2000, found that much of that weight tends to stick around. So the question is not what you ate on Thanksgiving Thursday and how quickly you can “undo the damage,” but rather whether the extended holiday season becomes a two-month period where your healthy routine goes out the window. Some things to look out for on that front:

None of this is a free pass for everyone. If you manage a health condition, you likely have less margin for error, even when talking about a single meal or a drink. Critical factors include timing, sodium intake, alcohol intake, and missed medication. A few non-exhaustive examples:

Whatever condition you’re managing, the move for all of them remains the same – be prepared. Check your plan with your care team before the holiday, not after, and call them if something feels off afterward.

Technically, feeling good on Black Friday starts on Thursday. A short and light walk after a feast can help smooth out post-meal blood sugar – the earlier the better, ideally within an hour of eating. Staying upright for a couple of hours to help with digestion, particularly if you’re prone to reflux, is also a great idea. If you’re on medication, remember to take it on time. And, at the risk of sounding prudish, and this goes for everyone: try to get to bed at a sensible hour.

Above all else, Thanksgiving is a holiday built around gratitude and kindness, so channel your inner Del Griffith and remember to be both grateful and kind, to yourself and to those around you – evidence suggests that’s also good for your health.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Wednesday in our Healthline Wellness Wire newsletter.

Phone apps list in GTA 6

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Every app that you can have on your phone

Every app that you can have on your phone

Phone apps in Grand Theft Auto 6 serve a purpose, but some are far more useful than others. Scooter Bros and RydeMe are among the best, whereas Snapmatic is mostly very disturbing. Nonetheless, you'd probably like to unlock them all.

Here's how to use your phone in GTA 6, including a list of phone apps.

To pull out your phone in GTA 6, press the up arrow on the D-pad. If you hold the arrow instead, you'll get a shortcut to the WAiNK vehicle scanner (handy when you're in a hurry and need a getaway car). Most phone apps are unlocked during the main story, so if you're wondering why you don't have the RydeMe app yet, you must simply keep playing.

Here's an overview of every GTA 6 app we've discovered so far, organized by how they appear on your phone. Additionally, we've listed each app's purpose and how to unlock them.

This is your messaging app, similar to WhatsApp.

This is a vehicle delivery service. You can pay to have your car or bike instantly delivered to your location. The higher the distance and vehicle damage, the higher the price.

It's a taxi service like Uber. Select a destination, pay the fee, and a car will come to pick you up. You can use this app to fast travel.

Unlocked during the "Buck Me!" main mission, which begins shortly after Lucia comes home in Chapter 1.

A list of phone numbers. You can call almost everyone you've ever met, but if you call someone randomly, you usually get their voicemail. You'll be using the WhatUp app for most of your communication.

You can use it to take selfies and send them to your partner.

This is your banking app. Deposit your cash at an ATM to add it to your BuckMe account. Your BuckMe checking account is used to make payments, while your Family Savings account is linked to main mission progress (you can't withdraw it).

Unlocked during the "Buck Me!" main mission, which begins shortly after Lucia comes home in Chapter 1.

This app tracks your strength, cardio, and focus levels, which in turn determine your character's health, stamina, and focus stats. Complete workout sessions to increase your fitness levels.

A selection of photos you've made with your camera app, plus photos that have been sent to you (usually as part of a mission).

This is a social media app similar to Instagram. You will see your friends and partners in crime post new photos and life updates all the time. It's of little practical use, though.

Read news articles about events in and around Vice City. They often report on crimes that you may have been involved in.

The weather forecast. It can be useful to check this if, for example, you're planning on a fishing trip (best to avoid doing so in a storm).

Use this app to purchase garages around Leonida, which increases the number of vehicles you can keep. You can't buy your own home, though.

After completing Moses' first quest in Chapter 2, steal the '82 Crawler for him to unlock Vice City Real Estate.

This app allows you to scan vehicles, which instantly tells you their type and security details. So, before you steal a car, use the WAiNK app to check whether the car has an alarm or tracker.

Here you can change your phone's wallpaper and ringtone.

Ancestry Launches Black Friday Deal — Save Up to 70%

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

If you purchase an independently reviewed product or service through a link on our website, Rolling Stone may receive an affiliate commission.

Genetic testing kits are popular for a number of reasons — some people are looking to screen for health conditions, while others want to learn more about their family. Ancestry has become one of the most popular options in the latter category, allowing users to connect with family members and build their own family trees.

Ancestry can also make a surprisingly thoughtful gift. Right now, Ancestry is offering its gift memberships for 50% off ahead of the holiday season. There are three tiers to choose from, all of which are discounted right now. Here’s what to know about Ancestry’s deals.

Ancestry’s DNA gift subscriptions are broken into U.S. Discovery, World Explorer, and All Access tiers, each of which is available for 50% off for a one-year plan. U.S. Discovery includes access to records from across all 50 states, while World Explorer includes access to U.S. and international records from over 80 countries. All Access, meanwhile, adds access to military records on Fold3 and articles on Newspapers.com.

The memberships include access to family records, newspaper clippings, and more, but they do not include the genetic testing kit itself. The kits are discounted during Black Friday as well, with discounts of up to 70% off. The test gets you a detailed origins report and connections to genetic relatives, including a pie chart breaking down your ancestral origins.

The results can also break down family origins into specific regions, even down to cities in some cases. Results can potentially include a timeline of familial migration, sometimes down to when and even why your family moved.

Ancestry’s kit is also available with the World Explorer membership or the All Access membership, and both bundles are currently discounted. Users can also protect their privacy in a number of ways; you can choose not to opt in to DNA matching, and you can also request to have your data deleted and your sample destroyed.

If you’re looking for a unique gift, Ancestry is worth considering. But these deals are limited; Ancestry’s gift membership promo ends on Dec. 1, while the 70% off deal on the Ancestry kit ends on Dec. 31.

Space station horror-FPS Derelikt is a lively retro throwback to Alien: Resurrection, though it's better at shooting than scaring

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

I'm a reasonable man. I don't expect every PS1-styled, cosmic horror, nakedly Alien: Resurrection-homaging retro FPS to suffocate me under a blanket of misery. Some of the lighter moments in Derelikt, which released last week, even land: I was grinning my cheeks off as I gorged on the Eldtrich knowledge sealed within an alien obelisk, while repeatedly hanging up on the aghast scientist who kept videocalling me to stop.

Still, Derelikt is fortunate that it's a good shooter, as it often gets muddled on whether it wants to be a horror game as well. It certainly threatens the spooks, trapping you in an oppressively dingy asteroid rig in the orbit of Saturn - the scariest of the ringed gas giants! - and warping most of the other staff into vaguely echinodemic monsterpeople. Also, opening the inventory reveals it to be made of rectangles, a classic symptom of a video game character about to get mangled in the nerves and face.

But nah, shotgun go boom boom. It turns out Derelikt's derelict dwellers are both perfectly susceptible to mundane lead, and sparing users of ambush tactics at most; several even stand around with their backs turned, as if waiting for you to surprise them with a lethal pipe swing. And, even on Medium difficulty, the opening spell of fight-or-run scarcity quickly gives way to an abundance of ammo and health pickups that can easily fill the pocket grid to bursting. I don't claim to have been playing perfectly, but I'm about halfway through and the only way I see myself running low on bullets is if I start using them to punch the complete works of Thomas Campion into a wall.

It's indecisive tonally, as well. The dialogue, and there are a fair few survivors to chat with, seems interested in emulating the gravitas-starved, "Woah man" style of New Millennium action game scripts. Which I can sort of see the logic behind, given the visuals and sound design also, and arguably much more painstakingly, mimic that era. But the downside remains that it ends up with a gravitas-starved, "Woah man" style, just with an extra dash of sideways-winking awareness. Again, it only sometimes works, and it's never scary or eerie. But then there are also more earnest attempts to spin uneasiness, with pages of deranged scribbles left behind by the dwindling crew, and at least a couple of genre-classic wall daubings, which tend to leave me wishing developers Visuwyg had just picked a lane.

Again, though, underneath all the smartarsing is a quality little shooter. The retro presentation does little to soften the heavy, industrial kick of its gunfire, and although many of the meatier freaks are more absorbent than they look, their pained thrashing really helps sell the impact of every individual shot. I also don't really mind the lack of Dead Spacey surprise attacks, as the station's narrow confines tend to force you into tenser, close-range encounters in an organic way anyhow.

More impressive still is how Derelikt manages to fabricate a cold, apprehensive atmosphere befitting its fallen space base, in direct defiance of its own quasi-comedic script. This is mainly thanks to the clanging, low-lit station itself, which teases you with just enough flashes of comfort – a stretch of marble floor here, a messy rec room there – to make your quick return to its steel guts feel all the more unpleasant. Some credit must also go to the puzzling, the cardkey hunts and code searches of which initially had me fearful of the more obstinate, opaque, classic horror style preferred by fellow backwards-looker Silver Pines. Instead, it's more like a compacted Deus Ex, hinting at the need to peek at people's computers and scour the environments for the needed codes. Which makes for generally smoother progress, yes, but also gets you more involved and present inside Derelikt's infested walls.

Midway-through impressions, then: it's a little silly, and perhaps a little confused, but Derelikt finds its focus whenever something needs making dead. I shall continue blasting xenos and burning my eyes out with magic rocks until – no, madam, please hold – further notice.

Planning to Stop Taking a GLP-1? Here’s How to Maintain Weight Loss

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Switching GLP-1 medications or undergoing bariatric surgery may help people maintain greater weight loss than stopping treatment entirely, according to a new study presented at ObesityWeek and published in eClinicalMedicine on November 14.

The Cleveland Clinic study compared five strategies for managing weight after treatment with GLP-1 medications, analyzing data from 20,486 adults with obesity or type 2 diabetes.

People who stop taking GLP-1 medications often regain weight. Researchers examined whether continuing treatment, switching medications, or undergoing bariatric surgery could help limit weight regain or support further weight loss.

The findings showed that people who underwent bariatric surgery or switched between semaglutide (Ozempic) and tirzepatide (Zepbound) experienced the greatest overall weight loss.

The study participants had an average age of 56.7 years, an average weight of 109 kg (240.3 lbs), and an average body mass index (BMI) of 38. All had been taking either semaglutide or tirzepatide for at least 90 days.

The researchers categorized the participants into five groups:

After 2 years, those who underwent bariatric surgery saw an average loss of 28.2% of their body weight or about 31.5 kg (69.4 lbs).

“We increasingly view medications and surgery as complementary tools rather than competing ones,” said Mir Ali, MD, medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study.

“For patients with severe obesity (typically a BMI of 35 or higher, or 30+ with metabolic conditions like type 2 diabetes), bariatric surgery remains the most durable, cost-effective intervention available,” he added. “Using a GLP-1 beforehand can safely reduce liver volume and lower surgical risk, while surgery provides the permanent anatomical and hormonal reset needed to maintain that weight loss long-term without relying indefinitely on weekly injections.”

During the same period, those who switched between semaglutide and tirzepatide lost 16% of their body weight, or about 18 kg (39.6 lbs).

Participants who switched from semaglutide to tirzepatide lost 3 kg (6.6 lbs) more than those who continued taking semaglutide. However, participants already taking tirzepatide lost 4.5 kg (9.9 lbs) more by continuing treatment rather than switching to semaglutide.

The other groups experienced lower amounts of weight loss:

After one year, participants with type 2 diabetes experienced HbA1c reductions of 1.76% following bariatric surgery, 1.43% after switching GLP-1 medications, and 1.32% after continuing their original medication, compared with 0.48% among those who discontinued treatment entirely.

After one year, participants with hypertension (high blood pressure) also experienced greater reductions in blood pressure following surgery or switching GLP-1 medications than those who discontinued treatment entirely.

“[This study] reinforces the need for a long term, multi-modal continuum of care rather than treating GLP-1s as a temporary fix,” said Ali.

Ali also recommended the following strategies to help maintain weight loss after stopping a GLP-1 medication:

The findings highlight the importance of having a long-term weight management plan in place before stopping a GLP-1 medication.

Before stopping a GLP-1 medication, talk with a healthcare professional. They can help you explore your options and determine which strategies may be most appropriate for your health needs.

Ali told Healthline that identifying the reason for stopping treatment is an important first step. He noted that people often discontinue GLP-1 medications because of gastrointestinal side effects, supply issues, or loss of insurance coverage rather than because they no longer need treatment.

He said that if side effects are the issue, adjusting the dose or switching to a different agent often solves the problem.

Ali also emphasized that stopping a GLP-1 medication shouldn’t be viewed as a test of willpower. Increased hunger after discontinuing treatment is a biological response, not a personal failure.

“Have a concrete ‘off-ramp’ plan in place with your doctor before taking your last dose, so you have medical, nutritional, or surgical support ready to protect the health gains you worked hard to achieve,” advised Ali.

The "shameful" measles data that lost the US its elimination status

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Measles became endemic in the US months ago—and it’s only accelerating

International health authorities formally announced on Tuesday that the US has lost its measles elimination status, a hard-fought public health triumph won in 2000 after decades-long vaccination efforts. The loss had been widely expected by public health experts for months, and the decision was leaked by the press last week. Still, having the designation officially stripped stings.

After the announcement Tuesday, public health experts, doctors, and political leaders decried the dramatic backslide for the US, calling it “shameful” and a “national embarrassment.” News articles point to the staggering vertical rise of measles cases this year, currently over 5,400 cases, more than any other year since 1991. An explosive measles outbreak in Pennsylvania accounts for over 1,500 of those cases. There have been 10 confirmed deaths across three states, several deaths just in the last week.

But the measles data that caused the US to lose its status—the bits of it that are public at least—paint a darker picture of where the US is in its rematch with measles, the most contagious virus known on Earth. For one thing, almost none of the data from this year—the cases, the explosive Pennsylvania outbreak, the deaths—factored in to the loss. It was lost earlier. What’s happening now is a runaway epidemic.

The US measles elimination status is a designation granted by the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), which is a regional division of the World Health Organization. Last week a committee of experts assembled by the PAHO, called the RVC, met to review measles data for countries across the region, including the US. (RVC is short for the Measles, Rubella and Congenital Rubella Syndrome Elimination Regional Monitoring and Re-Verification Commission.)

For their review of the US data, the RVC heard from an independent committee of experts from the US, called the NSC, which presented measles data collected from January 2025 onward. (The NSC is short for the US National Sustainability Committee for the Elimination of Measles, Rubella, and Congenital Rubella Syndrome.)

According to the PAHO, an elimination status is lost when epidemiological and laboratory data shows that a single viral lineage has spread continuously for at least 12 months within a country. At this point, the virus is considered to be reestablished or endemic. So, to withdraw the US elimination status, the RVC needed to see evidence that a single introduction of a measles virus led to a 12-month-long chain of cases, which requires a lot of data.

The full set of data the NSC presented to the RVC is private for now. The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention needs to review and approve such data for public release, which can be a lengthy process that has not yet occurred.

The Department of Health and Human Services, which oversees the CDC, did not respond to a question from Ars Technica regarding when the data may be released.

But Ars Technica spoke with the chair of the NSC, Noel Brewer, who tried to talk through the pieces of data in the NSC report to the PAHO that he could discuss—these are pieces that have already been made public at some point by others, such as by state health officials or researchers. Brewer said the NSC presented the PAHO with genetic data spanning January 2025 to January 2026, and also epidemiological data that extended to June 2026.

In a press briefing Tuesday, officials for the PAHO made clear that only some of the data presented by NSC factored into its assessment of the US elimination status. The RVC only looked at the reporting period between January 2025 and January 2026, which doesn’t include the massive outbreak ongoing in Pennsylvania, which began in April. “The number of cases in Pennsylvania did not actually contribute to the decision that was made by the Reverification Commission,” Rhonda Sealey-Thomas, PAHO’s assistant director, explicitly told reporters Tuesday. For 2025, the US reported 2,289 cases nationally.

Still, there was no ambiguity about the US losing its status; “multiple lines of data show clearly that the US has sustained transmission of measles,” Brewer told Ars.

Brewer, who is also the Gillings Distinguished Professor in Public Health at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, confirmed that the beginning of the end of US elimination was January 20, 2025, in Texas—that date was included in the letter PAHO sent to the US informing it of the loss of elimination status. That Texas outbreak was declared over on August 18, 2025, after 762 cases were confirmed in the state. But the virus carried on elsewhere and is still simmering.

“Since January 20, 2025, the US, Canada, and Mexico have been passing measles cases back and forth across borders,” Brewer said, and the national data shows multiple, “genetically distinct” measles introductions into the US. “However, there is one genomic lineage that predominates across the cases and ties many of them, the majority of them, together,” he said.

For people closely tracking the US measles status, a key question has been whether the big outbreaks that followed the initial one in Texas were clearly linked. If they were, it would be immediately obvious that the US had lost the elimination status; but if not, and the outbreaks were driven by different virus introductions, the US could have kept its measles-free status based on the technicality of how the status is defined.

Data that might hold the answer to this question has been elusive. The measles strain in Texas, D8-9171, appeared similar to the viruses seen subsequently in big outbreaks in Arizona and Utah. But, the strain was also similar to those from Mexico and Canada. Generally, there can be relatively small genetic changes among measles viruses, and genetic data alone is not used by PAHO to evaluate elimination. In June of this year, ProPublica tried to get at the answer itself, conducting its own genetic analysis of measles strains in the US. It concluded that “the measles virus still spreading in Utah as of this May is very closely related to the one that sickened Texans over a year ago.” But the outlet acknowledged this wasn’t a “smoking gun.”

To date, the question lingers. Brewer cannot yet explicitly disclose what the NSC reported to the PAHO about possible links between the Texas outbreak and any of the subsequent large outbreaks in 2025. That includes the one in nearby Utah or one in South Carolina, which began in October 2025 and ultimately tallied 997 cases by the time it was declared over in April, 2026.

For its part, the PAHO did not respond to multiple requests for comment from Ars Technica on linkages between the outbreaks.

But, in a twist, Brewer noted that the answer to the question might be moot; the lineage that started in Texas on January 20 might not be the only one that has been sustained and could have lost the country its status.  There are “one or two that have gone on for more than 12 months,” he said.

With the designation now formally lost, health and medical experts are coming to grips with the reality that measles is once again endemic in the US. Details of how the US should move forward are up for debate; the US is now required to submit a “corrective action plan” to the PAHO, outlining how the country intends to regain its elimination status. But, the fundamental solution is obvious and straightforward: The US needs to increase vaccination rates.

While the virus has been raging in religious communities with low vaccination rates, the entire US has seen slipping vaccination coverage. That makes it easier for the virus to gain a foothold virtually anywhere.

A protective vaccination rate is considered to be 95 percent; according to the latest data, only 92.4 percent of kindergartners were vaccinated with the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine in the 2025–2026 school year. Only 10 states have MMR vaccination rates at or above the 95 percent threshold. Meanwhile, non-medical exemptions from school vaccination requirements have never been higher, at 4 percent. Currently, 24 states have exemption rates at or above 5 percent, meaning that even if they are able to vaccinate every single non-exempt child, they would still not meet the 95 percent target. And this is just the data for young children. The vaccination coverage for adults is another concern, and the data there is scarce. In Pennsylvania’s massive outbreak, 1,055 of the 1,561 cases this year have been in adults ages 18 and older (less than 1 percent of the cases were fully vaccinated).

While some health officials have noted optimistically that 92.4 percent in children is just a few percentage points from the target, the reality of bringing up vaccination rates is more onerous.

PAHO Director Jarbas Barbosa stressed the point in the press briefing Tuesday: It’s “not the national average or the average in the state or even the average in a big city, you need to guarantee that in every community, village, you have this vaccination coverage up to 95 percent. This is very important,” Barbosa said.

The point has been demonstrated in the US. In 2019, the US came very close to losing its measles elimination status. Unlike now—when the virus is on a nationwide rampage, with 47 states and jurisdictions reporting cases—in 2019, the US nearly lost its status from a single, large outbreak that spread in New York. Though it didn’t explode on a national level, it took 11 months to stamp out in those under-vaccinated communities. In that year, the US reported 1,274 measles cases total.

The situation in the US has only gotten worse, and the outlook is bleak, Brewer warns. “The measles outbreaks in Canada and Mexico are winding down,” he noted. “However, in the US, our measles problem continues to accelerate. There is no end in sight.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

As overdose deaths fall, White House makes misleading claims about who deserves credit

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Families look on as President Donald Trump speaks during a ceremony to sign the "Halt All Lethal Trafficking of Fentanyl Act" in the East Room of the White House on July 16, 2025. Evan Vucci/AP hide caption

When the White House announced big improvements in the number of people dying from fatal drug overdoses earlier this week, officials pointed to hopeful new data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention showing U.S. drug deaths have plunged to their lowest level in roughly a decade, saving nearly 50,000 lives each year.

But leading drug policy researchers say the White House statement also made misleading claims about the timing and cause of the remarkable public health success when it said that gains occurred primarily over the last 18 months due to policies enacted under President Trump's leadership.

"The historic decline in drug overdose deaths demonstrates that America is unequivocally winning the fight against illicit drugs," White House Office of National Drug Control Policy director Sara Carter said in a statement.

Carter credited heightened border security under Trump and claims of success dismantling "narco-terrorist" organizations for the recovery. "Critically, this represents a an approximately 41.5% decrease in total drug overdose deaths compared to the peak under the previous administration," the statement said.

What the White House failed to disclose is that declines in overdose fatalities began much earlier, under the Biden administration, with most of the gains occurring before Trump returned to office.

"Since the spring of 2023, we have seen a steady decline in overdose deaths," said Vanda Felbab-Brown, a drug policy expert at the Brookings Institution. "The fact that it has been sustained is very important."

According to CDC data, reductions in drug deaths began in 2023 and have continued steadily for at least 39 consecutive months — not 18 months as the ONDCP statement said. By the time Biden left office, fatal overdoses had already dropped by roughly one-third, an improvement described by addiction experts at the time as unprecedented.

Nabarun Dasgupta, a street drug researcher at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, was one of the first scientists to detect the stunning drop in overdose deaths. He said the trend toward fewer deaths began in some regions and among some demographic groups as early as 2021 and rapidly gained momentum nationwide.

"It was really the period early 2024 through spring 2025 where we were seeing the greatest rate of decline that we've ever seen," Dasgupta said.

Deaths from fentanyl smuggled into the U.S. from Mexico first began to rise in 2013 during President Obama's time in office and accelerated during Trump's first term. Fentanyl overdoses spiked sharply the first two years of Biden's presidency, during the COVID-19 pandemic. Despite progress, fentanyl remains the leading cause of drug deaths nationwide.

Dasgupta, Felbab-Brown and other and addiction experts credit the Biden administration for rolling out policy responses that included wider access to healthcare, making the opioid overdose medication naloxone more readily available, and negotiating a deal with China that disrupted the flow of industrial chemicals used to make fentanyl.

President Joe Biden met China's President President Xi Jinping in Woodside, Calif., in November 2023, on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperative conference. China agreed to curtail shipments of the chemicals used to make fentanyl, the drug at the heart of the U.S. overdose epidemic. Doug Mills/Pool/The New York Times hide caption

The Biden team also scored significant drug interdiction wins against the Mexican cartels that smuggle fentanyl. "We have certainly been seeing over many years, going back to the Biden administration, important efforts to crack down against them," said Felbab-Brown.

Dasgupta and Felbab-Brown both said improvements in reducing drug mortality have continued under Trump. They said it's important to have an accurate timeline of the overdose crisis because researchers are still trying to understand why deaths involving fentanyl surged and why so many fewer people are now dying.

NPR asked the White House about progress in the fentanyl fight under Biden. In an emailed statement, ONDCP director Carter, a former podcaster, journalist and Fox News contributor, blamed Biden for failing to secure the U.S.-Mexico border and for allowing drug deaths to surge in the first place.

"While drug overdose deaths eventually began to decline following the peak, the Biden Administration doesn't get to take credit for overseeing the highest drug overdose numbers in history, then bringing them down from their own failure," Carter said.

Responding to CDC data showing drug deaths began to plummet much earlier than stated the White House announcement, Carter said "this could be misinterpreted as progress on a graph, but it was not progress."

According to Carter, the Trump administration is now well-positioned to build on overdose death reductions made over the last three years, in part by maintaining border security and by continuing to "relentlessly attack" drug gangs.

"We are dismantling and eliminating the violent cartels and drug trafficking organizations through coordinated operations," she said.

But Felbab-Brown at Brookings said she doesn't share the Trump administration's confidence that there has been significant progress "dismantling and eliminating" narco-terrorist organizations.

"I cannot think of one narco-terrorist organization that has been dismantled," she said. "They all continue operating and they are all very strong. They all continue smuggling drugs."

Military experts have also questioned the effectiveness, and legality, of Trump's ongoing strikes against civilian boats in the Caribbean and eastern Pacific, described as smuggling vessels by the Pentagon.

Dasgupta, the addiction researcher at UNC, said he's also concerned about whether progress reducing drug deaths can be sustained next year as the Trump administration implements deep cuts to Medicaid passed by the Republican Congress.

The Congressional Budget Office has estimated the cuts could mean as many as 10 million Americans losing access to health insurance coverage.

"Medicaid is the largest funder of addiction services in the United States," Dasgupta said. "Cutting Medicaid eligibility or making it harder to access forms of effective addiction treatment is guaranteed to leave people out in the cold."

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

The Most Common Fatal Diseases in Cats, and How to Avoid Them

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Catster is reader-supported. When you buy via links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission at no cost to you. Learn more.

Nobody enjoys thinking about serious illness in a pet, and itâs natural to want to look the other way. But considering many serious feline health conditions respond well to early intervention, a little knowledge can go a long way. Here are eight of the most serious, what they tend to look like, and practical steps that can help lower the risk. The aim isnât to worry anyone, just to help owners feel prepared.

Heart problems are notoriously hard to spot in cats, even during a routine health check, because the usual signs, like a murmur, donât always show up during an exam. The most common form of cardiac disease â hypertrophic cardiomyopathy â causes the heart muscle to thicken so it canât work as well, and it can cause sudden death. Vets rely on heart scans, chest X-rays, and blood tests to discover heart disease.

Lowering the risk: Genetics play a big part, so anyone buying a kitten from a breeder should ask whether the parentsâ hearts have been checked, particularly if your breed of choice is one with a higher incidence of cardiac disease, like purebred Maine Coons, Siamese, Sphynx, Ragdoll, and Burmese. A high-quality, nutritionally balanced diet formulated for cats is a vital ingredient in promoting heart health. Cat food must contain the amino acid taurine because cats cannot make it themselves, and a diet lacking this essential nutrient is a fast track to heart disease.

Kidney disease is common in older cats. The kidneys slowly lose their ability to filter waste, which shows up as drinking and urinating more, tiredness, weight loss, and a scruffy coat. It can also lead to high blood pressure and low red blood cell counts, which cause pale gums, weakness, and fast breathing.

Lowering the risk: Although kidney disease canât be entirely prevented, regular checkups with blood and urine tests can catch changes early. A balanced diet helps, and maximizing a catâs water intake by feeding wet food and providing a range of enticing water options can make a significant difference.

Feline Leukemia Virus (FeLV) is prevalent in cat populations around the world and is the most common cause of feline cancer. FeLV attacks the bone marrow, and the clinical signs of infection can be quite variable, but often include lethargy, weight loss, poor appetite, and weakness. Because FeLV cripples the immune system, affected cats are more susceptible to disease, and an otherwise simple infection can be deadly.

Lowering the risk: Vaccination is the best protection. Keeping cats indoors and away from possibly infected cats helps, as does quarantining newcomers and regularly cleaning dishes, toys, bedding, and litter boxes.

This is the most common hormone disease in cats, affecting about 10% of cats over age ten. It can lead to heart problems, kidney disease, blindness, and death. Cats lose weight quickly despite a huge appetite, and some suddenly go blind because of high blood pressure. Fortunately, the outlook is good with treatment, including medication, surgery, or radioactive iodine.

Lowering the risk: Hyperthyroidism canât be prevented, so spotting changes early and getting annual blood screening are the best weapons.

Up to one in a hundred cats develops diabetes in their lifetime, either because they donât make enough insulin or canât use it properly. Left untreated, it can cause serious complications. Signs include weight loss, drinking and urinating more, accidents outside the litter box, and bloody urine. Treatment is usually insulin injections, but if caught early, it can sometimes be managed through diet.

Lowering the risk: Feeding a high-quality, nutritionally balanced diet with low carbohydrate levels can help reduce the risks. The most common form of diabetes in cats is Type 2 (insulin resistance) and the best way to avoid this disease is by maintaining a lean, healthy body condition.

Rabies is almost always fatal, and cats are the domestic animal most likely to get it. It spreads through bites and scratches, often from wildlife such as bats, skunks, raccoons, and foxes. Some infected cats become withdrawn, others irritable or aggressive, and drooling and wide pupils are common. It is dangerous for people too.

Lowering the risk: Vaccines protect for one to three years. If contact with rabies is suspected, veterinary attention must be sought immediately. The disease is invariably fatal once clinical signs of rabies appear, so donât wait.

Feline Infectious Peritonitis (FIP) is caused by a mutated form of feline coronavirus. Early signs are mild, such as tiredness, but many cats develop a swollen belly from fluid, and it can progress fast. Until recently, FIP was almost always fatal, though newer treatments are changing this.

Lowering the risk: Good hygiene between cats and isolating newcomers until theyâre healthy reduce exposure. Keeping cats healthy and up to date on other vaccines may also help.

Spread by mosquitoes, heartworms are less common in cats than in dogs, but they can still cause serious disease, including sudden death. Wheezing and coughing are the most common signs, and the infection is hard to detect.

Lowering the risk: A monthly preventive is the best defense. If you live in an area where heartworm is present, it takes only one mosquito, so even indoor cats can be exposed.

Not every disease on this list can be prevented, but many can be avoided or managed simply by providing your cat with a high-quality diet and keeping up with regular vet checks. Blood and urine tests, appropriate vaccines, and monthly flea, tick, and heartworm protection all help, and keeping cats indoors lowers exposure to injury and illness. Catching problems early is often what makes treatment most effective, and a vet can help tailor a plan for each cat.

How to Plan Your Holiday Meals in a Year of Unprecedented Recalls

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Following a series of high-profile food recalls and outbreaks this year, many Americans may be concerned about keeping their families safe from foodborne illness during the holidays.

In fact, according to a recent national survey by Orlando Health, around 60% of U.S. adults say they’re more concerned about foodborne illness this year than in previous years.

Although several major foodborne illness outbreaks have ended and their associated recalls are no longer active, food safety remains an important concern. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) estimates that 48 million people in the United States experience foodborne illness each year.

However, there are simple steps you can take to reduce the risk of foodborne illness when preparing holiday meals and storing leftovers.

While there were several food recalls in the United States over the summer, two of the largest involved Salmonella linked to jalapeños and Cyclospora linked to lettuce.

These outbreaks prompted widespread recalls and raised concerns about the safety of certain foods, particularly fresh produce.

Although health officials have since declared the major outbreaks over, some consumers may still be hesitant to purchase foods associated with previous recalls.

Even with this declaration, you may still feel nervous about purchasing certain foods, but experts say you don’t need to worry.

“Here in the United States, we have the safest food supply in the world. The recent recalls show that our food safety system is working,” said William Schaffner, MD, Professor, Division of Infectious Diseases at Vanderbilt University.

Michelle Routhenstein, preventive cardiology dietitian at Entirely Nourished, told Healthline that past recalls do not mean entire food categories need to be avoided. The important thing is whether the specific product you have matches a current recall, based on details such as brand, lot number, package size, and expiration date.

“You should enjoy familiar foods, including properly cooked poultry, washed fruits and vegetables, and pasteurized dairy products,” she said. “The key is checking relevant recalls and practicing safe food handling.”

“Before shopping or cooking, review FDA and USDA (U.S. Department of Agriculture) recall information and compare any relevant notices with the labels on your ingredients,” said Routhenstein.

Check the pantry and freezer, too: something purchased months ago may still be affected.

“Stay alert to any news reports about food recalls and use good food preparation practices. Those simple actions should keep your holidays happy and safe,” said Schaffner.

Consumers can sign up for food recall alerts through the FDA or visit FoodSafety.gov for recall information from both the FDA and USDA.

Schaffner emphasized the importance of following safe food-handling practices when preparing everyday meals and holiday gatherings.

“Remember the longstanding advice: keep food hot or cold. Avoid long times at room temperature,” he said.

Routhenstein advised focusing on four basics: clean, separate, cook, and chill.

Schaffner noted the importance of refrigerating leftovers promptly rather than leaving them at room temperature for extended periods. He recommends clearing the table and refrigerating leftovers soon after the meal ends.

Routhenstein reiterated that you should refrigerate leftovers within 2 hours rather than leaving them out to cool.

Divide turkey into smaller portions and transfer sides and gravy into shallow containers to help them cool quickly, she advised.

Leftovers can typically be stored in the refrigerator for 3–4 days. If you don’t plan to eat them within that time, consider freezing them.

The important thing is to keep an eye out for any new recalls, practice food safety, and enjoy your holiday meals!

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/pharma/overtons-fda-nomination-has-been-pulled-white-house" hreflang="en">Heidi Overton’s FDA nomination pulled by White House</a>

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Heidi Overton’s FDA nomination pulled by White House" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The White House pulled the nomination for Heidi Overton, M.D., Ph.D., to lead the FDA just hours before the Senate was scheduled to vote on her confirmation.

The move hardly comes as a surprise after Overton’s confirmation hearing before the Senate Health, Education, Labor and Pensions (HELP) Committee drew contention.

The news was first reported by Axios on Tuesday evening. The White House confirmed the news to Fierce the following morning.

In September, the HELP committee questioned Overton in a hearing marked by tightrope-walking on issues like vaccines, abortions, flavored vapes and whether or not President Donald Trump is capable of being wrong.

Overton conceded multiple times during the hearing that vaccines approved by the FDA, as well as the “abortion pill” mifepristone, are safe and effective. But she stopped short of admitting that Trump has been wrong when dispensing medical information out of line with what scientific evidence shows.

Overton’s nomination faced mounting pushback and uncertainty from senators ahead of a planned HELP Committee vote on Nov. 18. Fierce reached out to Sen. Ed Markey, the veteran Democratic lawmaker from Massachusetts, ahead of the vote, and he told Fierce that he would “vote against her nomination, and I urge my colleagues to do the same.”

“The FDA needs a leader who will stand up for science and for our kids’ safety, not one who stood by while both were undermined,” he added.

Republican Sen. Bill Cassidy of Louisiana, who chairs the Senate health committee, and other Republicans had also raised questions about Overton's nomination.

As support for Overton looked thin, Republicans were discussing Sen. Roger Marshall, a Kansas Republican, as a potential replacement, Semafor reported today. Health Secretary Kennedy reportedly met with Marshall in recent days to discuss filling the role, according to Politico, but the White House told the publication that it is not considering him.

Robert Steinbrook, M.D., health research group director for Public Citizen, commented today urging the HELP committee to reject Overton’s nomination: “The FDA needs a strong commissioner who stands for science and the integrity of the agency,” Steinbrook said. “Overton is the wrong choice for FDA Commissioner and has done nothing to dispel the well-founded reasons to oppose her candidacy.”

Analysts from Citi shared that the White House’s decision to pull the nomination has a negative impact on biopharma, “extending uncertainty around permanent agency leadership.”

“We view the withdrawal as a modest sentiment negative, but not a material near-term change to the regulatory environment facing most drug and biologics sponsors,” the analysts said in the note.

The withdrawal of Overton's nomination leaves a gap at an FDA that has already faced significant upheaval. The White House only shifted its focus to Overton in August, after the agency had spent three months without a permanent leader.

Since former FDA commissioner Marty Makary, M.D., departed the agency in May, Kyle Diamantas, who hails from the agency’s food regulation division, has been at the helm in an interim capacity.

These Remote Atolls Are Among the Last Places on Earth Humans Haven’t Touched. Here’s What Scientists Found.

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Bikar and Bokak sit so far from people that they may show us what the ocean looked like before humans.

There are few havens on Earth as pristine as the Bikar and Bokak atolls. Formed from sunken volcanoes around which coral reefs grew, these atolls in the Ratak chain of the Marshall Islands teem with life and remain uninhabited by humans. Rainbows of fish swim among corals and sea anemones, while sharks glide silently through the water in pursuit of prey. Until recently, even marine biologists had rarely explored these habitats in depth.

That began to change when Alan Friedlander set out across an impossibly blue swath of ocean and led his team into paradise. Friedlander is a marine ecologist at the University of Hawai’i who served as chief scientist for National Geographic’s Pristine Seas program on the expedition, and he takes a special interest in remote and protected regions of the ocean. The people of the Marshall Islands have known Bikar and Bokak for generations, but the atolls had never been comprehensively surveyed when Friedlander and his team arrived. For the Marshallese, the ocean is home even more than the land is. Their survival depends on the health of the fragile reef ecosystems around the country’s 1,225 islands and islets, only some of which are inhabited.

The opportunity came in 2023, when Pristine Seas was invited to join the Marshall Islands Marine Resources Authority in surveying Bikar and Bokak, which are safeguarded by the Utirik Atoll community. Friedlander and his team received a traditional blessing and farewell on Utirik before sailing north to Bikar, which lies about 72 miles (116 km) from the nearest inhabited atoll. Bokak is even more isolated, about 239 miles (385 km) away. This remoteness is part of the reason the atolls have remained relatively untouched by human influence. Friedlander wanted to carry out an ecological assessment of everything from the land habitats to the reefs and the open sea. That meant studying the structure and biodiversity of these ecosystems, documenting how well they had been preserved and how the two atolls differed, and identifying the conservation measures they would need in the future.

“The remote northern atolls of Bikar and Bokak represent some of the least disturbed coral reef ecosystems remaining in the tropical Pacific,” Friedlander and his co-authors wrote in a study recently published in Frontiers in Marine Science. “Both atolls are uninhabited and geographically isolated from major population centers and industrial development. Previous expeditions documented portions of their terrestrial habitats, seabird colonies, sea turtles, and shallow coral reefs.”

The team’s first stop was Bikar, which curves across the water like a crescent moon. There, the researchers used a variety of sampling methods, including scuba surveys, photography, underwater video, deep-sea cameras, and a submarine, to record which species were present and in what numbers. Polynesian rats, which humans introduced, were sometimes seen scurrying through the island’s old-growth Pisonia forests. Even so, native species still thrive. Seabirds such as masked boobies, great frigatebirds, black noddies, and white terns filled the skies, while shorebirds like the migratory Pacific golden plover pecked at the sand in search of small invertebrates. Bikar’s beaches are also a nesting ground for green sea turtles; the team recorded 132 nest pits there. Its reefs are full of fish, including several species of snapper and grouper. Giant clams, which host photosynthetic algae, filter the water and are vital to the health of the reef.

Farther north and even more isolated, Bokak is an oasis that has seen very little human interference. Its drier, savanna-like landscape is covered in tufts of bunchgrass and tree heliotropes, which bear clusters of small white flowers. Many of the seabirds that soar over Bikar are also found on Bokak. Bokak’s waters are even richer than Bikar’s: its reefs are dominated by coral and hold more fish by weight. Friedlander dove in to investigate. He came across a school of humphead parrotfish, a threatened species once thought to have nearly vanished from the islands. He also saw an unusual reversal of the typical food pyramid. Reef sharks and black jacks outweigh their prey in total biomass. Because these top predators feed so often, they keep energy and nutrients moving quickly through the reef, which helps the ecosystem run efficiently.

After Bokak, Friedlander’s team had one more location to survey, and it offered a sharp contrast. From 1946 to 1958, Bikini Atoll was a center of U.S. nuclear testing, starting with Operation Crossroads. When the islanders were evacuated before the first tests, they took only a few changes of clothes, not knowing that they would never be able to resettle their home for good. The series of detonations devastated the atoll. From the ocean’s glassy surface, Bikini seems to be ringed by cities of coral. But divers who swam closer found that many of these formations, which had taken thousands of years to grow, were little more than skeletons. Still, Bikini Atoll is not without hope. After searching long enough, Friedlander found patches of live coral that had settled on the dead reef and were swarming with fish. It was a sign of how resilient life can be.

Even Bikar and Bokak can’t fully escape what is happening elsewhere. Their remoteness protects them from most direct human pressures, but climate change is slowly reaching them, too. When the water gets too hot, corals expel the symbiotic algae that feed them and give them their bright colors, a process known as coral bleaching. Leftover radiation from the nuclear-testing era is still a source of contamination in the region. Meanwhile, currents carry in trash from other countries, and plastics break down into microplastics. People are responding with conservation measures. In 2025, two years after the expedition, the government of the Marshall Islands designated the waters around Bikar and Bokak, about 18,500 square miles (48,000 square km), as the country’s first national marine sanctuary, as part of the community-led Reimaanlok conservation framework. Despite the threats that come from far away, Friedlander is optimistic.

“As relatively intact tropical marine ecosystems become increasingly rare worldwide,” he and his co-authors concluded, “remote atolls such as Bikar and Bokak serve not only as regional conservation priorities but also as globally important ecological reference systems for understanding the structure and future trajectories of tropical oceanic ecosystems in the Anthropocene.”

Elizabeth Rayne is a creature who writes. Her work has appeared in Popular Mechanics, Ars Technica, SYFY WIRE, Space.com, Live Science, Den of Geek, Forbidden Futures and Collective Tales. She lurks right outside New York City with her parrot, Lestat. When not writing, she can be found drawing, playing the piano or shapeshifting.
