Why Certain Pop Songs Trigger Inexplicable Emotion

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 17, 2026 | Reviewed by Abigail Fagan

Recently, I was scrolling through the Billboard Year-End Hot 100 singles of 1980. I was 14 years old that year, making it the absolute peak of my "musical reminiscence bump," the developmental window when our brains permanently wire the music of our youth to our deepest emotions.

While perusing the list, I realized there's one song that always stops me dead in my tracks and hits surprisingly hard whenever it catches me off guard in a random place, like a supermarket checkout line. That song is Elton John's "Little Jeannie."

Lyrically, this song doesn't say anything profoundly moving until near the end. And the refrain "I want you to be my acrobat, I want you to be my lover" always seems like a throwaway line. On paper, the song could easily be categorized as a seriously schmaltzy, mid-tempo early '80s pop song.

Nevertheless, every time I hear the opening percussion and cascading drum intro, I get verklempt. During the 20-second, lyric-free introduction that might sound like "Muzak-y" elevator music to some, my emotions swell and I'm filled with a deep sense of, for lack of a better word, "wistfulness" that doesn't make sense to me logically.

Curious if I was alone in having such a poignant response to a somewhat cheesy pop song, I looked at the YouTube comments to see if other listeners echoed my sentiment.

Notably, for many of the "Little Jeannie" versions on YouTube, all of which are sung in English by Elton John, the comment sections are dominated by foreign-language responses (lots of Portuguese!). According to Google Translate, these comments convey that even among non-English speakers or ESL listeners, "Little Jeannie" hits hard in bittersweet ways that seem to catch people off guard.

While sifting through various comments, I found two that elucidate the inexplicable power of "Little Jeannie" to stir emotions:

Why does a song that isn't inherently sad make someone cry? And how can a listener feel nostalgia for an era they didn't live through? As it turns out, the answers lie in a fascinating intersection of behavioral psychology, imagination, and neuroscience.

A new study published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences (PNAS) on November 16, 2026, investigated the sociocultural dimensions of music-evoked memories. The researchers played 30-second clips of billboard-topping hits to 223 college students, utilizing songs from their own teenage years (the 2010s) and their parents' teenage years (the 1980s and 1990s).

Using natural language processing to analyze the memories these songs evoked, the researchers found that certain songs reliably generated strikingly similar memory scenarios among strangers from comparable backgrounds, driven by culturally learned "scripts."

To find out where that emotion was coming from, the researchers stripped away the music and gave a separate group of participants just a song's lyrics to read. Without music accompanying the words to a song, the shared sociocultural dimensions of people's autobiographical memories and associations diminished.

Based on this observation, the researchers concluded that the lyrics alone don't drive our intense reactions to pop music. Instead, the emotional scripts are embedded in the musical sound and genre itself.

When a YouTube commenter notes that "Little Jeannie" isn't a sad song but makes their eyes well up with tears, it isn't necessarily the lyrics driving that reaction. It's the specific acoustic architecture created by an old Fender Rhodes electric piano, alto saxophone, and flugelhorn, all wrapped in layered vocal harmonies. This unique musical alchemy tugs directly at the heartstrings, completely bypassing the analytical mind where words reside.

How does neuroscience explain the enigma of a young listener feeling "ridiculously melancholic" when hearing a song released decades before they were born?

One possible explanation: While we experience a primary surge of memory attachment for the music of our own adolescence, we also develop a secondary attachment to the music of our parents and caregivers from earlier generations. As the authors explain in their recent PNAS paper:

"Interestingly, however, music-evoked autobiographical memories (MEAMs) are characterized by a potentially unique phenomenon known as 'cascading reminiscence bumps,' in which they exhibit a secondary reminiscence bump associated with music that was popular before listeners were born."

As children, we're often captive audiences in the backseat of a car or the family living room. Long before music was siloed into individual earbuds via streaming apps, "hard copy" formats like vinyl records, CDs, and mixtapes filled these shared spaces. Played aloud on common speakers, we passively absorbed our parents' adolescent soundtracks by osmosis.

Yet, even as modern listening habits shift toward isolated headphones, this intergenerational phenomenon persists. This new (2026) study suggests that these cultural "scripts" are so deeply wired into us that even if a younger participant had never heard a specific older song before, their brain can use the song's genre to automatically retrieve a feeling that fits the cultural vibe.

Because these acoustic architectures activate shared neural representations, the emotional script transcends both the generation and the delivery method.

Another recent study by Princeton's Elizabeth Margulis and colleagues, published in Nature Communications on August 18, 2026, used fMRI to explore the neural representations of imagined narratives that participants conjured up while listening to unfamiliar music.

During the experiment, researchers scanned people's brains while they listened to completely instrumental tracks. The scans revealed that across different test subjects, the music produced highly organized, shared patterns in the brain's default mode network (DMN). As the authors explain:

"This work demonstrates that music elicits detectable, modality-general representations, a kind of higher-order 'meaning', integrating both narrative imagery and its associated emotional profile, specific to particular excerpts."

Interestingly, Margulis's team found that the DMN doesn't need semantic language to engage in profound meaning-making. This helps to explain why it's almost impossible to pinpoint why certain music evokes shared imagined narratives; these emotions bubble up outside the realm of words (i.e., lexical semantics).

According to the latest 2026 research, music has the power to tap into a cultural frequency that bypasses our critical minds, reminding us that oftentimes, profound meaning is found in things we feel deeply but can't articulate.

Cara Turnbull, Benjamin M. Kubit, Kelly Jakubowski, Elizabeth H. Margulis. Music Illuminates Sociocultural Dimensions of Autobiographical Memory. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences (First published: November 16, 2026) doi:10.1073/pnas.2536885123

Elizabeth Margulis, Uri Hasson, Itamar Jalon, Jamal Williams, Grace Simmons, Karen Electra Christianson, Cara Turnbull. Music Evokes Shared Neural Representations of Imagined Narratives Across Sensory Modalities. Nature Communications (First published: August 18, 2026) doi:10.1038/s41467-026-76452-0

Kelly Jakubowski, Tuomas Eerola, Barbara Tillmann, Fabien Perrin, Lizette Heine. A Cross-Sectional Study of Reminiscence Bumps for Music-Related Memories in Adulthood. Music & Science (First published: October 23, 2020) doi:10.1177/2059204320965058

Opinion: The problem with ‘zero tolerance for bad behavior’ signs at hospitals

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We ranked the 25 most valuable publicly traded Chinese drugmakers

By Autumn Fiester, Aliza Narva, and Stacy C. Smith

Fiester is professor of medical ethics and health policy at the University of Pennsylvania’s Perelman School of Medicine and director of the Penn Program in Clinical Conflict Management. Narva is the director of ethics at the Hospital of the University of Pennsylvania. Smith is a registered nurse, certified health care ethics consultant, and qualitative researcher.

Enter any hospital today and you’ll see signs warning patients and visitors that abusive language, disruptive behavior, or aggression will not be tolerated. Some threaten removal from the facility. Others warn that security or police may be called for infractions. Almost all of them declare “zero tolerance” for any of these behaviors.

The motivation behind these signs is understandable. Workplace violence against health care workers is a serious problem in U.S. hospitals, with research demonstrating an increase in frequency over the past five years.

In response to this widespread problem, both state governments and hospitals have made a concerted effort to demonstrate that they are taking this problem seriously. They have enacted a range of anti-violence measures that include reporting systems, de-escalation training programs, weapon-detection portals, increased security presence, and posted behavior signs.  Our own health system has implemented similar measures.

While these efforts are laudable in intent, not all of them are evidence-based with demonstrable efficacy in curtailing workplace violence. That includes the now-ubiquitous posted hospital behavior signs. Multiple studies that have tested these signs do not deter patients or families from committing acts of violence or reduce the frequency of physical threats.

If posted hospital behavior signs were merely ineffective at reducing workplace violence, they would be pointless, but not harmful.

But we believe that behavior signage is more problematic than it appears. The content of the signs sends clear messages to patients and families about their tenuous status and value in the institution. These signs telegraph to patients and their loved ones that they are on notice, warned that their care can be withheld and their behavior policed — even punished. In the urgency to reduce violence against health care workers, hospitals have failed to consider the message these signs communicate and whether that message aligns or conflicts with the core values of medicine: trust, care, compassion.

In our review of posted behavior signs, we found that they target only patient and visitor conduct. They rattle off a list of patient and family “do nots,” the hospital’s mechanisms of behavior enforcement, and the dire consequences of behavioral infractions. Even when the signs give a nominal nod to the institution’s commitment to reciprocal expectations for respectful communication between patients and the clinical staff, the list of “do nots” applies only to patients and families. They list no parallel behavioral standards for nurses or physicians. That makes behavior signs ignore the relational aspect of conflict. Research on lapses in clinical professionalism points to a more nuanced explanation for some of the challenging behaviors exhibited by families and patients. The Joint Commission has even named such clinical behavior as one of the causes of violence against health care workers.

Behavior signs also display from a type of institutional amnesia, forgetting why patients and families have come to the hospital in the first place: They are in pain, anxious, frightened, grieving. They may have or soon receive a life-altering diagnosis or a heartbreaking prognosis. They are vulnerable. Is this a group of people who should receive stern warnings and threats of removal as they enter the building?

When signs espouse “zero tolerance” for “noncompliant,” “disrespectful,” or “demanding” behavior, they signal to patients and their families that raising concerns about their care can be interpreted as misconduct. In a setting where patients and families already feel profound vulnerability, voicing a complaint about their clinical care can feel too risky.

That silence risks patient safety. Data show that patient and family feedback can reveal genuine safety concerns and quality issues that would fly under the radar if they remained unvoiced. Without patients and families feeling welcome to express concerns, hospitals can miss the exact information that would improve patient care and prevent patient harm.

Hospitals do not have to choose between protecting hospital staff and preserving patient/family trust in the institution.

Imagine signs that convey not threats, but shared expectations and a willingness to help when things go wrong:

“Our staff is here to help you during what may be one of the most difficult days of your life. We ask everyone, including staff, patients, and families, to treat one another with respect. If something is not going well, please let us know so we can help resolve it.”

Such messages still make clear that violence will not be tolerated. But they also clearly convey that health care is a partnership, that the experience of illness can be challenging, and that civility helps everyone. They express behavioral standards, while also indicating that the hospital’s response to challenging interactions is to help, not punish. Some hospital systems have already replaced their “zero tolerance” signs with civility signs that project care and concern.

Protecting health care workers from violence must continue to be an institutional priority, but that effort requires evidence-based prevention programs, including efforts to understand and address the root causes of conflict.

Messages about patient, family, and clinical staff behavior can be delivered in a way that acknowledges the concerns of hospital employees while also preserving patients’ and families’ sense of belonging and their ability to ask hard questions, voice concerns, and participate fully in their care.

The words we post on hospital walls must communicate more than rules. They must communicate medicine’s values.

Autumn Fiester, Ph.D., is professor of medical ethics and health policy at the University of Pennsylvania’s Perelman School of Medicine and director of the Penn Program in Clinical Conflict Management. Aliza Narva, J.D., R.N., is the director of ethics at the Hospital of the University of Pennsylvania, where she leads the hospital’s ethics committee, ethics consultation service, ethics education initiatives, and nurse leader ethics fellowship. Stacy C. Smith, Ph.D., R.N., is a registered nurse, certified health care ethics consultant, and qualitative researcher whose work focuses on conflict and communication at the bedside.

Have an opinion on this essay? Submit a letter to the editor.

The smartest thinkers in life sciences on what's happening — and what's to come

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/hlth-2026-glp-1-costs-remain-challenge-new-cvs-study-aims-identify-right-price" hreflang="en">HLTH 2026: GLP-1 costs remain a challenge. A new CVS study aims to identify the right price</a>

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
HLTH 2026: GLP-1 costs remain a challenge. A new CVS study aims to identify the right price" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Cost and access to GLP-1s remains a challenge, but the benefits of these drugs aren't in doubt.

A new study from CVS Health aims to quantify at what price a GLP-1 drug has generated enough savings elsewhere through improved clinical outcomes. The healthcare giant used anonymized claims data from Aetna members in fully insured plans and found savings from reduced hospital and medical spending, as well as improved health outcomes.

But those benefits weren't enough to offset the increased pharmaceutical spending at the current price point, the study found.

The study tracked a mix of members who took GLP-1s from 2023 to 2025, so some patients had three years of follow-up care and management.

Amy Compton-Phillips, M.D., chief medical officer at CVS, told Fierce Healthcare that part of the challenge in this equation is that these drugs gained steam as a treatment for obesity at a high price. And while those costs have come down, the reductions haven't yet been enough to offset the expenditures.

Another challenge for employers in particular — where GLP-1 coverage has been most in flux — is turnover, she said. While GLP-1s do drive significant improvements, an employer may not reap the benefit of the reduced cost if an employee moves to a different company before those are realized, even though they paid for the medication initially.

The study sought to identify a price point where those savings would be felt and landed at $350 per month over a three-year period. Achieving that, Compton-Phillips said, would likely lure employers into embracing broader GLP-1 coverage.

"For them, it might be really worthwhile to say, 'Hey, at this price point, I can afford to cover this for my employees, knowing that in the near term the savings will pay for my investment,'" she said.

The study found obesity rates declining after a 2022 peak, as well as a reduction of between 34% and 42% in hospitalizations over three years. Patients also had 14% lower odds of a serious cardiac event.

For patients who remained on the drugs for three years, their total medical spending decreased by $9,400, but associated pharmacy spending increased in tandem by $13,300, the study found.

Clinical improvements also fell off when patients were not able to remain adherent to the medication over several years, highlighting the importance of a wraparound approach that supports broader care management rather than simply prescribing a patient a GLP-1.

Compton-Phillips said CVS' own weight management program brings nutrition, peer coaching and other supports into the fold, in addition to the prescription. This is especially critical, she said, as evidence suggests weight gain is common after a patient stops taking their GLP-1, making behavior change key.

"At the moment, there's not great evidence that if you come off the drugs, you will maintain the weight loss," she said.

The CVS researchers said that the ultimate goal of the study was not to demand that all GLP-1 prescriptions repay their own costs, but to ensure that drug costs align with patient benefits and the broader cost of access. By lowering the net price of these products, more people can access them, the study concludes.

"These drugs make people's lives better, and I want to give them out," Compton-Phillips said. Doing so becomes a challenge, though, "at the price they are today," she said.

Whale snot helps scientists sequence an entire genome

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 17, 2026 5:00 AM EST

Whale vomit can be turned into expensive perfume. Whale carcasses will sink to the bottom and become marine life habitats. Whale pee nourishes the entire ocean. But how about whale snot? The microbes and other biological material emitted from their blowholes when the giant cetaceans breathe has helped scientists sequence an entire whale genome: Take that whale vomit. The findings are detailed in a study published today in the journal Molecular Ecology Resources.

Whole genomes are crucial for animal conservation. With genomic data, researchers can measure population health, to see how much genetic diversity and inbreeding is present. It can also help identify individual animals for monitoring, help determine an animal’s sex, and estimate the population’s size.

To obtain genetic data from whales, marine biologists historically have had to conduct a skin biopsy. They’ll take a dart, stick it into the whale’s blubber, and take a small sample of the skin and blubber. However, during sampling, boats must get close to the whales, which can disturb them. In some locations and for some endangered species, getting close enough to take a biopsy isn’t permitted or possible.

That’s where whale snot—or blow—comes in. For this study, a team from Scotland’s University of St. Andrews, the University of Copenhagen in Denmark, the North Coast Cetacean Society, and Fisheries and Oceans Canada used several small drones off the coast of British Columbia. The drones were equipped with sterile petri dishes to collect the samples.

The team flew the drone through the exhaled breaths of one North pacific humpback whale (Megaptera novaeangliae kuzira) at a time, but still sampled several individual whales. The humpbacks generally took one to six breaths every time they surfaced—covering the drone and the dishes in whale snot.

The team collected 58 blow samples from 26 individual humpback whales. They also took repeated samples from the same whales, collected in different encounters and across two years to match the genomes. They then cross-referenced the genomes collected from the whale blow with genomes sequenced from traditional blubber biopsies taken from the same animals.

To keep the DNA from mixing, the team targeted the whale most upwind in the pod, since the blow downwind might mix with the blow from the whales up ahead. Resting whales were never sampled, and the team stopped the drone flights if a whale showed any reaction. More than one third of samples were collected by flying the drone directly from land—no boat required.

The DNA was sequenced to produce two types of genomes. Low-coverage nuclear genomes, which are inherited from both parents, and high-coverage mitochondrial genomes, which are inherited from the mother only.

The team found that the genetic diversity estimated from the blow samples was very similar to the estimates taken from traditional biopsies. Fortunately, the humpback population in the study shows little evidence of recent inbreeding. There is a small proportion of the genome that has long stretches of identical DNA, but the team believes that most likely reflects distant shared ancestry or past population bottlenecks.

According to the team, this research marks one of the first whole genome datasets for a group of North Pacific humpback whales. Previously, only one North Pacific humpback whole genome was publicly available.

“We will use these blow-derived genomes to not only assess population structure and health, but also assess kinship and family ties among humpback whales foraging in northern British Columbia,” Dr. Éadin O’Mahony, a study co-author and marine mammal ecologist who was at the University of St. Andrews at the time of the study, said in a statement. “The aim is to understand how their social bonds form: Are the whales that forage together family, or long-term friends?”

As expected, the North Pacific humpback whales were genetically distinct from their counterparts living in the North Atlantic. This confirms that data taken from whale blow works as well from data taken from conventional biopsies. The drone-captured genetic analyses also correctly determined every whale’s sex, and revealed three previously undescribed mitochondrial DNA variants, which could help show how some of these whales are related. The blow from whales swimming in groups also did not have any sign of contamination from neighboring whales.

About 16 percent of the DNA in each blow sample were also primarily from the microbes living in the whale’s airways. According to the team, this indicates that a single breath could tell us about the individual whale and its respiratory microbiome, opening up a potential new window into cetacean health.

That’s a whole lot of data from just a little bit of snot.

Resources for finding and receiving therapy

We include products we think are useful for our readers. If you buy through links on this page, we may earn a small commission. Here’s our process

This article is part of a mental health content series called “Mind Games,” in which Healthline editor Morgan Mandriota draws on personal experience and research to challenge misconceptions, reduce stigma, review trends, and share hot takes and lessons learned about what it means to be mentally well.

Coffee and I don’t exactly have the best relationship.

My morning cup tends to give me jitters, worsen my anxiety, and keep me up until 2 a.m. if I drink it past noon. As an already anxious human, the last thing I need is to exacerbate my symptoms, even if it’s coming from a delicious source.

Over a year ago, I noticed that my anxiety was higher than usual. I was also tossing and turning and waking up at all hours of the night for weeks on end.

One night in particular, I woke up every hour for 8 hours. Naturally, I wanted to blame it on the full moon or anything other than my beloved coffee, but alas, I realized that my caffeine consumption was the most likely culprit.

Could I have aimed to set a strict drinking schedule to see if that fixed things? Sure. But instead, I decided to quit cold turkey and undergo an experiment to see how I’d feel going coffee-free for the first time in years.

Over the next 8 months, I’d swap my coffee station for a matcha setup, dabble in exotic herbal blends from my local teahouse, and become a self-proclaimed tea connoisseur. Lo and behold, my anxiety eased up, and my sleep quality improved, too.

Fast forward to 4 months ago, when I hopped back on the coffee train. But this time, I’m relying on a handy amino acid to help avoid anxiety and other unwanted side effects: L-theanine.

Health and wellness touch everyone’s life differently. This is one person’s story. The views and opinions expressed are those of the speaker and do not necessarily reflect the views or positions of Healthline Media and should not serve as a substitute for medical advice from a healthcare professional.

L-theanine is mainly found in green and black tea and one type of wild mushroom. The mental health benefits of L-theanine consumption are an emerging area of research, but one 2024 review found that consuming around 200 to 400 milligrams (mg) may help relieve symptoms in people with anxiety disorders.

Still, anecdotal reports suggest there may be benefits of adding L-theanine to coffee. Over the last 5 years, the combo has been gaining traction on Reddit and TikTok for managing caffeine-related side effects.

Despite an overwhelming amount of rave reviews online, I couldn’t find much research to support these claims, so I spoke with a neurologist to see if they had any thoughts.

“This trend has real science behind it,” said Fawad Mian, MD, who is board certified in neurology, clinical neurophysiology, and sleep medicine and the founder of Ascend Regenerative.

“Controlled studies show caffeine plus L-theanine improves focus and attention more than caffeine alone, and some suggest it softens caffeine’s side effects, like tiredness, headaches, and blood pressure spikes,” Mian said. “It smooths caffeine’s effects but doesn’t erase them, so it’s not a license to drink more coffee,” he added.

With all of this in mind, especially after realizing just how calm I was while drinking black and green teas during my coffee break, I decided to give it a try.

After about 4 months of “testing,” I’ve found that adding L-theanine to my coffee works extremely well for me.

No more anxiety, scatterbrain, shakiness, or staying up until 3 a.m. And no lifestyle overhaul needed. The supplement, which I prefer in liquid form, has become a seamless addition to my daily routine.

Every morning, I make a fresh batch of French press coffee with (strong) local beans, then I squeeze some L-theanine into my cup.

I’ve noticed that I get the usual coffee benefits like improved focus and concentration, plus a nice, calm, stable energy throughout the day.

Even if I drink it until 2 p.m., I’m fighting to keep my eyes open at night before I finally fall asleep around 10. Perhaps this is because L-theanine may also support quality sleep.

Mindful L-Theanine Drops is the liquid supplement that I use every day.

I add a one-second squeeze (which contains 200 mg of L-theanine) into my morning coffee, then I’m good to go. I even started carrying a bottle in my bag just in case I get a latte from a coffee shop on a whim.

You can also add it to a glass of water, a cup of tea, or another drink of your choosing. It’s unflavored, so it doesn’t alter the taste of your drinks. Real secret stuff.

Out of curiosity, and because I’m a natural skeptic and overthinker, I asked Mian if my positive experience could be a placebo effect, or if I’m seeing real results.

“Expectation may play a small role, but the effect is biologically plausible,” he said. “L-theanine boosts calming brain activity that offsets caffeine’s stimulation, and 200 mg matches the doses used in studies.” However, he noted that it doesn’t speed caffeine clearance, so drinking coffee only in the morning still matters for sleep quality.

While L-theanine is generally safe to consume, it’s best to speak with a healthcare professional first, especially if you’re pregnant, nursing, receiving cancer treatment, or taking blood pressure medication or sedatives.

“Choose a third-party tested brand, and if caffeine makes you anxious, cutting back is still the simplest fix,” Mian added.

So, if you’re anything like me and drinking coffee also leads to anxiety and jitters for you, I highly recommend trying this supplement once you get the green light from your doctor. It’s been working for me. It may work well for you, too.

💌 Over to you: I’d love to hear your thoughts on this. Does coffee also give you unwanted side effects? Have you tried L-theanine to remedy them? Let me know, or ask any questions about my experience by emailing me here.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/most-nonprofit-hospitals-tax-breaks-outpaced-their-community-spending-2023-lown-institue" hreflang="en">Most nonprofit hospitals' tax breaks outpaced their community spending in 2023: Lown Institute</a>

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Most nonprofit hospitals' tax breaks outpaced their community spending in 2023: Lown Institute" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Just over half of the country’s nonprofit hospitals have charity care and community benefit investments that fall short of their tax savings, according to the latest of the Lown Institute’s analyses on more than 3,000 hospitals’ tax filings.

In 2023, the most recent year in which Internal Revenue Service Form 990s were available for review, the think tank identified $38.8 billion of industry-wide tax exemptions, or $12.8 million per hospital and 3.8% of total expenses on average. About $37.1 billion was collectively spent by the same hospitals on various community benefits, as reported in the IRS forms matched to other filings such as Medicare cost reports.

While the total exemption handily outpaces the benefits investments by Lown’s measure, the group underscored that these "fair share” deficits or surpluses also vary widely between facilities, and that some of the industry’s biggest names are on the wrong side of the divide.

Here, Mayo Clinic Hospital led the pack with a $366 million deficit, according to Lown’s analysis, followed by Tisch Hospital (-$294 million deficit), Stanford Hospital (-$255 million deficit), New York-Presbyterian Hospital (-$248 million deficit) and Massachusetts General Hospital (-$219 million deficit).

"Some of America's wealthiest hospitals fall hundreds of millions short of giving back what they receive from their communities, leaving those with fewer resources to pick up the slack," Vikas Saini, M.D., president of the Lown Institute, said in a release on the new analysis, released Tuesday.

Among those leading the latter group were North Shore University Hospital ($167 fair share surplus), Rochester General Hospital ($146 million surplus) and Englewood Hospital ($142 million surplus). Lown’s analysis includes a searchable hospital-by-hospital breakdown of the tax exemptions and community investment totals.

When splitting the 52% of deficit hospitals apart from the surplus hospitals, the former group logged about $15.5 billion above their reported community benefits—a deficit Lown said could wipe the medical debt of 12 million Americans. Fair share surplus hospitals outpaced their exemptions by $13.8 billion.

Total tax exemptions were also “very evenly distributed” among the hospitals, Lown said. The 30 hospitals, or about 1% of all hospitals, with the largest tax exemptions collectively avoided $6.8 billion, or about 18% of total exemptions. The 607 hospitals, or about 20%, accounted for three-quarters of the country’s hospital tax exemptions.

Lown’s measure of nonprofit hospitals’ tax breaks includes exemptions from federal and state corporate income taxes, state and local sales taxes, property taxes, tax-exempt bonds, tax-exempt charitable donations and federal unemployment tax exemptions. Not included are other benefits provided to many nonprofits, like Medicaid disproportionate share payments, 340B discounts or profits from indirect tax arbitrage.

Its tally of community benefit includes spending on patient financial assistance, subsidized care, payments to other nonprofits to improve health or various community health or social determinants of health investments.

Of note, it excludes Medicaid shortfall—the difference between hospitals’ costs and revenues when treating Medicaid patients—and spending on professional education and research. The hospital industry typically points to these billions of dollars of exclusions when discussing their tax breaks, with Medicaid shortfall alone accounting for nearly half of their preferred community benefit spending totals. Lown noted in its methodology that these accounting losses are often offset by other discounts and supplemental benefits and don’t always have a direct impact on community health, among other objections.

Lown said the disparities between hospitals and the outsized benefits being delivered by some are a call to amend, not eliminate, nonprofit status exemptions. The think tank accompanied its analysis with calls to increase reporting transparency and set spending targets for hospitals tied to variables such as size, exemption value, financial health and prior spending.

“Tax exemptions aren't a gift—they're an expectation, and regulators need to make sure all hospitals hold up their end of the bargain," Saini said.

Lown acknowledged the impending financial pressure hospitals will face with upcoming H.R. 1 policy changes, and said such changes should be accompanied by “short-term” financial protections.

Still, the group isn’t alone in drawing scrutiny to the tax breaks. Lawmakers and policy advisors have pointed to, and questioned the justification for, nonprofits’ exemptions when questioning health system leaders on collections policies or high prices.

Academics and researchers have also called for greater transparency or other overhauls of how community benefits are reported or calculated. Of note, a research article published earlier this month in Health Affairs reviewing hospital filings between 2013 and 2022 outlined a disconnect between different hospitals’ listed charity care eligibility thresholds and their relative spending, which more closely aligned with community need than their stated policy.

Can Non-Alcoholic Drinks Help You Stop or Reduce Drinking?

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 16, 2026 | Reviewed by Michelle Quirk

The New Year is just around the corner, and it is a common time for people to revisit self-improvement-related goals. If you want to reduce or stop drinking alcohol, you may be wondering if switching to non-alcoholic drinks could be helpful. As it turns out, for some people, non-alcoholic drinks could be a helpful substitute for alcoholic beverages, but there are also reasons to be cautious about this overall approach.

Before reading this post, if you are not already familiar with non-alcoholic drinks, take a look at my recent post, “Is there alcohol in non-alcoholic drinks?” The short answer is that the amount of alcohol depends on where you live. Generally, non-alcoholic drinks contain much lower alcohol by volume (ABV) than traditional alcoholic drinks, but they may still contain some alcohol. If you are trying to stop drinking alcohol completely, it would be important to check the rules where you live to make sure your chosen non-alcoholic drink has 0% ABV.

There are many reasons why someone may want to stop or reduce drinking alcoholic drinks, which I will not go over here. More relevant to this topic are the reasons why someone would want to consume non-alcoholic drinks, which are made to look and taste like alcoholic beverages, rather than traditional beverages that do not resemble alcoholic beverages (e.g., soft drinks, juices, water).

One reason for the popularity of non-alcoholic drinks is that someone may enjoy the taste of alcoholic drinks, like beer and wine, and wish to continue to drink similarly flavoured beverages (without the risks associated with consuming alcohol). Another reason people may choose non-alcoholic drinks is that they find it helps them continue to participate in social activities and routines that typically involve alcohol. For instance, when interviewing adults who drink alcohol heavily and have used non-alcoholic drinks to reduce drinking, some interviewees described that ordering a non-alcoholic beverage at a bar, club, or other social gathering allowed them to participate in the outing and blend in with their peers who were drinking alcohol.1 Further, unlike drinking alternatives like soft drinks, they describe that the experience of drinking a non-alcoholic drink remains symbolic as an "adult treat" given its look and presentation.

People may take different approaches to substitute with non-alcoholic drinks depending on their specific needs. Some people may completely substitute their alcoholic drinks for non-alcoholic drinks. Alternatively, people may replace alcoholic drinks in specific contexts, such as primarily substituting their home-based alcoholic drinks versus outing-based alcoholic drinks with non-alcoholic drinks.1 When replacing alcoholic drinks one typically consumes, some may replace them completely with non-alcoholic drinks while others may alternate between drink types (sometimes referred to as “zebra striping”).

While people describe using non-alcoholic drinks to reduce their alcohol use, an important question remains about whether these drinks can actually help you stop or reduce drinking. As it turns out, the qualities that make non-alcoholic drinks an attractive substitute for alcoholic drinks may also get in the way of successfully reducing one’s drinking. Aspects of drinking (beyond alcohol itself) can serve as powerful reminders—referred to as cues—of alcohol. Specifically, qualities of the drink like its taste, container, and where it is consumed can, in fact, lead to a strong urge to drink alcohol itself. By definition, non-alcoholic beverages will not lead to intoxication. Once consumed, these drinks may strengthen the desire to become intoxicated (rather than quench that desire), and lead someone to return to drinking alcohol.

Initial studies have suggested that non-alcoholic drinks may help people reduce their drinking. When adults who drink were provided non-alcoholic drinks free of charge over a 12-week period, they reported greater reductions in their alcoholic drink consumption compared to adults who were not provided these drinks.2 This reduction in alcoholic drinks continued a month after these non-alcoholic drinks were provided. A follow-up study suggested, however, that the reduced alcohol drinking was not observed amongst people with higher symptoms of alcohol use disorder.3 Additional research is needed to understand if non-alcoholic drinks may be a helpful way to reduce or stop drinking among people with an alcohol use disorder.

Ultimately, there would be a net health and mental health benefit if someone reduces the overall number of alcoholic drinks by substituting non-alcoholic drinks. For instance, if someone switches from drinking four alcoholic drinks per week to two alcoholic drinks plus two non-alcoholic drinks, they would be reducing their exposure to harmful aspects of alcohol. However, depending on an individual’s circumstances, the chosen strategy for which drinks to substitute can affect how much they benefit overall. For instance, if a person finds they have more difficulty moderating their drinking when out with friends (versus at home), having a strategy to rely on non-alcoholic drinks when out with friends may be more beneficial than substituting their alcohol drinks consumed at home.

While research is still in its early stages, non-alcoholic drinks show promise in helping people reduce their drinking. For some people, substituting non-alcoholic drinks may provide the benefits of drinking alcoholic drinks without the negatives; however, it is important to make sure the substitute approach does not become an add-on approach. In cases where non-alcoholic drinks are too similar to alcoholic drinks that it leads you to drink alcohol anyway, then the substitution approach is not successful. Therefore, if you are thinking about giving this strategy a try, it is important to keep track of your drinking carefully to determine if it is actually helping you to reduce the number of alcoholic drinks you consume.

1. Perman-Howe, P., Fenton, L., O’Donnell, R., Howell, R., Kersbergen, I., Clarke, Z., & Holmes, J. (2026). Exploring how and why people who drink alcohol at high-risk levels use alcohol-free and low-alcohol drinks when cutting down: a qualitative focus group study. BMC Public Health. https://doi.org/10.1186/s12889-026-29230-y

2. Yoshimoto, H., Kawaida, K., Dobashi, S., Saito, G., & Owaki, Y. (2023). Effect of provision of non-alcoholic beverages on alcohol consumption: a randomized controlled study. BMC Medicine, 21(1), 379. https://doi.org/10.1186/s12916-023-03085-1

3. Dobashi, S., Kawaida, K., Saito, G., Owaki, Y., & Yoshimoto, H. (2024). The effectiveness of reduction in alcohol consumption achieved by the provision of non-alcoholic beverages associates with Alcohol Use Disorders Identification Test scores: a secondary analysis of a randomized controlled trial. BMC Medicine, 22(1), 424. https://doi.org/10.1186/s12916-024-03641-3

STAT+: NIAID acting head John Powers III appointed as the permanent director

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Anil Oza is a general assignment reporter at STAT focused on the NIH and health equity. You can reach him on Signal at aniloza.16.

The acting director of the National Institutes of Allergy and Infectious Diseases, John Powers III, will become the permanent director, the agency announced Monday.

NIAID has been without a permanent director since March 2025, when the Trump administration ousted Jeanne Marrazzo. Powers has been serving in an acting capacity since June. A physician-scientist, Powers has worked with NIAID since 1999 in several roles focused on clinical research.

The second Trump administration has brought a renewed spotlight to the infectious disease institute, in large part because of the president’s ire for former director Anthony Fauci, and because of unsubstantiated accusations that research funded by the institute in China played a role in creating the SARS-CoV-2 virus. It has also seen tumult in its leadership. After Marrazzo was ousted, Jeffery Taubenberger was appointed acting director. He unexpectedly stepped down, and several other leaders at NIAID were reassigned to other institutes or asked to resign.

Everything You Need to Know About Korean Scalp Scalers

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

If you’ve ever been to a Korean head spa, you know it’s less of a hair wash and more of an entire reset. Before anyone touches a shampoo bottle, there’s usually a close-up look at your scalp on a magnifying camera, followed by a treatment that loosens all the oil, dead skin, and product reside that a regular wash leaves behind. It’s the step that makes your hair feel impossibly light afterward, and it’s called scaling.

You don’t need a spa appointment to get the same effect, though. Scalp scalers bring that deep clean home, whether that’s an acid-based treatment you leave on before shampooing or a salt scrub that lathers up in the shower. They’re especially useful if you lean on dry shampoo, work out often, or have fine hair that goes flat a day after washing.

Below, experts explain what a scalp scaler actually does, who should use one and how to keep from overdoing it, plus the formulas worth trying.

The name actually comes from the dentist’s office. “In Korean, ‘scaling’ is the same word we use for dental cleaning,” says Dr. Ellie Byun, a doctor of Korean medicine and licensed acupuncturist. “Plaque builds up on your teeth even when you brush, and in the same way, oil and dead skin build up on your scalp even when you shampoo.” For many Korean women, it’s a routine they’ve had for years. “I’ve been using scalp scalers since high school,” says Kristin Lim is the founder of Base-K.

What sets a scaler apart from a regular wash is time. “A clarifying shampoo only cleanses during the short time it’s on your scalp,” says Dr. Byun. “A scaler stays on for several minutes and chemically dissolves dead skin and oil with acids and enzymes, the same principle as an exfoliating toner for your face.” That said, plenty of Korean scalers, including several below, use salt granules instead of acids, or a mix of both. “A scalp scaler focuses on loosening dead skin and buildup, using acids, physical exfoliants or a combination of both,” says Lim. If you go the gritty route, keep it gentle. “A scrub physically buffs the surface with grains, and if you use it too often, it can make the scalp barrier sensitive,” says Dr. Byun.

“It’s especially good for oily scalps, flaking caused by buildup, fine hair that goes flat quickly, people who use a lot of dry shampoo and styling products, and anyone who sweats a lot,” says Byun. Flaking is where it gets tricky, though. “It can also help loosen surface flakes, but persistent flaking and itching deserve a closer look,” says Lim. “Dandruff and other scalp conditions may need targeted treatment rather than more exfoliation.”

The first thing most people notice is how light their hair feels afterward. “Removing that residue can leave the scalp feeling cleaner and the hair feeling lighter,” says Lim. With regular use, it can help with oil and flaking, too. “In a four-week study of a salicylic acid formula, researchers examined the scalp with a magnification camera and found that flaking, itching, redness, and oiliness all decreased,” says Dr. Byun. A calmer scalp may also mean better hair over time. “Studies show that when scalp health declines, hair quality declines too, and when the scalp recovers, hair quality recovers with it,” she says.

The biggest risk is doing too much. “Over-exfoliating weakens the scalp barrier and can cause irritation, dryness, and even a rebound increase in oil,” says Dr. Byun. It’s not the right fit for every scalp, either. “Individuals with sensitive or irritated scalps should approach exfoliating treatments cautiously,” says Joe Cho, team lead of media relations at Medicube. Even a mild label doesn’t change that. “Even a gentle formula may not suit a scalp that’s actively inflamed,” says Lim. If something feels off, don’t push through it. “If you notice sudden heavy shedding, pain, or lesions, see a professional,” says Dr. Byun.

“Some scalers go onto a dry or damp scalp before shampooing, while others are used on wet hair as part of the wash,” says Lim. Either way, the product needs to reach your scalp, not just your hair. “Part your hair in sections about an inch apart and apply it directly to the scalp along each part,” says Byun. “Then massage with your fingertips, never your nails, for two to three minutes, pressing slowly on the crown and the base of the skull.” Then rinse it out on time. “Leaving it on longer or scrubbing harder won’t improve the result,” says Lim.

Less is more with frequency, too. “I recommend once a week for oily scalps, and once every two weeks for normal or dry scalps,” says Byun. Your scalp will tell you when you’ve gone too far. “Signs you’re overdoing it include tightness, stinging, redness, or tenderness, more flaking instead of less, new itching, or a scalp that feels oilier than before,” she says. Lim also recommends sticking to one exfoliant at a time and avoiding combining several scalp exfoliants in the same wash.

Afterward, think of your scalp the way you’d think about your face after an exfoliating toner. “Korean care follows a simple idea: clear first, then nourish,” Dr. Byun says. “Use a mild, low-pH shampoo, then a lightweight scalp serum with niacinamide, panthenol, or centella, and apply conditioner only from the mid-lengths to the ends.”

Not directly. “While scalp health plays an important role in overall hair care, there’s currently limited clinical evidence that scalp exfoliation alone can directly stimulate hair growth or prevent shedding,” says Cho. Dr. Byun sees it as groundwork. “A scaler doesn’t grow hair on its own, but it does have a real, research-backed role,” she says. “So I see scaling as the first step in creating an environment where healthy hair can grow.” Lim is just as clear: “Promising that a scaler alone will regrow hair or stop shedding would be over-promising.”

Usually, scalp scales are safe for colored hair as long as you keep it where it belongs. “It’s generally fine on color-treated hair as long as you apply it to the scalp, not the hair itself,” says Byun.

When we test and review a product, we take a holistic approach to deliver well-rounded product recommendations. First, we lean on Vogue's vast network of experts—from board-certified dermatologists to celebrity estheticians—to gain professional acumen on the industry's standout products, ones these specialists would actually use on their clients. We pair their expertise with our editorial best practices to curate the thoughtful edits you read on our site.

As it relates to Korean scalp scalers, we selected the best based on the following characteristics: ingredients, exfoliant type, texture, packaging, finish, and the hair-care concerns they address. Ask Dr. Paola - HELP! What Does It Mean When a Dog’s Hackles Go Up in Different Places? (November 16, 2026)

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

I have a question about my dog, Jethro. Sometimes when he alerts, the hackles between his shoulders rise. Other times they are prominent along his lower back. What does each appearance indicate about how he is responding?â

That is a great observation! When a dogâs hackles rise, it is an involuntary response controlled by the autonomic nervous system called piloerection, and it tells us that his arousal level has increased. However, the location of the raised hair does not reliably tell us which emotion a dog is experiencing.

When the hackles rise between his shoulders, it can accompany alertness, excitement, uncertainty, or concern. When they appear along his lower back, the same range of emotional responses is possible. Neither pattern automatically indicates aggression, fear, or a particular level of threat. Think of hackles as a little emotional alarm bell; they tell us Jethro is experiencing heightened arousal, but not exactly why.

Different dogs will respond to different stimuli in different ways, so thereâs no âone size fits allâ explanation for why different areas are triggered. I would pay close attention to what is happening around him when this occurs. Is he alerting to a stranger approaching the house, another dog walking past, an unfamiliar noise, or something unexpected in the environment? You might want to start keeping a record of what is going on before and after Jethroâs hackles are raised, and whether itâs his shoulders or lower back that is involved.

Combine that context with his facial expression, ear position, tail carriage, posture, muscle tension, and ability to disengage. Looking at the whole picture helps us understand whether Jethro is curious, excited, uncertain, or uncomfortable, and, most importantly, how we can best support him. These details, together with what triggered the alert, give us a much clearer picture of how Jethro is feeling.

Ask Dr. Paola – HELP! My Senior Cat Won’t Leave the Bedroom (November 16, 2026)

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Catster is reader-supported. When you buy via links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission at no cost to you. Learn more.

Welcome to our "Ask Dr. Paola" series, where every other Monday we bring expert advice straight from Dr. Paola Cuevas (MVZ) to help our readers better understand their cat's health and well-being. Whether you're a new pet parent or a seasoned cat lover, Dr. Paola is here to provide answers to your most pressing questions. From nutrition tips and preventive care to troubleshooting common behavioral issues, Dr. Paola is ready to offer insights that will keep your kitty happy, healthy, and feline fine. Stay tuned for expert guidance on a range of topics that matter most to you and your cat, so you can make informed decisions and provide the best possible care for your furry companion. â

My cat, Mitzi, is 16 years and 8 months old. She has early-stage kidney disease and hyperthyroidism, which she is on medication for. About 2 months ago, she became deaf, and since then has refused to leave the master bedroom. I have moved her water bowl and litter box there and feed her there.

Is there anything I can do to get her to come out?â

At her age, sweet Mitzi has certainly earned the right to enjoy her golden years at her own pace, but I can imagine it is difficult to see her withdraw from the rest of the house, especially when you remember how she used to move around. However, I would not necessarily interpret her reluctance to leave the bedroom as something we need to correct. Losing her hearing can be quite disorienting. Imagine walking into a room where you can no longer hear footsteps, familiar voices, or the little sounds that help you understand what is happening around you. For Mitzi, the bedroom may now feel like her safest little world, where everything is familiar and predictable.

I actually think you have done a wonderful job adapting her environment by bringing her food, water, and litter box closer. Rather than encouraging her to leave right away, I would focus on helping her feel confident enough to explore if she wishes. You could start by sitting with her near the bedroom doorway, offering a favorite treat or a little affection. Gradually place treats a short distance beyond the door, allowing her to decide whether she wants to investigate.

Take care to only approach her within her visual field, avoid startling her while she sleeps, and keep pathways clear and nonslip. Think of it as extending her new world one comfortable step at a time, rather than asking her to return to her old routine.

There is one thing I would not overlook, though. Mitzi has both chronic kidney disease and hyperthyroidism, and at her age, reduced mobility or withdrawal can also be associated with arthritis, high blood pressure, visual changes, or inadequate control of her thyroid disease. I would recommend a senior cat veterinary checkup that includes blood pressure measurement, assessment for arthritis, and follow-up kidney and thyroid testing. Sometimes what looks like a behavioral change is actually a senior cat quietly telling us that they are in pain and something does not feel quite right.

Most importantly, please do not feel that you are encouraging a bad habit by keeping her essentials in the bedroom. You are adapting her home to meet her changing needs, and that is a beautiful expression of your love and care for her. If the bedroom is where Mitzi feels safe, eats well, drinks, uses her litter box, and enjoys your company, that can be a perfectly good quality of life.

At this stage, happiness is not measured by how much of the house she explores, but by how comfortable and secure she feels in the little world she chooses.

As a certified cat-lover, Radim was excited to join the Catster team on the mission to help all the cat parents in the world by providing useful and helpful information. He used to live with two wonderful cats before embarking on his travels around the world. Radim currently lives in Slovakia, surrounded by nature and his neighbors' cat community.

HIV Treatment Is Prevention

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 16, 2026 | Reviewed by Michelle Quirk

HIV treatment, antiretroviral therapy (ART), has seen dramatic advancement in the past four decades. HIV is now a chronic condition. When people take their ART as prescribed and maintain an undetectable viral load and a high T-cell count, life expectancy for those living with HIV approximates that of those who are uninfected (Trickey et al., 2023).

In addition to the direct health benefits of maintaining an undetectable viral load, there is an additional prevention benefit. When someone living with HIV has an undetectable viral load, they are unable to transmit HIV through sex (Eisinger et al., 2019). Public health campaigns promoting ART adherence have called attention to this phenomenon, terming it Undetectable = Untransmissible or U=U.

We might assume that prevention messaging that draws on U=U would be most effective among people living with HIV who are partnered with someone who is uninfected. Having an undetectable viral load would decrease or eliminate the potential for sexual transmission of HIV to their partner. Alternatively, people living with HIV may be generally motivated to take their ART and maintain an undetectable viral load when they are having sex with partners who may be uninfected regardless of relationship status.

In two recent studies (Hillesheim & Starks, 2026; Starks et al., 2024), our team examined relationship status group differences in ART adherence and viral detectability in large samples of sexual minority male adults.

Ordinarily, we expect people in relationships to have better health outcomes compared to single people (Kiecolt-Glaser & Wilson, 2017). In both of these papers, findings suggested this was true for sexual minority men living with HIV in non-monogamous relationships and those whose partners were uninfected. These groups were more likely to be adherent to ART and report an undetectable viral load compared to single sexual minority men. Presumably, these are groups where men may be most likely to consider preventing transmission of HIV to sexual partners.

There was also a warning sign in both papers. Sexual minority men in relationships where both partners were living with HIV were significantly less likely to be adherent to their ART and achieve an undetectable viral load. For these men, prevention motivation may be of limited relevance. It may be important to emphasize the personal health benefits of HIV treatment to maximize HIV outcomes in these subgroups.

Effective HIV treatment not only prolongs the lives of people living with HIV, but it also eliminates the potential for sexual transmission. This prevention motivation may explain why rates of ART adherence and achieving an undetectable viral load are higher among sexual minority men in non-monogamous relationships and those with uninfected partners. We may need different messages, emphasizing the personal health benefits of HIV care, to reach subgroups who have low levels of sexual HIV prevention motivation.

Eisinger, R. W., Dieffenback, C. W., & Fauci, A. S. (2019). HIV viral load and transmissibility of HIV infection: Undetectable equals untransmittable. Journal of the American Medical Association, 321(5), 451–452. https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2018.21167

Hillesheim, J. R., & Starks, T. J. (2026). Relationship status and quality associations with HIV care cascade outcomes among sexual minority men living with HIV in the US: Indications of a dyadic coping paradox. AIDS and Behavior, 30(7), 2256–2267. https://doi.org/10.1007/s10461-026-05050-4

Kiecolt-Glaser, J. K., & Wilson, S. J. (2017). Lovesick: How couples' relationships influence health. Annual Review of Clinical Psychology, 8(13), 421–443. https://doi.org/10.1146/annurev-clinpsy-032816-045111

Starks, T. J., Sauermilch, D., Doyle, K. M., Kalichman, S., & Cain, D. (2024). Main partner relationships and the HIV care cascade: Examining the predictive utility of sexual agreements, partner concordance, and drug use among sexual minority men living with HIV in the US. Annals of Behavioral Medicine, 58(6), 422–431. https://doi.org/10.1093/abm/kaae019

Trickey, A., Sabin, C. A., Burkholder, G., Crane, H., d'Arminio Monforte, A., Egger, M., Gill., M. J., Grabar, S., Guest, J. L., Jarin, I., Lampe, F. C., Obel, N., Reyes, J. M., Stephan, C., Sterling, T. R., Teira, R., Touloumi, G., Wasmuth, J. C., Wit, F.,…Sterne, J. A. C. (2023). Life expectancy after 2015 of adults with HIV on long-term antiretroviral therapy in Europe and North America: A collaborative analysis of cohort studies. Lancet HIV, 10(5), e295–e307. https://doi.org/10.1016/S2352-3018(23)00028-0

Physical Therapists Share the Most Effective QL Stretches and Exercises for Runners With Lower Back and Hip Pain

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Here’s how to rehab this deep lower back muscle at home—and when it’s time to book a session with your PT.

Most runners are familiar with the big muscles involved in running, such as the quads, hamstrings, and glutes. But there are lesser-known—yet still important—players that can influence running performance. One example: the quadratus lumborum, or QL for short.

A deep trunk muscle, the QL attaches to the lower spine and the back side of the pelvis, Nicole Haas, PT, DPT, a physical therapist and founder of Boulder Physiolab in Colorado, tells Runner’s World. It serves a few different functions in daily life, such as helping you arch your back, bend to the side, and rotate your upper body, Haas explains.

When you run, the QL plays more of a stabilizing role, working to control your pelvis and trunk while you stride, says Spencer Agnew, PT, DPT, a physical therapist at Sword Health and run coach with Wisconsin-based Ascent Endurance Group. The QL also assists with breathing, specifically when you inhale, Agnew adds.

If this muscle is tight or irritated, you’ll likely feel pain in your back or sacroiliac joints, which link the pelvis and lower spine, Agnew says. To help you avoid these aches, we asked the PTs to explain what can cause an angry QL and how to relieve it.

There are several reasons your QL might be yelling at you. A common one? Your hip flexors are tight. In particular, when the psoas (a big, rope-like hip flexor muscle that runs from your lower back to the front of your hip) is shortened, it can pull your pelvis into an anterior tilt, meaning your pelvis is tipped forward, arching your lower back. In many cases, this yanks on your QL as well, because the psoas and QL both insert into the same group of vertebrae in your lower back, Agnew explains.

Another culprit is weak glutes. When your glutes aren’t strong enough to help you maintain good running mechanics, your knee can collapse inward as you land and your opposite-side hip can dip down, Haas explains. This, in turn, can cause your trunk to side bend and rotate, thus placing more strain on your QL over time. Running downhill can exacerbate the problem, Haas adds, because that can cause your lower back to arch and place further stress on your QL.

Yet another cause of an irritated QL is upper-back stiffness. Restrictions in the upper part of the spine can shift more workload onto the QL, Haas explains. So can issues in your shoulders, hips, and mid-back. If these areas aren’t moving like they should due to pain or injury, your QL may receive added stress, Haas says.

Keep in mind that in some cases, your QL may hurt because it’s taking on a bunch of extra stress it’s not meant to, but the muscle itself probably isn’t tight. “It’s more like your QL is just irritated because everything has been collapsing around it and loading it,” Haas says. “It’s overworked and underappreciated.”

A tight-feeling QL is worth addressing because it can lead to a few not-so-great issues if left unchecked. A big one? Compromised running mechanics.

A tight QL, as mentioned, can shorten your hip flexors and pull your pelvis into an anterior tilt, which will reduce your knee lift as you stride, Agnew says. As a result, “you’re not going to be able to generate as much force moving forward, and you’ll have more of an arched-back position when you run,” he explains. In many cases, this can deliver a double whammy of reduced performance and increased back pain, he says.

On top of that, a tight QL can also hinder your breathing. As mentioned above, one of the QL’s jobs is to help you inhale, so when this muscle is tight, you may not be able to breathe as deeply or easily, Agnew says.

Plagued by what you think may be an irritated QL? Try Agnew’s three-step at-home series to help reduce your discomfort.

Perform this sequence as often as every day if you’re in significant discomfort that’s limiting your ability to run. In the case of minor pain, incorporate this series two to three times per week. On the days you run, perform the myofascial release, pelvic tilts, and hip hikes before you lace up, then tackle the other components afterward.

This can help turn down the pain response in a barking QL by desensitizing soft tissue, Agnew explains.

The pelvic tilts help you learn how to coordinate and control pelvic motion so you can maintain proper mechanics while running, and the hip hikes help activate your QL. The kettlebell side bend increases blood flow to the muscle, which can aid in healing. Finally, the suitcase carry increases strength, stability, and engagement of the muscle, which can ensure it functions properly during a run.

Lengthen your QL—as well as tight hip flexors—with this two-for-one half-kneeling lunge stretch.

Do these moves daily or several times per day to mobilize your lower back. Perform them before or after a run. Important caveat: If your QL hurts more after stretching—or if your QL pain returns soon after stretching—then stretches are likely not the solution and you should stop doing them, Haas explains.

To start, do one set of these moves daily to help your body learn the neuromuscular patterns. (The caveat: If you feel sore, then skip a day.) After about six weeks, do these moves two to three times per week as part of your regular strength-training routine.

This exercise targets the glutes and works on pelvic control in a movement pattern that’s similar to running. Think of it as an opportunity to train proper glute use in your running form, which will help unload any strain on your QL caused by weak glutes.

This exercise can improve core stability and strengthen the glutes, resulting in improved running mechanics and less stress on your QL. You’ll need a long, looped resistance band.

Sometimes, despite your best at-home efforts to treat an irritated QL, it’s still best to get checked out by a physical therapist. Haas recommends booking a PT appointment if you try the above stretches and strength moves and don’t see improvements after about two weeks.

Agnew suggests getting evaluated by a PT if you’re in so much pain that it’s inhibiting your running. In this case, getting seen sooner can help mitigate pain before further issues arise, Agnew explains.

Jenny is a Boulder, Colorado-based health and fitness journalist. She’s been freelancing for Runner’s World since 2015 and especially loves to write human interest profiles, in-depth service pieces and stories that explore the intersection of exercise and mental health. Her work has also been published by SELF, Men’s Journal, and Condé Nast Traveler, among other outlets. When she’s not running or writing, Jenny enjoys coaching youth swimming, rereading Harry Potter, and buying too many houseplants.

U.S. to Lose Measles Elimination Status After 26 Years. Here’s Why

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

The United States will likely lose its measles elimination status, a major public health achievement that was secured 26 years ago.

The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), the World Health Organization’s (WHO) regional office for the Americas, determined that measles has spread continuously in the U.S. for more than 12 months. This marks the end of the country’s measles elimination status, which it has held since 2000.

Researchers familiar with the discussions told KFF Health News that the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) determined this week that measles is once again spreading through sustained transmission within the country. PAHO is expected to make an official announcement on November 17.

PAHO’s review examined measles spread starting January 20, 2025, when the first person became ill in a large West Texas outbreak, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC).

CDC data show 5,412 confirmed measles cases this year as of November 12. That’s the highest annual total since 1991 and more than double the 2025 count of 2,289 cases.

Andi Shane, MD, chief of pediatric infectious diseases at Children’s Healthcare of Atlanta, called the U.S. losing measles elimination status “a public health alarm that we are not being effective and we’re not controlling measles as well.”

The measles virus is highly contagious. It spreads through the air when an infected person coughs or sneezes. People can catch it in a room up to two hours after an infected person has left, according to the CDC.

“There’s really been very little measles in the United States over the past 25 years until January 2025,” William Moss, MD, a professor of epidemiology at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, told Healthline.

Losing the status itself doesn’t change much about daily life for most Americans, Moss said. The concern is the measles outbreaks, hospitalizations and deaths behind the loss, he said.

He called the loss “a big red flag” after “enormous efforts over the past 26 years” to keep the status.

A country loses its elimination status when there is “uninterrupted circulation of the same virus genotype and lineage for 12 months or more,” according to PAHO. Shane explained it this way: “For the past 12 months in the United States, we’ve had ongoing circulation of the measles virus and person-to-person transmission is occurring.”

Canada lost its elimination status in November 2025, and the U.K. and five European countries lost theirs earlier in 2026.

The U.S. can regain the status. “Other countries have done that,” Moss said. Brazil, which lost its status in 2019, regained it in November 2024. Venezuela, which lost its status in 2018, regained it in 2023, according to PAHO.

Before the measles vaccine, measles infected an estimated 3 million to 4 million Americans each year, according to the CDC. Among reported cases, about 400 to 500 people died each year, 48,000 needed hospital care, and 1,000 developed encephalitis, or brain swelling.

Here’s a timeline of measles elimination in the U.S.:

Moss said elimination efforts “really intensified” after a large outbreak from 1989 to 1992. Part of that push was “really promoting school entry vaccination checks,” he said.

“That was one of the key policy changes that I think allowed for measles elimination in the United States.”

Every state and Washington, D.C., require children to be vaccinated against measles to attend school, though exemptions vary by state, according to the nonprofit health policy organization KFF.

Fewer kindergartners are getting the MMR vaccine, according to the CDC.

Kindergarten MMR coverage fell from 95.2% in the 2019–2020 school year to 92.4% in 2025–2026. About 280,000 kindergartners started the 2025–2026 school year without records showing they had completed the MMR series, the CDC reported.

Shane said “95 out of 100 people need to be protected against measles” for a community to get herd protection.

“So since we’ve been below that for many, many months, that is also contributing to the loss of our elimination status,” she said.

“It takes two things to have a measles outbreak,” Moss said. A community needs low vaccine coverage, and someone with measles has to enter it and “start the chain of transmission,” he said.

About 96% of this year’s cases were among people who were unvaccinated or had unknown vaccination status, up from 93% in 2025, according to the CDC.

Moss said many of those cases are adults.

“This is particularly true in Pennsylvania, where about two-thirds of the cases are in adults,” he said. Many never got vaccinated as kids and grew up while measles was rare, so they never built natural immunity, he said.

He noted that having so many unvaccinated adults puts pregnant people and adults with chronic conditions like lung disease at risk of serious illness. The U.S. has already seen “dozens of pregnant women actually getting infected during pregnancy,” he said.Infected parents can also spread measles to young children and grandparents, Shane said. Shane traced much of the hesitancy to the pandemic. “With COVID there’s been an overall distrust in vaccines and vaccination that initially started with COVID but has now spread to other vaccines,” she said.

Moss pointed to the federal government. “We know what it takes to achieve and sustain elimination. We have the expertise. We have the resources,” he said.

He cited staff cuts at the CDC and said messaging from Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and President Donald Trump has undermined vaccine confidence. He called federal messaging on vaccines “really mixed and weak.”

Moss also criticized the President’s August 10 executive order calling for the MMR vaccine to be split into separate shots for each disease.

“There’s no justification for that, and those vaccines don’t even exist in the United States,” he said.

Measles may cause ear infections, diarrhea, pneumonia, encephalitis and, in some cases, death, according to the CDC. The disease may also weaken the immune system for weeks to a year after recovery, raising the risk of flu, COVID-19, or RSV, Shane said. “It’s not a benign childhood disease,” Moss said.

The best protection against measles is two doses of the MMR vaccine, which are about 97% effective, according to the CDC. Shane said, “There are not very many other things we do in life that give you such great protection.” People who missed doses can still catch up, she added. “If you just don’t know or can’t tell, there’s no harm — as long as you’re eligible — in getting another MMR vaccine.”

Shane added that “parents really want to do the right thing for their children.”

“We know that the MMR vaccine — that’s the vaccine that provides protection against measles — is a safe vaccine. It’s a very effective vaccine,” Shane said.

Babies under 12 months are too young for the vaccine. “So they depend on the protection of the people around them,” Shane said.

The CDC recommends a first MMR dose at 12 to 15 months and a second at 4 to 6 years. Babies 6 to 11 months old who will travel internationally should get an early dose, and adults without proof of immunity should get at least one dose, according to the CDC.

To stop the outbreaks, Moss said the U.S. will need to “increase vaccine coverage, particularly in those communities that have had long-standing low vaccine rates.”

So far, the response has been “more reactive than proactive,” Moss said. Public health teams scramble once an outbreak starts, he said, and he wants more outreach to at-risk communities before measles arrives.

“I am worried about the ability of the United States to regain measles elimination status in the current climate,” Moss said.
