Mexican drug lord Miguel Angel Felix Gallardo dies age 80

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Mexican prison police secured the hospital where Miguel Ãngel FÃ©lix Gallardo was admitted earlier this week

Notorious Mexican drug lord Miguel Ãngel FÃ©lix Gallardo has died in hospital aged 80.

FÃ©lix Gallardo was widely considered one of the biggest drug smugglers in history and is credited with building an alliance between criminal groups in Colombia and his own Mexican cartel.

Known as The Godfather or the 'Boss of Bosses', FÃ©lix Gallardo was the head of the all-powerful Guadalajara Cartel, which reigned over Mexican drug trafficking at the height of the cocaine boom of the 1980s.

At the time, it created a new modus operandi in the illegal drug trade which endures to this day. He had been in state custody since 1989.

The name Miguel Ãngel FÃ©lix Gallardo will echo through the annals of drug-trafficking history more than any other except perhaps Pablo Escobar's.

He led the organisation which revolutionised drug smuggling in the region by entering into partnership with the Colombian cartels, particularly Escobar's MedellÃ­n Cartel, to combine production in the Andes with Mexican transportation across the border into the United States.

At one point an estimated 80% of all cocaine that entered the US came from the MedellÃ­n Cartel. FÃ©lix Gallardo and his organisation played a major role in smuggling it in.

At its height, the Guadalajara Cartel under FÃ©lix Gallardo influenced Mexican politics and law enforcement, controlling entire police networks and state governorships.

In 1985, the net started to tighten around the leaders of the cartel following the torture and murder of a DEA agent, Enrique 'Kiki' Camarena.

Camarena had angered them after he had discovered and then destroyed a vast marijuana plantation in Chihuahua, which belonged to the criminal group.

His death sparked one of the biggest manhunts the US government has ever launched in North America.

In 1989, FÃ©lix Gallardo was captured and sentenced to decades in prison. In ill health, he was placed under house arrest since 2022 and died in hospital in his native Guadalajara.

In the 1980s, Joaquin 'El Chapo' GuzmÃ¡n was introduced to FÃ©lix Gallardo, who put him in charge of handling logistics for his drug organisation.

From the subsequent break-up of FÃ©lix Gallardo's organisation after his arrest, the Sinaloa Cartel emerged, led by GuzmÃ¡n, now another man synonymous with large-scale drug trafficking.

In 2019, GuzmÃ¡n was sentenced to life in prison for charges including drug trafficking and money laundering following one of the most high-profile trials in recent US history.

Tallulah Willis: I Stayed Out Past My Bedtime to Celebrate My Mom at the Baby2Baby Gala

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The whole endeavor began as it usually does, with a search for the perfect dress. Turns out it would be a yellow Carolina Herrera column-shaped confection (if you care), teetering on yellow satin pointed peekaboo-toe platforms, with my first press pass thudding around in my corresponding yellow duchess satin clutch.

Stylist Brad Goreski and his assistant Clarke, who work with my mother, Demi Moore, sourced the ensemble after finding Mom’s beautiful black ruffled two-piece (adorably also Carolina Herrera).

Around 6:50 p.m. on Saturday, my older sisters, Scout (in cocoa-brown beaded Elie Saab) and Rumer (in whimsical archival Vera Wang), florist Eric Buterbaugh, Brad, my mom and I are about to leave her house for the Baby2Baby Gala, an annual fundraiser for the non-profit that dedicated to providing diapers, clothing, and other basic necessities to children and families in need across the country.

Demi is taking photos against a pale brown backdrop when I hear her say, marginally worried, “Yes, the outfit is amazing, but they didn’t give me pockets.” I cannot fault her. A pocket, particularly in an evening gown, creates an entirely different architecture for one’s night.

When we make it to the Pacific Design Center in West Hollywood, Rihanna is blasting and a gorgeous electric General Motors car, part of the silent auction, is parked out front. I’m holding the bottom of my dress to avoid any “yard sales” on the carpeted floor when I hear, “Is it you? I hope it’s you!” It’s my very new, lovely friend Jackie Tohn, whom I met at a Nobody Wants This premiere party.

Inside, beautifully dressed people laugh and chat. I spot Miranda Kerr, who is both a Baby2Baby board member and major donor, and pass Nara Smith leaning over a dolled-up Elsa Hosk while “Espresso” blasts from overhead speakers.

Table 4 is front and center. Some could say the best seats in the house. Fitting, as Baby2Baby is honoring my mother with the Giving Tree Award tonight. The scene around our table is a frenzy of joyful energy. I make a beeline for Paris Hilton to thank her for the facial I received at her spa this week, while Dancing With the Stars alums Rumer and Jenna Dewan immediately pull out their phones to compare pictures of their kids.

Then I spot Sofia Richie Grainge and her husband, Atlantic Records CEO Elliot Grainge. I snake through throngs of elegant people and poke Elliot’s back twice. “Elliot, it’s Tallulah! We went to Wildwood together!” He instantly remembers me. “Tallulah was the cool girl in class,” he tells Sofia. I blush, but don’t feel the need to correct him.

Scout approaches and says, almost incredulously, “Hi, I’m Scout…you guys just signed me at Atlantic Records.” Elliot grabs her shoulders. “We’re so happy to have you. Are you happy with us?” She can’t say “yes” fast enough.

The program begins with Keke Palmer, who completely owns the stage, telling hilarious stories about her son before shifting into heartfelt candor about the gift of watching him grow. Kerry Washington thanks the sponsors and announces Baby2Baby’s new permanent headquarters, “a promise to millions of children. Not just for today, but for decades to come.”

Then Kelly Sawyer Patricof and Norah Weinstein, the organization’s CEOs, come to the stage, sharing the work—no, the magic—they have created over 15 years. Their first gala in 2012 raised $1 million. Last year’s raised $18 million. To date, they’ve distributed an astonishing 850 million essential items to people in need across all 50 states, responded to over 150 disasters, developed their own diaper production line during COVID, and expanded into mental health support for new mothers. The mission remains the same, but the scale is impossible to comprehend.

Now Rumer, Scout, and I are all crying during a short video about two families impacted by Baby2Baby. The feeling is palpable for the rest of the room.

As the live auction begins, I catch my mom discreetly reading over a printout of her speech. I don’t feel nerves coming off her, but a desire to match the elegance and integrity surrounding us.

Andrew Garfield takes the stage and speaks directly to my mom about the woman he knows. “She is a miracle,” he says.

Then it’s Demi’s turn. Holding her gleaming award in two hands, she thanks Andrew for carving out time amid his Artificial press tour. My mother adores Andrew, a feeling shared across our family.

Alone on stage in her Carolina gown (sans pockets), she asks everyone to breathe. “Inhale. Exhale.” That breath, she says, is the relief Baby2Baby provides when a mother no longer has to choose between dinner and diapers. “Your child matters, you matter, and you are not alone.” She leaves us with the idea that the essence of this work is to be remarkably human. Baby to baby, mother to mother, soul to soul.

Then Cardi B takes the stage and the atmosphere shifts with a jolt. Everyone is on their feet dancing. The official after-party begins, but I’m turning back into a pumpkin in towering satin stilettos. It was a remarkably beautiful night, and I’m left with the overwhelming desire to do more—to show up for someone who may not have the ability to show up for themselves or their precious loved ones.

But for this on-scene reporter’s inaugural correspondence, it was ultimately Baby2Baby2Bedtime.

17 Years Ago, the Longest Running Sci-Fi Show Flipped Its Core Concept On Its Head

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

David Tennant has long been recognized as one of the greatest actors alive. He’s played scoundrels, cops, serial killers, and even a demon — an actual one, not a member of the Conservative Party, though he’s played one of those too.

If there’s one thing you can’t say about Tennant, it’s that he lacks range. He can play a lover, a fighter, or the scummiest person you know. But his most iconic role as the time-traveling alien known as the Doctor in the sci-fi family show Doctor Who brings all three together.

That might be a shocking statement to process. One of the Doctor’s core moral philosophies, articulated as early as Classic Who in 1963, is that the Time Lord is ultimately a peaceful person. During the Twelfth Doctor’s (Peter Capaldi) regeneration in 2017, one of his very last statements was that the future Doctor should “never be cruel, never be cowardly, and never ever eat pears! Remember, hate is always foolish, but love is always wise. Always try to be nice, but never fail to be kind.”

And yet, Tennant’s Tenth Doctor was the opposite of all those things during the 2009 autumnal Doctor Who special, "The Waters of Mars."

There have always been moments when the Doctor has shown that, for every person he has saved over the centuries, there have been just as many whom he has condemned.

However, the most memorable episode in which Whovians see the Doctor at his lowest point morally comes when the Time Lord lands on Mars in 2059 and encounters humanity’s first colony there: Bowie Base One. Led by Captain Adelaide Brooke (Lindsay Duncan), the colonists seem to be doing quite well for themselves, and there is hope that more of humanity will be able to join them in the future. Unfortunately, once the Doctor realizes who they are, another realization follows: it’s a fixed point in history in which everyone on Bowie Base One will die in an unknown cataclysm. Worse, it’s set to happen on the day the Doctor arrives.

The reason is revealed with the arrival of the Flood — an incredibly intelligent, waterborne alien virus. A single drop of the virus infects humans, turning them into zombie-like creatures with one sole aim: to keep spreading. One by one, the personnel of Bowie Base One perish, though one member cleverly destroys all the ships so the Flood cannot spread to Earth.

Throughout it all, the Doctor is racked with indecision. Messing with fixed points in history is usually seen as a big no-no, but he had done so before with his previous companion Donna Noble (Catherine Tate) when they saved a single family during “The Fires of Pompeii.” However, if he saves Adelaide, her granddaughter Susie won’t be inspired to become the first human captain to pilot a lightspeed ship to Proxima Centauri, a major historical event.

It’s a conundrum that arises while the Doctor is traveling alone, still reeling from the loss of his companion, Donna. In several previous episodes, the alien race known as the Ood has also warned him that his time is running out — regeneration is on the way.

This knowledge leaves the Doctor furious. No matter how many he saves, it seems all he does lately is lose. Now that he’s on his own, the fact that time and circumstance take so much away has driven him to the brink. He’s tired of losing; he wants to win.

After revealing to Adelaide her impending doom and her granddaughter’s achievement, the Doctor, in a moment of profound, unbridled hubris, delivers a speech that must be read in full to appreciate just how villainous it sounds:

“There are laws of time. Once upon a time there were people in charge of those laws, but they died. They all died. Do you know who that leaves? Me! It's taken me all these years to realize that the laws of time are mine and they will obey me!”

It’s a quote that feels exactly like it would come from the Doctor’s worst enemy, fellow Time Lord, The Master. Unlike the Doctor, the Master has always wanted to dominate others, making the Doctor’s subsequent rescue of Adelaide in the TARDIS feel even worse.

Unfortunately for the Doctor, his victory in giving time itself a big ‘F U’ is short-lived. Back on Earth, Adelaide is shaken by the Doctor’s self-perception as a god over time and berates the Time Lord for thinking he gets to live and choose who lives and who dies. She walks away and kills herself, righting history once more.

The Doctor's overwhelming pride has always been part of him. As much as he loves exploring the universe and often falls in love with humanity, he undoubtedly thinks himself better than most. There have been too many episodes to count in which a character has called the Doctor out for thinking others are stupid.

The Time Lord’s arrogance often leaves it to his companions to reel him back from the brink of destruction by reminding him of his humanity. In “The Waters of Mars,” it is easy to see why: If they don’t, the Doctor can easily become the very monster he so often fights.

Air pollution’s link to blood sugar has a surprising temperature twist

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A European study links air pollution and temperature swings to higher blood sugar, offering clues to how environmental conditions may interact.

Air pollution is usually discussed as a threat to the lungs, while heat is treated as a seasonal health risk.

In a study of more than 21,000 Europeans without diabetes, particle pollution and temperature variability were linked to higher long-term blood sugar. The association with each depended on exposure to the other.

In modeled scenarios, reducing air pollution was associated with a larger drop in blood sugar under warmer, more variable conditions.

The study found no measurable association between residential greenery and blood sugar.

Nicole Probst-Hensch and her colleagues at the Swiss Tropical and Public Health Institute and the University of Basel led the work.

Each person was measured once or a few times. So the study can show that pollution, temperature and blood sugar go together, not that one causes the other.

The team pooled nine long-running studies from Sweden, the Netherlands, Spain, the Czech Republic, Germany and Switzerland.

Together, they held 33,989 blood samples from 21,259 people, drawn between 1999 and 2021.

Ages ran from eight-year-olds in a Dutch birth cohort to the oldest adults in a Stockholm study.

The largest single group was 5,464 people from the GCAT study at the Germans Trias i Pujol Research Institute (IGTP) near Barcelona.

Each blood sample was tested for glycated hemoglobin, or HbA1c, which reflects average blood sugar over the previous two to three months.

Anyone with a diabetes diagnosis, diabetes medication or a reading in the diabetic range was left out, allowing the researchers to examine environmental associations in people without diabetes.

Each home address was then matched to a year of air pollution, greenery and temperature, from Europe-wide maps built by the EXPANSE project.

Of the three pollutants on the maps, only PM10 showed a consistent link to higher blood sugar.

PM10 includes airborne particles up to 10 micrometers across, including both fine and coarser particles. Nitrogen dioxide showed no consistent association, while the link with the finer PM2.5 was less consistent.

The other signal was how much the daily temperature swung over a year, more than the average temperature itself.

Their statistical method was built to find exposures that act together, and it did. Coarse particles and temperature swings interacted, so the effect of one depended on the level of the other.

The temperature-swing terms held up in nine of ten rounds of re-testing on different slices of the data.

One interaction, fine particles with average temperature, did not, and the authors treat it with caution.

Why would coarse particles matter for blood sugar? Larger particles get trapped in the upper airways, are moved up to the mouth and are swallowed, the authors wrote.

That fits other studies suggesting air pollution may alter gut bacteria.

The team then ran three futures through its model. The first cut coarse particles and nitrogen dioxide by 10 micrograms per cubic meter of air, and fine particles by 5.

These reductions were intended to approximate progress toward World Health Organization guideline levels. In the model, they were associated with lower blood sugar.

The second scenario raised the yearly average temperature by 0.9°F (0.5°C) and widened daily temperature swings by 1.8°F (1°C). The model associated these changes with higher blood sugar.

The third scenario combined cleaner air with warmer, more variable temperatures. Together, these changes were associated with a larger decrease in blood sugar than cleaner air alone.

The researchers expressed these modeled changes in standard deviations, a measure of how much blood sugar varied across the group.

Cleaner air alone was associated with a decrease of about 0.06 standard deviations, compared with about 0.07 under the combined scenario.

That is small for any one person. But most future diabetes comes from the many people at low or moderate risk, the authors wrote, so a small shift across a population matters.

The pattern was stronger in the 28,089 samples from nonsmokers and absent in the 5,900 from smokers.

Smoking causes inflammation of its own, the authors wrote, which may mask the effect.

Trees and parks are the usual prescription for a hot, polluted city. The study measured greenness around each home from satellite images, and the distance to the nearest green space.

Neither was linked to blood sugar, on its own or in combination with pollution or heat.

The links between pollution, temperature variability and blood sugar appeared almost exclusively in cities. In the less urban half of the samples, no stable link appeared.

The authors put that down to higher pollution and stronger heat islands in urban areas.

Natàlia Blay, a postdoctoral researcher at GCAT and a co-author, put the result in a wider frame.

“Including temperature in this approach is particularly relevant in the current context of climate change, where environmental exposures cannot be understood in isolation,” she said.

Diet and physical activity were not included in the models.

Participants had blood sugar measured once or a few times, so the study could not establish whether pollution caused higher blood sugar or whether other differences between people explained the association.

When the team split the group by body weight, the links disappeared in both halves.

The authors suggested that body fat might help explain the association, although this analysis could not establish its role.

The temperature maps began in 2003, so earlier blood samples were matched to later weather. HbA1c reflects only the three months before a blood draw, while the exposures were yearly averages.

Traffic noise, which other studies have tied to diabetes, had no Europe-wide map and was left out.

Whether cleaning the air of a hot city lowers its diabetes rate is the question the scenarios point at but can’t answer.

The authors wrote that noise belongs in the next round of this work, once Europe-wide noise maps exist.

The full study was published in the journal Environment International.

These are some of the strongest 3D printer filaments for durable parts

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Stronger filament doesn't automatically mean a stronger print, and the toughest materials can require better hardware too.

If you're looking for one winner, it's not exactly convenient that a 3D print can be "strong" in a few different ways. A rigid bracket should resist bending, while a part that gets knocked around may last longer if it can flex and recover instead of breaking. In this article, we're going to hone in on rigidity and the types of materials that work well for structural parts.

Carbon-fiber-reinforced nylon (PA-CF) and polycarbonate (PC) are two of the strongest practical materials for the kind of filament-based 3D printers that are accessible to the average maker. Both materials can handle demanding functions, higher temperatures and considerably rougher treatment than everyday polylactic acid (PLA) or glycol-modified polyethylene terephthalate (PETG).

Sure, there are stronger, more expensive specialty materials that could vie for the title of heavyweight filament champ. But let's stick to the pros and cons of carbon-fiber nylon versus polycarbonate, so you can decide which material packs the most punch for your projects.

Carbon-fiber-reinforced nylon (PA-CF) combines nylon with short carbon fibers — hence the name. Nylon already handles demanding functional parts well, and the added fibers make the finished material stiffer and more resistant to bending under load. How resistant, you ask?

Bambu Lab's dry-state test puts its carbon-fiber-reinforced nylon 6 (PA6-CF) at 151 megapascals (MPa) of bending strength. Its heat-deflection temperature is 186 degrees Celsius, meaning it resisted deformation under a specified load until around that temperature. Those figures help explain why carbon-fiber nylon is used for brackets, fixtures and other working parts instead of for items that simply sit pretty on a shelf.

A strong filament, however, does not necessarily make a strong print. How you orient the object on the print bed, plus wall thickness, infill and part shape, can significantly change how much abuse the project can take. Bambu Lab does not claim the 151 MPa result applies to every finished print. Pricing is another factor to note, as Polymaker currently lists a 1kg spool of its Fiberon PA6-CF20 at $52.99 at time of writing

There is some hardware housekeeping to contemplate too. Carbon fibers are abrasive, so your old faithful brass nozzle may not last long. Bambu recommends a hardened steel nozzle, requires PA6-CF to be dried before use, and lists a nozzle temperature of 260 to 290 degrees Celsius with an enclosed printer when using this material.

The National Institute for Occupational Safety and Health (NIOSH) says fused-filament desktop printers can emit respiratory irritants and ultrafine particles, so good ventilation or filtration is important to consider.

Unlike carbon-fiber composites, polycarbonate (PC) produces strong parts without chopped fibers. It's a stiff engineering plastic with good heat resistance, making it suitable for housings, brackets and other parts that need to withstand more than light use.

Once again, you'll need to consider your equipment and workspace before deciding if PC is the best and strongest filament for you. Similar to PA-CF, Polymaker recommends a nozzle temperature of 250 to 270 degrees Celsius, a 90 to 105 degrees Celsius heated bed, and an enclosed printer. The company says large, dense parts require a heated chamber, which helps reduce uneven cooling and warping. Good workplace ventilation or exhaust is recommended.

When it comes to strength, print direction matters, too. Polymaker measured 69.1 MPa of tensile strength across the printed layers and 52.8 MPa between them. PC's bending strength clocked in at 106 MPa, a drop compared to the 151 MPa logged for PA-CF.

The big difference with PC versus PA-CF is the price. Polymaker lists PolyLite PC at $29.99 for a 1kg spool and PolyLite PLA at $19.99at time of writing. The bigger pain point could be your printer, since lower-cost models may struggle to maintain the temperatures PC prefers.

Vitamin C could help block cancer-linked chemicals in your stomach, study finds

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Vitamin C may do more than support everyday health. New research from the University of Waterloo suggests that it could also help prevent potentially cancer-causing chemicals from forming during digestion. Using mathematical modeling, scientists investigated how vitamin C influences chemical reactions in the digestive system that have been linked to cancer risk.

Exposure to nitrates and nitrites has increased in North American diets over the past several decades. These compounds are found in cured meats such as bacon and salami, as well as in fruits and vegetables grown in soil or water affected by pollution. Although nitrates and nitrites contribute to important functions in the brain and cardiovascular system, they can also participate in potentially harmful reactions once they reach the stomach.

One of these reactions, called nitrosation, can produce substances that researchers suspect may contribute to cancer development. Understanding what causes these chemicals to form, and what might prevent their formation, has been a longstanding challenge.

Why Vitamin C Could Change the Cancer Risk Equation

"Since at least the 90s, researchers have been studying the link between cancer and these compounds, with conflicting results," said Dr. Gordon McNicol, a post-doctoral researcher in applied mathematics and the first author of the study. "Our work suggests that the presence of dietary Vitamin C may help explain these inconsistencies."

To investigate this possibility, the researchers developed a mathematical model that simulates how nitrates and nitrites travel through the body and undergo chemical changes. The model incorporated several interconnected systems, including the salivary glands, stomach, small intestine, and plasma, the liquid portion of blood.

Their simulations revealed that vitamin C consumed alongside nitrates and nitrites may interfere with the formation of potentially harmful compounds. This could help explain why foods containing nitrates do not necessarily carry the same risks.

For example, leafy vegetables such as spinach naturally contain both nitrates and vitamin C. According to the model, the presence of vitamin C could limit the chemical reactions associated with cancer risk, potentially helping explain the protective effects suggested by some dietary research.

The researchers also explored whether vitamin C supplements might offer similar benefits when taken after eating.

Their findings suggest that taking vitamin C after each meal could moderately reduce the formation of nitrosation products associated with cancer risk. This effect could be particularly relevant when consuming foods containing nitrates and nitrites, including processed meats such as bacon and salami.

However, the results come from mathematical simulations rather than clinical trials. Although the model predicts that vitamin C can reduce the production of potentially harmful chemicals, further research is needed to determine whether this translates into a measurable reduction in cancer risk in humans.

New Clues for Future Cancer Prevention Research

The researchers believe their model could help scientists better understand the complicated relationship between diet, digestive chemistry, and cancer risk. It may also provide guidance for future experiments examining how nutritional choices influence the formation of potentially harmful compounds.

"This work provides a mechanistic roadmap for future clinical and laboratory studies by identifying the key interacting drivers of these potentially harmful chemical reactions, including nitrite exposure, antioxidant intake, meal timing, gastric conditions and oral microbiome activity," said Dr. Anita Layton, professor of applied mathematics and Canada 150 researcher chair. "This model can help researchers design more targeted experiments and interventions, focusing on when and in whom nitrosation is most likely to occur."

By identifying how factors such as vitamin C intake, meal timing, stomach conditions, and bacteria in the mouth interact, the model could help researchers develop more precise strategies for studying and potentially reducing cancer-related chemical reactions during digestion.

The study, "Vitamin C as a nitrosation inhibitor: A modelling study across dietary patterns and water quality," was published in the Journal of Theoretical Biology.

Materials provided by University of Waterloo. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Does Menopausal Hormone Therapy Lower Dementia Risk? Timing and Genetics May Hold the Answer

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Women who have longer reproductive life spans or use menopausal hormone therapy (MHT) may have better brain health as they age, according to a new study.

The research, published November 4 in Neurology, found that longer lifetime exposure to estrogen was associated with fewer signs of damage to small blood vessels in the brain.

The findings could offer new insights into the relationship between estrogen and brain aging, particularly as women account for approximately two-thirds of Alzheimer’s disease cases worldwide.

Researchers also found that certain associations between hormone therapy and brain health differed depending on whether women carried a genetic variant associated with an increased risk of Alzheimer’s disease.

However, the relationship between estrogen and brain health is complex. While the findings suggest that longer estrogen exposure may be associated with healthier brain aging, they do not establish that hormone therapy can prevent Alzheimer’s disease or dementia.

The researchers drew data from the Gothenburg H70-1944 Birth Cohort, a longitudinal study of 1,203 individuals born in 1944 who resided in Gothenburg, Sweden.

The final cohort included 352 cisgender women, with an average age of 70, who had no neurological disorders and reliable self-reported data on their estrogen exposure.

Individual estrogen exposure was measured through structured interviews. It included:

The researchers used this information as a proxy for lifetime estrogen exposure, though the study didn’t measure actual hormone levels.

Participants were also given a brain MRI at baseline and then again at a 5-year follow-up.

The researchers found that those with a longer reproductive span had a lower risk of small vessel disease (SVD). Participants with a longer reproductive life span had an 8% lower risk of having two or more signs of SVD. However, later menopause, rather than earlier menstruation, appeared to be the main driver of this association.

Those who used MHT also tended to have fewer signs of SVD and better microstructural integrity in the white matter.

In individuals who carried the APOE-ε4 variant, which increases a person’s risk of Alzheimer’s disease, MHT was associated with greater cortical thickness in brain regions vulnerable to Alzheimer’s disease. However, there was no association for those without this variant.

The researchers noted that longer estrogen exposure was associated with stable cortical thickness and white matter trajectories over time.

Dung Trinh, MD, internist at MemorialCare Medical Group and Chief Medical Officer of Healthy Brain Clinic in Irvine, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that estrogen has several important effects on the brain that may help support cognitive health.

It influences communication between brain cells, supports the brain’s ability to use glucose for energy, helps regulate neurotransmitters, and plays a role in maintaining healthy blood vessels and reducing inflammation and oxidative stress.

“As estrogen levels decline during menopause, some of these protective effects may diminish. Replacing estrogen closer to the menopause transition could potentially help preserve some of these functions during a vulnerable period for brain health,” he said. “While these findings are encouraging, MHT has not been proven to prevent Alzheimer’s disease or dementia.”

Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who also wasn’t involved in the study, agreed and noted, “Starting menopausal hormone therapy earlier in the menopause transition may help minimize some changes by supporting the brain during this period of hormonal fluctuation.”

“There is growing interest in whether early estrogen therapy may also help reduce the long-term risk of Alzheimer’s disease, although more research is needed before we can establish that benefit,” she added.

Ross told Healthline that this research reinforces the importance of recognizing menopause as more than a reproductive or hormonal transition.

“We know that women are disproportionately affected by dementia and Alzheimer’s disease, and some women experience noticeable cognitive changes beginning in perimenopause,” she said. “If future research confirms that starting hormone therapy earlier can help protect long-term cognitive health, it could influence how we approach menopause treatment and encourage more proactive conversations between women and their healthcare providers.”

“The goal should be to individualize treatment based on a woman’s symptoms, medical history, and overall health rather than taking a one-size-fits-all approach,” Ross added.

Trinh agreed that an individualized approach to menopause management is important.

“This does not mean that every woman should start hormone therapy to prevent dementia. Instead, it highlights the importance of understanding which women might benefit, when treatment should begin, and which formulations are most appropriate,” he said. “Factors such as age, time since menopause, cardiovascular risk, metabolic health, family history, and potentially genetic factors may all help guide future recommendations.”

“We still need stronger clinical evidence before changing current prescribing guidelines specifically for dementia prevention,” Trinh added.

Hailey Bieber Says She's Been Diagnosed With PMDD: What Are the Signs, Symptoms?

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Hailey Bieber is sharing her experience with premenstrual dysphoric disorder (PMDD), a condition she says has fueled her growing interest in women’s health research.

The Rhode Beauty founder recently announced a $5 million, three-year partnership with the Foundation for Women’s Health in an interview with Women’s Wear Daily (WWD).

“As a woman, I feel frustrated by the lack of research, the lack of awareness, the lack of certain conversation around it,” Bieber told WWD.

Motherhood has also shaped her perspective. Bieber welcomed her son, Jack Blues, with her husband, Justin Bieber, in 2024, describing the experience as a “really intense and special journey.”

She also shared that she “struggled” with PMDD after giving birth, noting that there’s “not a lot of conversation or research around it.”

“When we can identify with each other and have conversations relating to each other and talking about the things we’re dealing with and struggling with, I do think it makes it feel like less of a lonely journey,” Bieber said.

PMDD is a complexcondition that causes severe physical and psychological symptoms that typically occur in the days or weeks leading up to menstruation.

“What distinguishes PMDD from the more common PMS (premenstrual syndrome) is the severity of the symptoms and how significantly they can interfere with daily functioning,” Sari Eitches, MD, dual board certified physician and medical advisor to Terra Mare, told Healthline.

“Another defining feature is the timing: symptoms typically worsen in the week or two before a period, resolve within a few days after menstruation begins, and are minimal or absent during the rest of the cycle,” she said.

Eitches also noted that PMDD is a chronic condition that can affect women at any stage of their reproductive lives, from adolescence through perimenopause, as long as they are having menstrual cycles.

“It is caused by a sensitivity to cyclic hormonal and neurotransmitter fluctuations, so [it] can occur in anyone with a menstrual cycle, though stages of hormonal fluctuation- including postpartum, weaning breastfeeding, and perimenopause- can be a trigger,” she added.

According to research published in 2024, approximately 1.6% of women and girls meet the strict diagnostic criteria for PMDD, equivalent to an estimated 31 million people worldwide.

A higher percentage of 3.2% have a provisional diagnosis, meaning a healthcare professional may believe a person has PMDD, but symptoms have not been measured for a sustained amount of time to meet the criteria for a confirmed diagnosis.

Eitches said that women with a personal or family history of depression or anxiety may be more prone to developing PMDD, as are women with a family history of PMDD itself.

“PMDD is understood to be caused by a combination of heritable biological sensitivities and environmental triggers. These can include a history of trauma, chronic stress, and other chronic medical conditions,” she noted.

Eitches also said that you should consider seeing a doctor if your symptoms are severe enough that they’re interfering with your ability to go to work or school, maintain your relationships, or participate in your life in the same way that you do during the other weeks of the month.

To diagnose PMDD, a healthcare professional will review your medical history and symptoms and may perform a physical exam. They will typically ask you to track your symptoms over at least two menstrual cycles.

A PMDD diagnosis generally requires at least five symptoms, including at least one mood-related symptom, that occur before menstruation, improve shortly after a period begins, and significantly interfere with daily life.

Treatment options for PMDD include prescription medications and over-the-counter treatments that may help relieve certain symptoms, such as:

Eitches noted that cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) can also help manage symptoms.

Lifestyle changes may also help manage PMDD symptoms, including practicing relaxation techniques, eating a balanced diet, limiting sugary and salty foods, and getting regular physical activity.

Eitches noted that getting enough high quality sleep and resting on a consistent schedule is also important.

“I also recommend focusing on a whole-foods-based diet, limiting added sugar and caffeine, and avoiding cigarette smoking,” she said.

DJ Khaled Shows Off Dramatic Weight Loss After Hitting 300 Pounds

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

DJ Khaled is dropping more than just hit records these days ... the music mogul has shed nearly 100 pounds, and he's giving Wegovy some of the credit for his jaw-dropping transformation!

Starship flight 15 powers to orbit—setting up a possible catch on Saturday

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

One way or another, the company will get some more data on Saturday.

STARBASE, Texas—Starship lifted off right on schedule Friday from South Texas, making its 15th test flight in more than three years.

The largely successful launch took place in the early evening; as the massive rocket rose into the atmosphere, the Sun sank toward the western horizon. Conditions were ideal for the launch, with the atmospheric winds so calm the rocket’s white plume did not dissipate for many minutes. The weather in South Texas is not always comfortable; summers are long and hot. Friday evening was perfect at Starbase.

The first stage ascended toward space flawlessly, releasing the upper stage above the Gulf of Mexico. From there, the Starship upper stage ignited its Raptor engines and powered its way into a nominal orbit. This was the second time SpaceX has flown Starship into low-Earth orbit.

When it came time for the first stage to return to the ground, it dropped like a rock. However, due to an unspecified issue, the vehicle was commanded to divert into the Gulf rather than return to the Starbase facility to be caught by the launch tower. It created a fantastically powerful explosion, even for observers more than 10 miles away.

The primary aim of Friday’s launch was the deployment of 26 Starlink V3 satellites from a narrow door at the base of the spacecraft. This occurred about 40 minutes into Starship’s flight, and all 26 satellites—which have solar panels that stretch 60 meters end to end when fully deployed—safely made it into orbit, where they will now use onboard propulsion to raise their altitude.

This means that Starship is now an operational, orbital rocket. It is the largest and most powerful machine humans have ever launched into space. The challenge for the company now is to make Starship reusable and dramatically increase its flight rate.

In that sense, the first part of Friday’s mission was not a success. SpaceX had hoped to return the first-stage booster, refurbish it, and fly it again. The company has previously landed and reflown Starship boosters but has yet to do so with the latest iteration of the vehicle, version 3.

However, SpaceX still has a chance to make history on Saturday with the potential return of the Starship upper stage. SpaceX has never attempted to catch the vehicle’s upper stage.

Doing so will require Starship to pass a series of tests in orbit and subsequently during its return to Earth to ensure it is fully healthy. This is critical because Starship will be passing over parts of Mexico and the United States before returning to Starbase, and SpaceX does not want to endanger people on the ground. Additionally, it does not want to risk damage to its launch towers.

If Starship passes these health checks, it would land at about 3:15 pm local time in Texas (21:15 UTC). If it does not, the company has earlier options to de-orbit the vehicle into an ocean.

Successfully returning Starship’s upper stage would be historic, as well as significant for SpaceX’s efforts to reuse the massive rocket. It would also give the company valuable information about the vehicle’s performance during reentry.

One of the biggest hurdles left in Starship’s development is the vehicle’s heat shield, which must protect the upper stage during its return through Earth’s atmosphere. More than that, SpaceX aims to make Starship rapidly reusable, and to do this, Starship’s tiles must be light and resilient.

SpaceX has been experimenting with tiles since the beginning of the Starship program, trying to find the right combination of low mass, heat resistance, and durability so the tiles require minimal refurbishment between launches. For this flight, SpaceX said it is applying learnings from the previous test flight in September, including additional retention mechanisms on tiles in areas deemed to be at the highest risk of falling off during ascent and addressing areas with a flow path for plasma to get behind the tiles.

Another problem on recent flights has been heat incursion into the small gaps between the edges of the tiles. According to SpaceX, this Starship has a large portion of the heat shield equipped with a curved tile design that has shown the ability to reduce heating in the gaps between tiles.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/trump-administration-delays-enforcement-medicaid-work-requirements-six-months" hreflang="en">Trump administration delays enforcement of Medicaid work requirements for six months</a>

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Trump administration delays enforcement of Medicaid work requirements for six months" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Trump administration is giving states an optional six-month grace period for enforcing new Medicaid work requirements, which were due to go into effect January 1.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) announced Friday an optional "hold harmless" period through June 30, 2027, to give states a transition period to further modernize systems, improve processes and streamline the applicant and beneficiary experience.

During the "hold harmless" period, states will still assess whether Medicaid applicants and beneficiaries meet the work requirements or qualify for an exclusion, but otherwise eligible people will not lose or be denied coverage solely because they fail to meet the requirement, CMS said.

CMS says the transition is intended to give states time to improve eligibility systems, automate verification, train workers and improve the beneficiary experience.

"States have made real progress, and they’ve been clear with us about what it takes to get this right for beneficiaries—at scale and for the long term," Dan Brillman, Deputy Administrator and Director of the Center for Medicaid and CHIP Services, said in a statement. "If the state can use data to show that someone is working or qualifies for an exclusion, that person should not have to track down paperwork to prove it. And when someone needs support, they should know exactly what to do, when to do it, and where to go. That’s how we build a Medicaid program that works better for people, connects them to work, education, and training opportunities, and supports paths to prosperity."

During the six-month enforcement pause, CMS encourages states to use existing data to automatically verify qualifying activities and exemptions before requesting additional information from beneficiaries.

States are also encouraged to simplify notices and make the process easy to navigate so beneficiaries understand what the state has already verified, what information is still needed and how to respond or get help. States should also test eligibility systems end-to-end, particularly how the systems handle missing, outdated or conflicting information, to resolve operational issues and minimize inappropriate coverage disruptions.

CMS also called on states to coordinate with community partners to connect applicants and beneficiaries who need assistance with opportunities for employment, education, job training and volunteering.

The work requirements were established as part of last summer's massive H.R. 1 legislation, or the One Big Beautiful Bill Act. Under the mandate, certain Medicaid applicants and beneficiaries ages 19 to 64 must meet a statutory work requirement, unless excluded. There are exemptions in place for the medically frail, disabled and others who are unable to meet the mandates.

CMS’ June 2026 interim final rule outlines qualifying activities, including 80 hours per month of work, community service or participation in a work program; enrollment in an educational program at least half-time; a combination of qualifying activities totaling at least 80 hours per month; or meeting the specified monthly income threshold.

State Medicaid administrators, health insurers, physicians and hospitals have pushed back on the Medicaid work requirements, citing concerns about financial and operational challenges, a spike in uncompensated care and the rule's strict "medical frailty" exemptions.

Industry groups argue the requirements impose onerous documentation burdens. The Alliance of Community Health Plans said that the final rule adds documentation requirements and tightens criteria for exemptions to the requirements, which will make it harder for individuals who need coverage to secure exemptions they may qualify for.

The organization also noted that most individuals enrolled in Medicaid are already working or in school.

Jennifer Tolbert, deputy director of the program on Medicaid and the uninsured at KFF, said in a quick take post back in June that "the rule adopts a restrictive definition of medical frailty that differs from states’ early expectations."

"Given the complexity of the provisions in the rule, states will likely face significant challenges in operationalizing the requirements in the next six months," Tolbert wrote. "With the short implementation timeline, states were already making implementation decisions that may not align with the rule."

Studies estimate that between 5 million and 10 million people could lose Medicaid coverage in 2028 following the rollout of new work requirements.

The Trump administration's tight definitions of medical frailty and documentation burdens have triggered multiple lawsuits from states, medical organizations and advocacy groups. A coalition of 26 states sued the Trump administration over its rollout of Medicaid work requirements. arguing that key provisions could cause eligible people to lose coverage and disrupt states’ implementation efforts. The states argue CMS’ new definition of who qualifies as “medically frail” is too restrictive and could make it harder for people with serious health conditions to receive exemptions.

In October, a Massachusetts judge sided with the states and rejected the Trump administration's definition of "medical frailty" for critical exemptions.

A group of physician groups, individual Medicaid enrollees and the city of Columbus also sued the federal government to challenge the work requirements.

State Medicaid agencies have warned that the accelerated IT and administrative build required by January 1, 2027, is putting severe pressure on local welfare offices and safety-net hospital operating margins, as Senior Writer Anastassia Gliadkovskaya reported.

Want to Turn Back the Clock? These 10 Diets May Help You Age Better

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

You’ve got way more options than just the Mediterranean diet.

When healthy aging is the goal, it makes sense to try everything you can to make it happen. That means doing your best to exercise regularly, keep up with doctor's appointments, and eat a well-balanced diet. You have lots of options when it comes to the last one—but it can be hard to know which of the many popular eating plans is right for you. Thankfully, new research has helped narrow the choices.

The study, which was published in Nature Communications, analyzed several different diets to see how they impacted biological aging. (Biological aging is the age of your cells, while your chronological age is how old you are.) Popular eating plans like the Mediterranean diet and the DASH (Dietary Approaches to Stop Hypertension) diet made the cut, of course, but several lesser-known diets did too. Here’s what to know.

Meet the experts: Jessica Cording, RD, author of The Little Book of Game-Changers; Keri Gans, RD, author of The Small Change Diet; and Sonya Angelone, RD, PhD, a former spokeswoman for the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics and a San Francisco-based dietitian.

For the study, researchers analyzed data from 6,470 participants who were 30 and older. Researchers looked at some of the participants' biomarkers that correlated with biological age and compared that with information about their diets.

After analyzing the data, researchers found that healthier eating in general was linked with slower biological aging, no matter which diet people chose. Each specific eating plan offered an edge with certain perks, though. Case in point: The DASH diet (which is specifically meant to stop hypertension) had more cardiovascular benefits for biological aging, while the MIND diet (which combines the Mediterranean and DASH diets for brain health) offered cognitive perks. But researchers discovered that there were a lot of shared biological aging benefits between the different types of diets.

These are the 10 diets flagged as being good for biological aging:

All of these diets are slightly different, but there are a few common threads. "They generally emphasize fruits, vegetables, whole grains, and other nutrient-dense foods," says Keri Gans, RD, author of The Small Change Diet.

The diets also stress the importance of minimally processed foods, while limiting refined carbs, added sugars, and processed meats, according to Sonya Angelone, RD, PhD, a former spokeswoman for the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics and a San Francisco-based dietitian. "The recommended foods provide fiber, antioxidants, polyphenols, vitamins, minerals, and healthy fats that support metabolic health and help protect cells from oxidative stress and chronic inflammation," she says. Oxidative stress and chronic inflammation are both linked to a higher risk of disease, Angelone points out.

These diets also support the heart, brain, and digestive systems, says Jessica Cording, RD, author of The Little Book of Game-Changers. "All of these things factor into the aging process and somebody's quality of life as they age," she says. "When you're eating to support these different organ systems, you support your longevity and lower your risk of disease."

There are a few things to take away from the findings, according to dietitians. One is that there isn't a single eating plan that's superior to others when it comes to longevity. "It's the overall quality of what we eat consistently that matters most, rather than following a particular diet or avoiding individual foods," Gans says.

While there are plenty of diet plans out there that claim they can help you live longer, Angelone stresses this: "Healthy aging doesn’t have to include expensive foods, supplements, or restrictive diets."

Finally, the study underscores the power of nutrition in keeping you healthy. "Our genes are not our destiny," Angelone says. "We can change the expression of our genes—genomics—with nutrition and lifestyle habits."

Supporting healthy aging with the way you eat means giving your body the fuel it requires to function well, Angelone says. "Healthy aging isn’t about finding a perfect diet or miracle food or supplement," she says. "It is about consistently providing foods that supply nutrients needed by our bodies to function optimally, repair cellular damage, and protect themselves. This will impact how we age."

Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: What a Missed Deadline Taught Me About Switching Off

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

I missed a deadline last week. The piece you’re reading was supposed to be filed before I headed out on a long weekend to Italy for my wife’s birthday.

The main problem was I simply ran out of time to write the piece. An urgent stakeholder ask out of the blue, a couple of unanticipated work fire drills, and a long-standing evening social commitment earlier in the week made for a perfect storm, and something had to give.

The other problem and consequence was that I carried a chunk of guilt into the break.

It caught up with me at 6 am on the first morning, when I found myself drafting this column in my head. I should have been dozing peacefully. I continued to ruminate on my lengthy to-do list at work deep into day one as we headed from Venice into the Dolomites. My body was in Italy, but my brain was clearly still at my desk.

Switching off, particularly from work, is hard for most of us, and especially in an age of ubiquitous screens and wall-to-wall information competing for our attention. For me, that always-on reality plays a major role in the difficulties I have disconnecting from the daily grind.

I’m fortunate to love my job, and if I’m candid, I probably take a little too much of my identity from it. I hold on tight, and the phone in my pocket is an ever-present enabler.

A meme that has stuck with me over the years goes something along the lines of being an adult is staring at medium screens all day only to come home and stare at a little screen while a big screen is on in the background. The irony is not lost on me that I came across this rather withering take on modern life while scrolling on said little screen.

Regardless of the role the screen plays, psychologists who study employee well-being describe what I was missing for the early part of the weekend as “psychological detachment.” A state where you are genuinely able to not think about work while away from it. The science suggests it’s an important thing to work on.

A 2025 meta-analysis of 32 studies found that vacationing has a strong effect on well-being, and that the effect lasts longer than earlier research suggested. A second, smaller analysis of eight studies points to detaching from work, along with physical activity, as among the most helpful things to do while you’re off. In another study that tracked teachers during a two-week break, exhaustion, anxiety, and low mood all declined, while those who kept ruminating about work recovered less.

Whatever the science says, I know that for me the act of stepping away from work and being disciplined about not checking back in while I’m away is critical for my mental health and for staving off burnout. Getting out in nature and hiking or cycling or swimming keeps me present. It resets and recharges my batteries, and it tops up my creativity in a way that I find benefits what I am then able to bring back to the office.

Sure enough, as we drove deeper into the mountains on a crisp, blue-sky day, I could feel the golden autumnal hues and majestic rock formations drawing me in, closer to an elemental feeling of calm and happiness. That sense of equilibrium stayed with me all weekend, and while I’d be lying if I said my mind didn’t occasionally drift to my desk, it was fleeting, with the knowledge that all the things would be waiting for me when I got back. Just like this piece was.

With Thanksgiving on the horizon, and the holidays coming thick and fast thereafter, I’ll leave you with this bit of hard-earned advice: if your job is deeply entwined with your life and part of who you are, as mine is, time away can sometimes feel like a small act of disloyalty. It isn’t. In fact, it’s probably the most important thing you can do to remain faithful to your work ethic, and to yourself.

And if you’ll be working over the holidays, or you’re not working right now, or you don’t have the benefit of someone on hand to carry some of the load for you (shout out to all the Grannies out there), or your vacation looks very different from mine, that’s all okay. Find the pockets where and when you can: an evening, a walk, an hour in front of a screen, or better yet a book, and let work wait. Above all else, be kind to yourself. That’s how you really give yourself a break.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Wednesday in our Healthline Wellness Wire newsletter.

LNGSHOT Talk Musical Attitude & Group’s Turning Point: ‘S.W.I.S.H.’

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Ahead of the EP's release, Billboard Korea sat down with LNGSHOT for an exclusive interview.

LNGSHOT return with their new EP S.W.I.S.H. The release continues the “hustle” the group has kept up since debuting in January this year with their first EP SHOT CALLERS. Since then, they have put out two mixtapes, the EP Training Day collecting their pre-debut work, and a string of OSTs and singles.

S.W.I.S.H. captures LNGSHOT’s style in a different way from SHOT CALLERS. Every track on SHOT CALLERS has its own distinct feel. On S.W.I.S.H., you can hear how the tracks connect to each other. Woojin added that it is “an album with a really fun musical attitude.”

S.W.I.S.H. marks both LNGSHOT’s growth and a turning point for the group. Ahead of its release, Billboard Korea sat down with LNGSHOT for an exclusive interview.

Since September, you’ve been special guests on Jay Park’s world tour SERENADES & BODY ROLLS. How did it feel to perform for overseas audiences, and are there any moments that stand out?

Woojin: I remember that a lot of Louis’s family came to our show in France. We were also lucky to tour with Jay Park for two months, and every show sold out except in one city. The crowds were so responsive, and that gave us a lot of strength. Early on after our debut there was so much to worry about. But the more we performed and the more the audience responded, the more relaxed we got, and we could just have fun. We really enjoyed it. We’re the first idol group Jay Park has created, so I think when we look back later, these two months on the road with him as special guests will feel like a one-of-a-kind moment.

Ryul: I think I learned how to enjoy myself on stage. He also passed on a lot of know-how, like fan service, how to treat fans, and how to handle a busy schedule. He taught us how to take care of our mental health and how to stay mentally strong. We were preparing our comeback while touring, so we matured in some ways, and we learned a lot about what we need to do on stage and going forward.

How did you balance the tour with preparing for your comeback?

Woojin: Even before the tour, we kept making songs whenever we had time. In July and August, before we left, we sorted out all the songs, re-recorded them, and revised every part. During the tour, we shot the music video overseas, changed the choreography, and did more recording. Funnily enough, “Bananas” is a demo we’d been considering since before our debut.

Tell us what makes this album S.W.I.S.H. and its title track “Bananas” different.

Woojin: Looking back to our debut, I think we got off to a good start. I think we had the energy to show people what makes us appealing. On this album, more and more of our own style is coming through, the same way the Memphis sound did on SHOT CALLERS.

“Bananas” is a song with a really fun musical attitude. The hook makes you feel like things are about to get hyped and you’re about to turn up, but then there’s a twist where it dies down. That’s where it hooks you even more. That attitude is what makes it fun.

Ryul: The more you listen to S.W.I.S.H., the more strangely addictive it gets. The tracks are connected, so it’s even better when you listen all the way through.

Ohyul: Every track is good. We started with SHOT CALLERS by making what we love, and that start went well, so I believe people will connect with this too. We made this album in our own style as much as we could.

What time of day would you recommend listening to S.W.I.S.H.? Tell us what feels right to each of you.

Ohyul: No matter what time it is, if our music makes you turn up, I think that makes it a good song. So I hope people listen anytime and turn up. (laughs)

Ryul: All of us, me included, keep a close eye on fan reactions through our 4shoboiz account (LNGSHOT’s second Instagram account). The Instagram algorithm showed me a post from someone who said listening to “Never Let Go” (track 5 on SHOT CALLERS) before bed helps them fall asleep. I hope the songs on S.W.I.S.H. also stay with listeners, whatever time of day they listen.

How was “Bananas” chosen as the title track?

Woojin: Before our debut, the production team sent us links to demos. There were quite a few songs, and the one we picked was “Bananas.” When I heard the hook, the shock was like getting CPR. It felt kind of like an electric shock, too.

Before it became the title track, there were five versions of “Bananas,” with names like “Bananas A” and “Bananas D-1.” There was even a version with a band feel. While refining the song, we also tried putting the parts in all kinds of different places.

Ryul: Everyone liked “Bananas.” I was really surprised when I first heard it. The version on the album was reshaped into LNGSHOT style as we wrote some of the parts ourselves.

Ohyul: Right. The demo of “Bananas” was shocking. Before our debut, we recorded it in our practice room, sent it to Jay Park, and played it for him too. So it’s amazing to see that song make it all the way to the title track.

We heard “Bananas” honestly captures the complicated emotions you felt as everything around you changed so quickly after your debut. What were those emotions?

Woojin: For me, it felt like reaching a big turning point. I’d been a trainee since elementary school, and I debuted at 19, right before becoming an adult. Preparing for stages and working on music while going to school felt surreal. Going to school and then catching a flight at the airport, actually being with an artist like Jay Park, all of it. Music was a big part of why I became a fan of Jay Park in the first place. Meeting rappers and beatmakers was amazing too.

There was pressure, too. I was attached to SHOT CALLERS and the response was good, so the pressure was huge. When we came back with the next EP, we had to show something even better. I think that feeling comes through in “Bananas.” I see “Bananas” as a song with two sides. Both the lyrics and the beat have that duality, and I really relate to it.

Ohyul: I think this album holds more emotions than SHOT CALLERS. A lot of fun moments and a lot of shocking moments went into it. When the beat in “Bananas” switches, I get that dual feeling Woojin talked about, and I like that. Listening to it feels like being transported to another place.

Woojin: It’s almost like an out-of-body experience.

Did you play the new songs for any peers or friends? How did they react?

Ohyul: Early in recording, I played it for Woonhak from BOYNEXTDOOR, and he really loved it. We play each other a lot of our music, not just EPs but even practice tracks.

While working on this album or on tour, what feedback from Jay Park stuck with you?

Woojin: What he kept saying was “This is really sophisticated.” He said he was moved that, only nine months after debut, our music already sounded mature and even polished.

Ryul: He said we feel more grown up. We also got closer to him during this tour. We were already close, but it feels like we’ve gotten even closer.

S.W.I.S.H. stands for “Swag With Integrity Shapes History,” and we heard it reflects LNGSHOT’s direction of starting a new wave with a swag that is genuinely your own. What does “swag with integrity” mean to you?

Ryul: To me, “Kim Ryul’s swag” is “swag I try to hold on to.” Staying the same, always. I try to be the same person whether I’m working or not. I think that’s more human and cooler. Jay Park is always the same too, with no line between work and his personal life, and I’ve learned a lot from seeing that side of him.

What does S.W.I.S.H. mean to each of you?

Ohyul: It’s like the starting line of a marathon. This is LNGSHOT’s first comeback, so it feels like we’re setting off again, in a different way from our debut. I want to look at LNGSHOT’s journey over the long run. You don’t finish a marathon in one go. You run it while managing your pace. Like a marathon, S.W.I.S.H. feels like an album where we’re just starting to run.

Ryul: I think it’s an album that marks a turning point. We’re releasing it at an important moment, not just for me but for LNGSHOT.

Woojin: To me, S.W.I.S.H. feels like the end of my teens. It’s like a year-end party of an album. Maybe because I’m still in school, it still hasn’t really sunk in that I’ve debuted. But I think S.W.I.S.H. might be the album that lets me say goodbye to my trainee days. It’s sad, but I’m grateful too.

It’s year-end awards season. It must be hard not to think about Rookie of the Year.

Louis: We’ve given it our all and worked hard the whole way, so whether we win or not, I don’t think we’ll have any regrets. If we don’t win, that’ll become motivation in its own way.

Ryul: If we win, I plan to send a thank-you message to our SHOTTIES and give a better reaction than anyone. (laughs)

Ohyul: Of course it would be great to win. But I realized that if you keep thinking about just one thing, beyond the award itself, you end up chasing only that and might feel empty later. So I decided to focus on what I can do right now.

What kind of year was this for each of you, and what do you hope next year will be like? What do you want to achieve next year? Even small things are fine.

Woojin: Something I realized on tour is that next year I want to put on a show just for SHOTTIES (our fandom name). Festivals and events are great too, but I want to do a show that’s LNGSHOT’s own. For the rest, I’ll go with the flow. (laughs)

Ryul: I have no plans. This year we did a lot of overseas schedules, so next year I want to see our fans in Korea up close more often.

Louis: I don’t have a specific goal. It’ll keep being tough, but I want to keep having fun like we have so far.

Ohyul: For me too, it’s starting our solo concerts. Just being able to kick off with a solo show in Seoul would mean so much.

What’s Going on With Katseye?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Katseye started the year off strong, but the past few months have been marred with hiccups. What’s going on?

In February, the six members of the group delivered a spirited performance of “Gnarly” during the Best New Artist medley at the 2026 Grammy Awards. In April, they made their Coachella debut on the heels of their single “Pinky Up.” By then, they were down to five members, with Manon Bannerman on an indefinite hiatus to focus on her “health and wellbeing,” a break that is currently in its ninth month.

“I’m really grateful for the patience and kindness everyone has shown during this time,” Bannerman said in April, adding that she’d had “positive conversations” with the group’s labels. “I feel supported. I’m happy and I’m healthy.” Katseye powered full-speed ahead in the meantime.

Over the summer, the group announced their Wildworld Tour, a global trek scheduled to put them on arena stages in the fall. Leading up to the launch, Katseye released their third EP, Wild, in August and made a major push to expand their audience with an appearance as musical guest on Saturday Night Live in September.

They were now down to four members, with Sophia Laforteza on a hiatus expected to conclude next January. “Making this decision wasn’t easy, but I’m learning that health has to come first,” she said over the summer. “I’m realizing that if I don’t take care of my mind and body now, I won’t be able to keep doing what I love the most for so long.”

And so Katseye began the North American leg of the tour as a quartet. But last week, their Detroit concert was postponed shortly before they were scheduled to hit the stage. “Megan [Skiendiel] is unwell and unable to perform tonight, and we want to make sure she has the time she needs to rest and recover,” the group shared on Instagram. Two more postponements followed with the confirmation that Skiendiel’s previously undisclosed illness was actually a concussion. This came on top of her sprained ankle, which resulted in the cancellation of their appearance at Olivia Rodrigo’s Daisy Chain Fields Festival in August. You Loong's Deluge Location Guide For Phantom Blade Zero

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

It's useful for when things get a little too hot in the Wulin.

Most traversal Phantom Edges in Phantom Blade Zero appear as you play through the main story, but You Loong's Deluge is different. It's not far off the beaten path, and you need it to complete one of the side quests, but it's still easy to miss if you don't explore every corner.

It's one of the more useful Phantom Edges in the game because it lets you reach areas you can't access without it. You can also use it effectively in combat, making it a great asset in Soul's arsenal. Here's how to get your hands on You Loong's Deluge.

You Loong's Deluge can be found in Pangzhen, northwest of the Green Serpent House and southwest of the Lion Dance Hall, with the latter being the closest bell. Unfortunately, it can be found in the possession of You Loong, Leader of the Blackwater Crew.

If you're coming from the Green Serpent House, you can easily walk right past You Loong, because he actually isn't visible from the main path. You have to hang a bit to the left of the main path and explore a bit before you find the wide-open space that's always indicative of a fight.

You can identify this wide-open space by a large body of water filled with candle-lit lanterns floating on the surface. As you approach, You Loong will burst from the water and initiate the fight that earns you You Loong's Deluge.

No build is complete without great accessories. Here are the 10 best in Phantom Blade Zero.

Surprisingly, You Loong isn't an exceptionally challenging fight, though that depends on your build at the time. He's not overly massive, not exceptionally fast and doesn't hit hard enough to drain your health in one strike. Most of his attacks can be parried or blocked easily, and he doesn't have many red-glint attacks you have to dodge.

One main thing to keep in mind is to take advantage of him when he dives below the water. While he's under, you can not damage each other, so it's a great opportunity to heal with medicines that replenish HP over time. After a while, he'll burst from the water and use small ranged attacks, but you can easily parry them.

If you want to attack him before he comes out of the water on his own, you can walk directly to the section of bubbles that indicates where he is, and he'll burst out regardless of where he is, using the same ranged attack as he would otherwise. It's a good way to corner him or get the jump on your next flurry of attacks.

Keep following this pattern until you've whittled down his health, staying patient and using the periods when he stays underwater. After enough cycles, you'll claim You Loong's Deluge.

You Loong's Deluge is one of the Phantom Edges in the game that's really the most beneficial depending on what build you're using. It creates a constant stream of water you can use to push enemies away while also applying the wet condition.

This means that after you use it on an enemy, they become more susceptible to both lightning and ice damage, so if you're using weapons that apply those conditions, pairing them with You Loong's Deluge will be exceptionally useful.

It's also useful against enemies that use fire weapons. After using the Deluge on one of these enemies, you will do higher damage to them, regardless of what weapons you're using.

You can also use it as a traversal item because it lets you extinguish the large, burning branches that block some pathways throughout the world, regardless of whether you have it equipped. This opens up more doors to collect some of Quick Hand Zhang's treasures and extra resources.

If you've got decision paralysis about which Phantom Edges to use, look no further.
