<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/cms-pauses-hospice-home-health-medicare-enrollments-fraud-crackdown" hreflang="en">CMS extends moratorium on new Medicare enrollments for hospice, home health agencies</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
CMS extends moratorium on new Medicare enrollments for hospice, home health agencies" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Trump administration is extending its moratorium on new Medicare enrollments from hospice and home health agencies.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services said Thursday that it will extend the moratorium by six more months as part of its efforts to combat fraud, waste and abuse in the Medicare program.

CMS said that the initial moratorium window has allowed the agency "to conduct targeted investigations and deploy advanced data analytics" to identify potential cases of fraud. An extension was deemed necessary to continue that effort, as the "circumstances warranting the imposition of the moratoria have not yet abated."

The agency said that hospice and home health have long been considered high risk areas for fraud.

“We’ve made real progress over the past six months, but fraud in hospice and home health persists and our work isn’t finished,” said CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., in the announcement. “Medicare beneficiaries deserve protection from these bad actors, and American taxpayers need to know the programs they rely on aren’t being bled dry by fraudsters.”

Like the original moratorium, the extension applies nationwide. However, the extension will allow currently enrolled hospice providers or home health agencies to add new practice locations to their existing enrollment in Medicare. Providers currently enrolled in Medicare will not be impacted and can continue to provide services to beneficiaries.

The Trump administration has issued a six-month moratorium on hospice and home health agencies enrolling in Medicare as part of its efforts to combat fraud.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services said in an announcement on Wednesday morning that the "data-driven" decision targets a key source of fraud activity. It follows a similar announcement made earlier this year of a moratorium on durable medical equipment, prosthetics, orthotics and supplies companies.

During the six-month window, CMS said it will focus on identifying and investigating bad actors and deploying advanced analytics that help accelerate the removal of hospice and home health providers that are likely perpetrators of fraud.

CMS added that recent action under the broader White House fraud push has suspended payments to 773 hospice providers and 23 home health agencies in the Los Angeles area, totaling about $70 million in funds.

“We’ve seen systemic and deeply troubling fraud in the hospice and home health space, with bad actors exploiting some of our most vulnerable Medicare patients and stealing money from the American taxpayer,” CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., said in the agency's press release. “Today we’re shutting the door on fraud—preventing new bad actors from entering Medicare while we aggressively identify, investigate and remove those already exploiting them."

"This is about protecting patients, restoring integrity and safeguarding taxpayer dollars," Oz said.

CMS said that the moratorium will not affect providers currently enrolled in Medicare, and they can continue to offer key services to members. It will apply, however, to new applicants for participation and "certain changes in majority ownership," which CMS said can be used to "obscure control by bad actors."

The moratoria will also prevent suspected fraudsters from evading punishment by moving across state lines, CMS said.

The announcement, the agency said, builds on a number of steps CMS has taken of late to address fraud in hospice and home healthcare, including increased oversight in states with elevated risk of fraud, such as Arizona, California, Georgia, Ohio, Nevada and Texas.

CMS is also conducting hospice site visits across the country to monitor operations and potentially identify suspicious behavior. In addition, it expanded a demonstration across several states—Florida, Illinois, Oklahoma, Ohio, North Carolina and Texas—that allowed for pre- and post-claim reviews of submissions from HHAs, which may stop improper payments before they're made.

The National Alliance for Care at Home, in a statement responding to the news, said it welcomes the administration's scrutiny and appreciates that "providers will still be able to conduct face-to-face recertification visits via telehealth during the enrollment moratorium, which will help avoid unnecessary care disruptions for patients and families." Still, it said the action raises concerns over access to care in high-demand areas and urged CMS to focus its efforts on "high-fraud markets" or actors without catching the rest of the sector in its net.

“CMS must use data-driven, risk-based program integrity measures and focus resources on boots-on-the-ground surveys and enforcement of existing oversight mechanisms that root out the blatantly bad actors without potentially limiting patient access to care or punishing high-quality providers operating in good faith," Alliance CEO Jennifer Sheets said in the statement.

How Kelly Rowland Keeps Her Eczema Flare-Ups Under Control During Winter

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Wintertime can bring everyone down with its colder weather and less sunlight. But for those with eczema, this time of year can wreak havoc on their skin, too.

That’s the case for Grammy-winning singer, actress, and Destiny’s Child alum Kelly Rowland, who has been living with eczema since her 20s. During the winter months, she often dreads the cold.

“[You] don’t know what the environment is going to give you—because mine is also environmental—or what the weather is going to give you,” Rowland told Healthline.

Cold weather and indoor heating can reduce humidity, drying out the skin and triggering or worsening eczema flare-ups.

“[What we know] about the skin barrier is that it really is like a gate or wall against the outside world,” Camille Howard-Verovic, MD, a dermatologist at Active Dermatology, told Healthline.

In healthy skin, the barrier controls what enters and leaves the skin. However, for people with eczema, Howard-Verovic said they have skin barrier dysfunction, which allows substances such as allergens and irritants to penetrate the skin.

Cold weather can increase transepidermal water loss, a process in which moisture escapes through the skin’s outer barrier and evaporates into the surrounding air.

“People with eczema have a tremendous amount of transepidermal water loss, so you’re just drier,” said Howard-Verovic. “[You’re] not holding onto the water as well, so that causes the itching and the rash that we feel when we have eczema.”

Rowland first noticed eczema flares behind her ears as well as on her elbows, arms, and the back of her knees.

“[It] looked like a rash, so that’s what I thought it was all this time, and I dealt with it for so long until after I had my son and then it started to get worse on my face,” she said.

She eventually went to a dermatologist who diagnosed her with moderate-to-severe eczema, a chronic inflammatory skin condition that can cause intense itching, painful skin cracking, thickened skin, and redness or swelling.

When Rowland learned that eczema can be genetic, it made sense to her.

“I remember seeing big rashes on [my mom’s] neck and her arms…so she had it, and I guess I was the lucky one,” said Rowland.

At first, she tried eliminating foods that she thought might be triggers. She also bathed in oatmeal.

“[I] literally have had a tub full of oatmeal, like literally went to the store to get store-bought fresh oats,” she said. “I tried everything, and it doesn’t help until you go talk to a doctor about it.”

At first, she tried prescription topical medications but found them cumbersome.

“I don’t have the time, if I’m being honest, and we live in a world that is moving so fast…I started buying like three tubes of whatever it was I was using and losing it; it was just too much for me,” Rowland said.

Rowland said she found relief with Ebglyss, a prescription injectable medication for moderate-to-severe atopic dermatitis. She has also partnered with Eli Lilly and Company, its manufacturer, to raise awareness about eczema.

Rowland said she’s sharing her experience to help others living with eczema, including people she’s met who have experienced greater challenges managing the condition.

“One lady was telling me about how she’s having a tough time [looking] in the mirror cause she doesn’t know who’s looking back at her, and she saw her skin before eczema and after,” said Rowland.

When she first had symptoms, she said she felt embarrassed, too.

“[Because] it was just in the middle of my arm space and…[if I] put out my hand to shake someone’s hand, you don’t know if somebody’s judgy,” she said.

Rowland hopes being open about her condition will encourage others to advocate for themselves and talk with their doctor about treatment options.

Because eczema can look different on deeper skin tones, Howard-Verovic noted that a dermatologist can help make the correct diagnosis.

“Some people may not even know that they have eczema,” she said. “We know that eczema is a rash; it can appear red on certain skin tones, and it can be grey or white on certain skin tones, so it can look a little different.”

In addition to medication, Rowland said managing stress helps reduce her eczema flare-ups.

“Before [when] I went on tour, because I was under so much stress, it flared up like crazy, like you wouldn’t believe,” she said. “We were in the back trying to figure out how to cover certain things up, and we figured some of it out, and some of it was really tricky, but what helped was when I actually just calmed down…not that it helped overnight, but it did just kind of bring the flare down.”

She said red light therapy, feeling grounded outside without her shoes on, getting vitamin D from natural sunlight, and cooking are ways she reduces ongoing stress.

“Cooking calms me when my kids aren’t here, and I have a really good [Zen] playlist on,” said Rowland. “[If] you ask me a question [in those moments], I might give you my heart; I’m just so happy.”

She also tries to cut back on sugar, which she believes triggers flares.

Another way to reduce flares, according to Howard-Verovic, is to avoid hot showers, especially in the winter.

“[We] know eczematous skin likes lukewarm water,” she said.

Because Howard-Verovic and her daughter both live with eczema, she set a rule about when it’s time to get out of the shower.

“[If] you see condensation on the bathroom mirror, you’ve spent too much time in the shower,” said Howard-Verovic. “[That] really hot, hot water feels really good in that moment…but when you get out all that water evaporates, and there’s no moisture in the atmosphere, and it dries your skin out, and now your skin is unhappy again.”

If you prefer to take baths, she said use gentle cleansers.

“[There] are bleach baths for really, really bad flare-ups,” she noted.

Diluted bleach baths are sometimes recommended for people with eczema, but they should only be used according to a healthcare professional’s instructions.

As soon as you get out of the shower or bath, Howard-Verovic said to apply a quality moisturizer to your damp skin. According to the National Eczema Association (NEA), lotions containing the following ingredients can help ease dry skin associated with eczema.

To help identify appropriate products, look for ones that have the NEA’s Seal of Acceptance.

High Fat Diet Linked to Higher Colon Cancer Risk, Early Research Shows

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

In a new study, researchers report that a high fat diet may potentially increase the risk of colon cancer.

The researchers from Arizona State University said a diet high in certain fats can cause lasting changes in intestinal stem cells that lead to the development of tumors and the possibility of colon cancer.

The study was conducted in mice, but the researchers noted changes in the DNA of intestinal stem cells that may explain their growth and survival.

The researchers published their findings on November 12 in the journal Stem Cell Reports.

The researchers said the study is important because 1 in 25 people develop colon cancer in their lifetime. In the United States, that translates into about 108,000 new cases of colon cancer every year. About 55,000 males and 55,000 females are expected to die from the disease in 2026.

Experts not involved in the study say the research provides valuable information, but they have reservations about the results, as the findings do not yet apply to humans.

“The findings provide a potential mechanism linking diet to intestinal cancer development, but we need to be cautious about translating these results to humans,” said Dalia Perelman, MS, CDE, a research dietitian at Stanford University in California.

“We already have evidence from human studies that obesity and certain dietary patterns, particularly those high in processed meats and low in fiber-rich foods, are associated with increased colorectal cancer risk,” Perelman told Healthline. “This study adds a potentially important mechanism to investigate, but it does not establish that dietary fat itself increases colorectal cancer risk in humans.”

Ketan Thanki, MD, a colorectal surgeon specializing in benign and malignant diseases of the colon, rectum, and anus at the MemorialCare Todd Cancer Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in California, shared similar views.

“This study provides a better understanding at the cellular level of how a high fat diet may contribute to colorectal cancer development,” Thanki told Healthline.

“Animal studies are an important starting point for understanding biological processes, but we must be cautious about directly applying their findings to humans,” he added. “Mice and humans share many cellular and biological mechanisms, which makes these studies valuable in identifying potential pathways involved in disease development. However, human diets, genetics, metabolism, and environmental exposures are more complex.”

To reach their findings, the Arizona State researchers fed mice what the scientists described as a Western-style high fat diet for 6 months.

They then compared those results with those from mice given a low fat diet.

The researchers said the mice on the high fat diet gained more weight than the mice on the low-fat diet. They also developed more intestinal stem cells, and those cells grew at a more rapid rate.

They added that mice genetically predisposed to intestinal cancer who were fed a high-fat diet developed more and larger tumors.

The researchers noted changes in the DNA of intestinal stem cells that may help explain their increased growth and survival.

They also pointed out that stem cell behavior returned to normal after high fat diet mice were switched to a low fat diet, although some DNA changes persisted. After four weeks, approximately 18% of these changes remained.

When mice were returned to a high fat diet, the researchers said their intestinal stem cells responded more quickly and strongly than those of mice exposed to the diet for the first time.

They concluded that the study suggests that maintaining a healthy, low fat diet may help regulate intestinal stem cell growth and reduce colon cancer risk.

Kristin Kirkpatrick, RD, president of KAK Consulting and a dietitian at the Cleveland Clinic Department of Wellness & Preventive Medicine in Ohio, wasn’t involved in the study but highlighted a particular aspect of the findings.

“What I found particularly interesting was that even after the mice returned to a low fat diet, some of the molecular changes persisted,” she told Healthline. “This raises questions about how our dietary patterns over time may influence long-term health.”

Experts said these new findings are interesting but noted numerous caveats.

“One important takeaway is that dietary habits over time matter,” said Thanki. “Even if someone has consumed a high fat diet for years, making changes now may still provide meaningful health benefits.”

“I also think these findings are particularly important for children and adolescents, as establishing healthier dietary habits early in life may provide long-term benefits,” he added.

However, Thanki noted that not all fats are the same. “The health risks depend partly on the type of fat consumed, how much is consumed, and what foods make up the overall diet,” he said.

“Diets consistently high in saturated fats, particularly those containing large amounts of processed meats, fried foods, and highly processed products, can contribute to obesity, elevated cholesterol, cardiovascular disease, and metabolic dysfunction. These factors can also contribute to chronic inflammation and may increase the risk of certain cancers, including colorectal cancer,” Thanki continued.

Perelman said the diet used in the study was extremely high in fat, providing 60% of calories from fat, primarily from lard.

“We know that the health effects of dietary fats differ depending on their sources and fatty acid composition, and we cannot assume that a diet rich in unsaturated fats from foods such as olive oil, nuts, and seeds would produce the same effects,” she noted.

Perelman added that the researchers primarily studied stem cells from the small intestine rather than the colon.

“Although these tissues share some biological characteristics, they have important differences, and we cannot assume that the same changes would occur in the colon,” she explained.

Finally, Perelman noted that “the mice consuming the high fat diet also gained weight, making it difficult to separate the effects of dietary fat itself from those associated with increased body fat and other metabolic changes.”

Not all high fat foods are unhealthy. Some high fat foods that offer healthy benefits include:

“The goal shouldn’t necessarily be to eliminate fat because our bodies need healthy fats for normal functioning,” said Thanki. “Instead, people should focus on reducing saturated and trans fats while incorporating healthier unsaturated fats in appropriate amounts.”

Thanki had several dietary suggestions. Among them:

“Consistency matters more than perfection. Even modest dietary changes, maintained over time, can have meaningful health benefits,” Thanki said.

Perelman agreed. “The overall quality of the diet matters. Diets that are very high in fat may displace other foods and nutrients that have important health benefits,” she said.

“For colorectal cancer specifically, higher intakes of dietary fiber and whole grains have consistently been associated with lower risk,” Perelman noted.

“Therefore, rather than focusing exclusively on reducing fat, it is important to consider the overall dietary pattern and whether it provides adequate amounts of fiber-rich plant foods and other beneficial nutrients.”

Kirkpatrick agreed that the type of fat is what is important. “A Mediterranean-style diet that includes olive oil, nuts, seeds, and fatty fish can be relatively high in fat but is consistently associated with positive health outcomes,” she said.

“On the other hand, a diet high in saturated fat, processed meats, and ultra-processed foods may increase the risk of certain chronic diseases, particularly when it’s also low in fiber and other protective nutrients.”

Kirkpatrick also noted that many lifestyle habits contribute to a person’s overall health.

“Our health is influenced by so many factors beyond diet, including how we manage stress, our sleep habits, physical activity, genetics, and overall lifestyle,” she said.

Trump admin loses fight to spy on random journalists’ LinkedIn DMs

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Confidential sources “lifeblood” of free press, court rules in LinkedIn fight.

The Trump administration’s fight to stop suspected leaks of top-secret military information on LinkedIn can’t sweep in “a vast number of confidential sources” of random journalists “who have no conceivable relationship” to the Department of Justice’s investigation, an appeals court ruled Tuesday.

The order came after the DOJ asked LinkedIn to turn over 18 months of communications from targeted journalists who collectively published more than 1,000 articles on many different topics over that time period. The request was not limited to journalists involved in a controversial report from a news outlet that Trump said exposed confidential military information in a foreign country. (The outlet is not being named to protect national security secrets.) Instead, the DOJ cast a wide net, requesting extensive LinkedIn data on journalists who had any possible link to the journalists around the time the report was drafted.

The majority of journalists have LinkedIn accounts and publish their contact information to encourage sources to connect on the platform, LinkedIn argued. So allowing that government overreach went beyond harming targeted journalists and threatened to undermine a critical platform where journalists frequently connect with confidential sources, judges ruled. Even more concerning, the Trump administration secured a secrecy order barring LinkedIn from telling anyone about the sweeping request for a year. That left journalists unaware for far too long that their First Amendment rights had been compromised, judges ruled.

Penning the unanimous opinion from a three-judge panel, Ronald Reagan-nominated judge J. Harvie Wilkinson III rejected LinkedIn’s request to quash the order entirely and ordered the US district court to substantially narrow the order to avoid posing a grave threat to press freedoms.

Without such an intervention, the US risked silencing dissidents who raise alarms about “events of large importance and governmental behavior of dubious legality and integrity,” Wilkinson wrote. “A request by the government to scoop up reporters’ records wholesale and ascertain the identity of their sources would tip the balance against informed discourse in a manner inimical to democratic health.”

“Many of the users identified have no conceivable relationship to the leak investigation,” Wilkinson wrote. “Instead, they are affiliated with media outlets, universities, and law firms which may have been relevant to the host of other stories the targeted journalists were investigating during the same considerable time period.”

As Wilkinson explained, the district court failed to consider LinkedIn’s claims that the request imposed undue burdens and threatened the First Amendment rights of users who would have no way of knowing that their communications were being reviewed by the government. The lower court reasoned that the request imposed no burden on LinkedIn because the company could feasibly respond to it, then ruled that, regardless of how these matters were weighed, the balance would tip toward the government’s interest in obtaining the LinkedIn data.

This was an error that must be corrected, the appeals court ruled.

“It is not appropriate for district courts to approve requests purely because the records requested are logistically feasible to compile,” Wilkinson wrote. He further emphasized that “serious security and speech claims are in tension in this case. There is space under law for both.”

The appeals court has ordered the district court to go back and assess how the disclosure order can be narrowed. That includes entertaining a very limited alternative to the government’s secrecy order that LinkedIn had proposed—which would have notified a single attorney of the news outlet under investigation about the government’s request.

The Trump administration fought that limited request, arguing that the news outlet’s attorney may not be trustworthy or could possibly tip others off to the LinkedIn probe, but Wilkinson rejected those arguments. Noting that the justice system often deals with “imperfectly informed litigants,” Wilkinson said that “the government should be given an opportunity to explain why LinkedIn’s narrow disclosure would somehow compromise its investigation.”

“Unless the government’s response to LinkedIn’s proposal is more persuasive than anything the government has presented thus far, LinkedIn’s narrow exception to the breadth of the Secrecy Order must be adopted,” Wilkinson wrote.

LinkedIn, which is owned by Microsoft, does not seem to expect the government to be able to defend the sweeping request on remand. While a narrowed order may not protect all irrelevant users from being caught in the government’s hunt for the military leaker, it would stop Trump from accessing a broad range of journalists’ interactions with confidential sources. In a statement, a Microsoft spokesperson told Ars that “this is an important ruling for the right to privacy and press freedom.”

“Microsoft has consistently defended journalists’ ability to protect confidential sources and the principle that government demands for information should face meaningful judicial scrutiny and be balanced against First Amendment protections,” Microsoft’s spokesperson said.

Several civil rights groups backed LinkedIn in the fight, including the Reporters Committee for Freedom of the Press (RCFP). In a statement to Ars, RCFP President Bruce D. Brown celebrated the ruling as a win for all journalists, not just those who rely on LinkedIn to connect with confidential sources.

“This ruling is a big deal and will quickly become one of the leading opinions in the area—one we will be quoting from it for a long time,” Brown said. “The court recognized that confidential sources are the ‘lifeblood’ of investigative reporting and that dragnet demands from technology companies to reveal those sources violate the law.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

This Modern Take On A Classic Dungeon Crawler Is Making Me Feel Just So Happy

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Indie of the WeekRake In GrassReviewUnderkeep

My delight withÂ Underkeep was immediate, when I was still very early in the game’s first dungeons, I killed a worm creature and it dropped what looked like a wheel of moldy cheese. This is one of my overriding memories of playing 1987’s Dungeon Master, a game to whichÂ Underkeep owes a great debt, even if its outdoor zones perhaps are more reminiscent of 1993’s sequel, Skullkeep. Or, if you’re not a million years old, thinkÂ Legend of Grimrock. Wait, that’s 13 years old? [crumbles into dust]

Underkeep is a very straight take on the old-school “blobber,” an RPG in which you move your party of multiple characters as a single entity or “blob,” and it is deeply welcome. Since Grimrock so successfully revised the adored format (best exemplified by games like Wizardry,Â Eye of the Beholder, and the aforementioned Dungeon Master, among many others), very few have followed in its tile-based steps.Â Underkeep very much does, and while I have a few reservations, it’s kept me enormously happy for the last week.

At the start you pick your four characters, created by mixing races and classes, then adding a profile picture, and are then immediately plonked into the woods. You need to reach the Village of Underkeep, but there are monsters, dungeons, and puzzles in your way. So off you go to do some classic adventuring, with a party of four taking it in turns to unleash their attacks on enemies you encounter. As such, you’ll want to pick your four characters sensibly, with tanky fighters up front and rangers and spellcasters in the rear.

Unlike some blobbers, Underkeep doesn’t let you move around mid-turn, so this isn’t about darting around in squares to dodge enemy attacks, but rather about planning the order of your four attacks, picking between basic whacks and arrow shots or more specialist moves that can be unlocked and upgraded when you level up. It’s only after all four of your characters have made their attack or other action for the turn that you can change tiles, and at this point the enemy can attack you back.

Once you reach Underkeep, things really come to life. Here there are a handful of stores, plus a few characters to chat with and take quests from. Then you can head off in a whole bunch of different directions to try to complete these tasks. Here Underkeep delivers its best and worst elements, with the free-roaming quest structure reminding me a little ofÂ Betrayal at Krondor orÂ Might & Magic as it grants you a lot of enjoyable freedom in filling out your map, taking on new enemies, and stumbling upon mini-dungeons, fun little side-quests, and odd little puzzles with neat equipment as rewards. However, within all this freedom you’re frustratingly restricted by unpredictable difficulty. Heading north to the Caerlin Woods might seem like a good idea, until you encounter the griffins and realize you’re not even vaguely ready to be here. So you go south to the Cemetery and all seems well, right up until you hit another wall.

This process of elimination sometimes required to find the next direction you can actually handle going in gets irritating, and it’d be a lot better if the game were able to communicate whether you’re ready for an area in some subtle way. Then again, it is enormously satisfying to go elsewhere, gain a couple of levels and some better weapons, and then carve your way through a place that previously wiped you out far too easily.

Talking of being wiped out,Â Underkeep is very generous with its reincarnation of fallen heroes. UnlikeÂ Dungeon Master which required you to find all-too-rare locations and cast certain potent spells, here you need only hand over a sum of gold to a statue that appears in each region and your friend will be back, albeit on one plip of health. Healing is also quite readily available, either by potions you find or craft, or more commonly through rest. But here again things are a bit odd. Resting requires food, and lots of it. Bedding down to recover health, stamina and mana can see you going through literally tens of meals, and given they’re quite expensive to stock up on, and unpredictable in the volume consumed, this also always feels unwieldy. That and the fact that you can’t interrupt rest midway. I would love to be able to see how much of my resources will be consumed before resting, and then choose to wake up so many hours/meals in.

Any more complaints? Well, I would like a more interesting spell system, perhaps casting by runes, but you can also pick from a fairly interesting spread when leveling up, and jump between them on the fly. You’ll need to focus quite intensely on one or two skills given how rarely level-ups come, and there’s no option to respec, but I found this need to commit or spread my bets interesting to engage with.

But back to celebrating. I love that there’s a decent amount of story here, and that the characters you meet are invested in the tale. I enjoyed finding a missing band of dwarfs, and getting involved in their disagreement over how to handle a furious dragon. It’s not exactly complicated or deep, but it’s a lot more than I’m used to in this genre, and works nicely and unobtrusively. I also really enjoyed the way the classes and races I picked make a difference in moment-to-moment encounters, my Elk ranger useful for picking up on danger in the woods, and my Raven wizard excellent at detecting errant magics. Also, at a certain point, because of how I handled a particular side-quest, all of my characters now occasionally turn into mushrooms. It doesn’t seem to be hindering things.

What a pleasure that such a game can still be made in 2026. This doesn’t have the ludicrous difficulty of aÂ Wizardry, nor the in-depth lore of aÂ Might & Magic, but rather feels like its own very deliberate thing. It’s notÂ easy (although die-hard dungeon crawlers might say it is) but it’s perhaps more simple. I like simple.Â Ocarina of Time is in its own way simple, and it’s been a splendid week going back and forth between these two games, as hugely dissimilar as they are.

Oh, and it’s very pretty, the enemy designs are terrific, and the soundtrack is absolutely fantastic. So yay!

Opinion: What MAHA’s support for Russell Brand tells us about the movement

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Yamey is a professor of global health and public policy at Duke University in Durham.

The British comedian Russell Brand is a major figure in health secretary Robert F. Kennedy’s Make America Healthy Again movement. He regularly joined conference calls organized by the super PAC MAHA Action, on one occasion dialing in naked from a bath tub. He was a keynote speaker alongside Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services Administrator Mehmet Oz at the September 2026 MAHA Summit. He is also a convicted rapist who was just sentenced to 10 and a half years in jail.

During Brand’s trial, Tony Lyons, MAHA Action’s founder, was at the courthouse to support him. Lyons had argued that Brand was falsely accused, saying “he’s been vilified, persecuted, deplatformed, demonetized, censored, prosecuted.” On Tuesday, after the conviction came down, MAHA Action Inc.’s newsletter published a 3,700-word defense of Brand.

I’ve followed MAHA closely and I’m not at all surprised that the movement’s leadership is standing by Brand. There is a rot of toxic masculinity and violence at MAHA’s core.

Even before the prison sentence, MAHA happily palled around with Brand, despite the British police charging him in April 2025 and again in December 2025 with multiple counts of rape and sexual assault. The charges did not come out of nowhere: In 2023, three British outlets jointly published a special investigation into allegations against Brand.

National Institutes of Health Director Jay Bhattacharya, who has said he wants the NIH to become “the research arm of MAHA,” shared billing with Brand at a November 2025 MAHA Summit. Earlier that year, Kennedy posted a photo of himself clutching a lizard, as Brand and a shirtless Oz put their arms around him, all smiles.

Perhaps most shocking of all, Oz chuckled at a joke about sexual consent that Brand made at the October 2026 MAHA Summit.

Oz told the audience that Brand was almost late because he was backstage “hugging the Secretary of Agriculture,” Brooke Rollins, to which Brand replied, “She initiated that contact, I should make clear, doctor!” I agree with Holly Baxter, senior reporter at The Independent, who said that of course the jocular exchange between Brand and Oz looks bad now, “but it should’ve looked bad then, when the court case was on the horizon, so let’s not expect much hand-wringing from Oz, MAHA-maven RFK Jr., and the other Trump health officials who embraced Brand over the past two years.”

The alliance between MAHA and Brand is not an aberration, it’s a pattern of befriending violent misogynists. MAHA made Mike Tyson, another convicted rapist who said in 1989 that he enjoyed hurting women, the face of its healthy eating campaign. Bhattacharya even posted a picture of himself with a Tyson face tattoo and the message “Be like Mike Tyson.” The New York Times reported that Donald Trump Jr., who championed the MAHA movement at Turning Point USA’s 2025 annual conference, “once called Andrew Tate a friend and publicly defended him against previous criminal allegations.” Tate is a leader of the manosphere who faces extradition to Britain on multiple charges including rape and sex trafficking.

You might think the shellacking that MAHA-allied candidates suffered in the midterm elections, despite major financial support from MAHA Action, would have led the MAHA bosses to rethink their allegiances. You’d be wrong.

On Nov. 6, for example, Kennedy celebrated Stephen Miller’s appointment as President Trump’s chief of staff, saying, “Next to me, Stephen Miller is the strongest MAHA supporter in the administration. It’s time to unleash the Kraken.” Miller is the man behind the inhumane family separation policy in Trump’s first term. He is also the architect of ICE’s brutal tactics during Trump 2.0. Kennedy has defended ICE tactics on Joe Rogan’s show and provided support for the horrific treatment of undocumented immigrants. Across the country, unaccompanied immigrant children as young as 3, who came to the U.S. to be reunited with their families, have faced sometimes tough interrogation by judges, nearly always having to represent themselves in court without a lawyer. Kennedy’s Department of Health and Human Services is legally required to care for them through its Office of Refugee Resettlement (ORR), and federal law requires these children be provided with legal counsel, fair hearings, and accommodation appropriate to a child’s age. But Kennedy, who has adopted harsh anti-immigrant rhetoric and who falsely argues that immigrants are disease vectors, refused to renew the contract to provide legal services for these children. And almost 90% of undocumented children are now being held in unlicensed ORR shelters in Texas, exempt from oversight and inspections.

Since the MAHA wipe-out in the midterms, there have been an endless slew of articles pondering how MAHA will change course. But MAHA’s rotten core of violence, including its love for Brand (who Lyons called “an incredible human being”), is one big reason why I’m deeply skeptical that “MAHA 2.0” will be any different.  Beth Mole, senior health reporter at Ars Technica, notes that leaders of the grassroots wing of the MAHA movement remain “doggedly loyal” to Kennedy. They’re loyal to a man who has worked with Trump to roll out policies that I believe are inherently violent, who has espoused eugenicist views, and who shows zero signs of remorse.

There was violence in the MAGA/MAHA administration enacting policies that will throw 15 million people off health insurance, that cut off an estimated 2 million kids from food assistance, and that killed federal health research that could have improved the health of minoritized Americans. There is eugenics in the way that Kennedy blames the weak for their sickness, believes only “sick kids should die of measles,”  and once said he wants to remove Black kids from their families and “reparent” them on government-run farms.

So when I think of MAHA’s future, there’s an old proverb that comes to mind: “The fish rots from the head down.” The head is, of course, Kennedy, MAHA’s supreme leader. Unless he radically changes his views and policy directions — which seems extremely unlikely — MAHA is destined to be that rotting fish in perpetuity.

Gavin Yamey is a professor of global health and public policy at Duke University in Durham.

MN: Why Milan and Inter are calm despite Council request over new stadium plan

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A potential obstacle has already arisen after the Generali Stadium announcement, even if all parties remain calm for now.

As MilanNews recall, Tuesday morning’s edition of Corriere della Sera carried a report that had everyone on edge, just days after the renderings of the project were published. However, by the afternoon some quite important clarifications had arrived from Mayor Giuseppe Sala.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

The newspaper reported that the Milan City Council had approved a bipartisan measure to halt the current environmental assessment process for the Generali Stadium project and request a new one that encompasses all the interventions the two clubs intend to undertake.

This includes the demolition of the current stadium once the new stadium is operational. The goal is to make the green light for the work conditional on a prior Health Impact Assessment (HIA).

MilanNews have learned that the City Council’s motion is non-binding, meaning Mayor Sala’s administration will not be obligated to follow the council’s request. More importantly, it does not suspend the Strategic Environmental Assessment (SEA).

The two clubs remain calm regarding the next phases, especially since the SEA and the VIS are two separate and distinct phases that must both be addressed before work on the new stadium can begin.

The Strategic Environmental Assessment (SEA) is a procedure established by Italian and European legislation that evaluates the environmental sustainability of a plan or program prior to its approval.

It differs from the subsequent Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA), which instead examines the environmental effects of a specific project prior to its potential authorisation and implementation.

The Council has therefore advanced a request that would not currently be foreseen under normal formal procedures. In any case, the two clubs are confident in the material produced, providing even more than necessary by integrating the Environmental Report with all the health-related details currently available.

Milan and Inter have no intention of avoiding any type of assessment. On the contrary, the EIA is scheduled for early 2027, by spring, with completion expected within a few months. Further investigations into the project and its impacts will therefore be undertaken shortly, as per the timetable.

Yesterday afternoon, Mayor Sala issues a statement on the news that broke, once again emphasising that the project will move forward.

“It’s clear that continuing to call the stadium issue into question is a gift to the right. I don’t want us to delude ourselves into thinking we’ve already won. I respect the City Council’s opinion, but the Council will respect the fact that I have no intention of reconsidering,” he said.

“We’ll discuss it in the City Council, but I have no intention of reconsidering the stadium. We’ve been talking about it for three years; everyone should do what they think best. I will continue to be consistent, and I will try to move forward with the stadium project.”

Councillor Martina Riva also gave a comment to MilanNews too: “Small obstacles? They won’t be a problem, we are convinced of the validity of our operation and we will move forward: the city, the teams and the fans who want a fully accessible stadium deserve it.

“I really like the Generali Stadium, it has exceeded all expectationsaccessibleI really like the Generali Stadium, it has exceeded all expectations.”

STAT+: Doctors on Kennedy’s influential preventive care panel have major conflicts of interest, filings reveal

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Chelsea covers the Department of Health and Human Services with a particular focus on power and influence at the top. Confidential tips can be sent via Signal at chelseacirruzzo.42.

WASHINGTON — Federal officials have granted blanket waivers to every member of an influential, yet little-known federal panel that evaluates preventive medical services, allowing them to serve despite some having clear financial stakes in decisions they might make.

The waivers, signed by a top federal official and obtained by STAT, show a willingness by health secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s department to overlook financial conflicts of interests, though Kennedy frequently decries corporate influence in health care. In the case of two members, the documents show that they own private medical practices that could profit should the panel recommend particular types of care.

Other conflicts being waived include stock holdings in pharmaceutical and medical device companies and employment at public research universities that provide medical care. Some members of the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force, especially the newer ones, have more extensive conflicts than others.

Opinion: Low-income parents can’t afford to take chances with nutritious food their picky eaters might reject

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

By Priya Fielding-Singh and Darcy Cherlin

Fielding-Singh is the director of policy and programs at the Global Food Institute at the George Washington University. Cherlin is the research project manager at the Global Food Institute at the George Washington University.

As high food costs and cuts to food assistance squeeze families’ budgets, concerns are growing about whether low-income parents will be able to keep putting food on the table.

But these financial pressures threaten to do more than increase hunger. They also risk harming children’s diets and health in the long run — by making nutritious foods not only harder to afford, but also increasingly risky for parents to buy.

To understand why, consider the most universal of parenting frustrations: picky eating. Most parents experience it, but the stakes vary widely. For well-off parents, watching a child push away a helping of vegetables is annoying but manageable. A carrot left untouched one day can simply mean trying again the next.

But for parents living close to the bone, that same rejected carrot is more than frustrating. It represents money that could have been spent on something their child would have actually eaten.

Simply put: Children’s food refusals come at a cost — one that few cash-strapped families can afford.

Such financial risks profoundly shape how low-income parents feed their kids, as sociologists have documented for years. A decade ago, Caitlin Daniel first showed how the risk of wasting money on rejected nutritious foods led low-income parents to prioritize buying less healthy items they knew their kids would eat. Years later, one of us (Priya) found that catering to kids’ preferences was also driven in part by parents’ earlier bouts of food insecurity: Determined to spare their children those experiences, caregivers often relied on familiar, comforting foods they knew would fill their kids’ stomachs.

Our newest study adds another dimension to this picture. Low-income parents across three states described going to considerable lengths to prioritize children’s nutrition, often at the expense of their own diets.

At the same time, to conserve resources and keep dinnertime from turning into a battle, they paid close attention to their kids’ likes and prioritized buying what they knew their children would eat. As one mother put it, she’d rather her daughter be “happy and fed than sad and hungry.”

Within such severe financial constraints, catering to children’s preferences is strategic. But it can also undermine children’s diets and health over time. That’s because raising kids to be healthy eaters is a long game: Children’s tastes develop gradually as they encounter and become familiar with different foods, and young children often need to try unfamiliar, healthier ones — like bitter vegetables — multiple times before accepting them.

That’s why pediatricians and nutrition experts routinely encourage parents to keep offering fruits and vegetables, even when kids turn them down. It’s smart advice, but it assumes parents can shoulder the costs of following it.

For families with little money to spare, doing so has always been a gamble. And today, it’s becoming an even bigger one. Groceries now cost nearly a third more in dollar terms than they did in 2020. Meanwhile, participation in SNAP — the nation’s largest food assistance program — has fallen by over 5 million people, including an estimated 2 million children, since summer 2025.

These economic pressures are now showing up in parents’ food decisions. A national survey found that more than half of low-income parents reported cutting back on groceries, while over two-thirds worried about having to choose between paying bills and buying healthy food for their children.

Forcing parents into such trade-offs doesn’t just threaten their ability to keep children fed. It also undermines their efforts to help children eat — and learn to enjoy — nutritious foods.

Fortunately, there are ways to address both problems.

First and foremost, we need to ensure children have enough to eat. That means restoring access to food assistance. The next Congress should reverse the cuts to SNAP enacted through H.R. 1 and stop SNAP benefit costs from being shifted to states — a shift that could jeopardize continued access to a program millions of families depend on to put food on the table. To make a bill reversing the cuts veto-proof, it should be a bipartisan effort.

But we also need to invest in programs that support parents’ efforts to feed their children nutritious foods, including incentives that make those purchases more affordable and less financially risky. Protecting and strengthening WIC’s fruit and vegetable benefit and expanding produce incentives like Double Up Food Bucks for SNAP recipients can help families stretch their grocery dollars further, giving them more room to buy nutritious foods their children might not eat the first time around.

Schools and childcare programs must also be critical partners in this work. Beyond providing nutritious meals, they can give kids regular opportunities to encounter and try healthier foods without leaving parents to shoulder the full financial burden. Expanding initiatives like the Department of Agriculture’s Fresh Fruit and Vegetable Program and Farm to School programs, which help introduce children to new foods through fresh produce, taste tests, gardens, and other activities, can help.

Nutrition experts are right to encourage parents to keep introducing healthy foods, even when their children refuse them. But advice that families can’t afford to follow simply won’t cut it. We need food policies that not only ensure children have enough to eat, but also give parents the resources and support to help them develop healthy eating habits that last.

Priya Fielding-Singh is the director of policy and programs at the Global Food Institute at the George Washington University and the author of “How the Other Half Eats: The Untold Story of Food and Inequality in America.” Darcy Cherlin is the research project manager at the Global Food Institute at the George Washington University.

1 in 3 Infants Diagnosed With Peanut Allergy May Not Have the Condition

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Peanut allergies have been on the rise in recent decades.

According to statistics provided by Food Allergy Research & Education (FARE), an estimated 6.2 million children and adults in the United States are allergic to peanuts.

However, new research published in the November 2026 issue of The Journal of Allergy and Clinical Immunology suggests that over a third of infants thought to have this diagnosis might not actually have a peanut allergy.

In fact, having a history of reacting to peanuts and positive allergy testing on their own may not be sufficient to diagnose a peanut allergy for many infants.

The study’s authors suggest that a threshold oral food challenge — in which the child is given increasing amounts of the suspected food allergen under medical supervision to look for an allergic reaction — should be routinely considered to help confirm peanut allergy.

This additional testing could safely prevent children who are not allergic to peanuts from undergoing unnecessary treatment with oral immunotherapy.

It could also spare families from burdensome food restrictions and allergy precautions.

The researchers’ goal was to evaluate the accuracy and safety of the Allergy Development to an Accelerated Path to Tolerance (ADAPT) peanut oral immunotherapy program, which was established in 2024 at 10 public children’s hospitals across Australia.

This program provides a standardized approach to evaluating and treating infants with suspected peanut allergies.

The study included infants who had experienced a mild to moderate reaction to peanut before 12 months of age and had evidence of peanut sensitization through allergy testing.

Researchers reviewed medical records to examine how accurately the program’s approach identified peanut allergies and whether it could safely guide treatment.

A key part of the evaluation was a threshold oral food challenge. The challenge involved up to seven doses, ranging from 15 to 1,300 milligrams of peanut protein.

Medical teams monitored the infants for reactions and provided treatment when necessary.

Researchers reviewed records from April 2024 through October 2025. During that period, 1,275 infants were assessed through the program, and 778 underwent an oral food challenge.

The researchers also examined allergy test results and whether infants who reacted during the challenges could begin oral immunotherapy at an appropriate starting dose.

At the time of the oral food challenge, the infants had a median age of 13 months.

Of the 778 infants who underwent an oral food challenge, 507 (65.2%) had an allergic reaction.

However, 271 (34.8%) did not react to peanut during the supervised test, despite having a history of a previous reaction and positive allergy test results.

These findings suggest that relying on medical history and allergy tests alone may lead some infants to be labeled with a peanut allergy when they can actually tolerate the food.

Among the infants who reacted during the challenges, 61 experienced anaphylaxis, a severe allergic reaction that can affect breathing and blood pressure. This represented 12% of infants with a positive challenge and 7.8% of all infants who underwent testing. Medical teams safely managed these severe allergic reactions.

Researchers also found that infants who reacted to peanut generally had higher skin prick test results and Ara h 2-specific IgE levels than those who did not react.

However, these test results did not reliably indicate how much peanut would trigger a reaction or how severe that reaction would be.

Of the 394 infants who began peanut oral immunotherapy, 384 (97.5%) successfully started treatment at a dose below the amount that had triggered a reaction during their food challenge.

The researchers say these findings support considering supervised oral food challenges to confirm suspected peanut allergies before starting treatment or recommending long-term avoidance.

Erin Banta, MD, a board certified allergist and immunologist who treats both children and adults at Latitude Food Allergy Care in Great Neck, New York, said these findings are significant because they provide evidence for early use of oral food challenges in infants with peanut reactions.

“In those with mild-moderate prior reactions to peanut, early oral food challenge may allow for either delabeling of food allergy (in 1/3 of the patients in this study) or diagnosis of food allergy,” she told Healthline.

Banta, who wasn’t involved in the study, added that early delabeling takes a significant burden off the patient and their family.

“On the other hand,” she explained, “early diagnosis allows for early initiation of treatment with oral immunotherapy at a time in the patient’s life associated with the greatest success rates.”

Banta further noted that the study offers different pathways for oral immunotherapy based on the patient’s reaction threshold. This means that some patients may be able to complete treatment more quickly due to their individual threshold dose.

Lindsey Forstie, FNP, a family nurse practitioner with clinical training and experience in allergy and asthma care at Carefree Allergy in Phoenix, AZ, added that while further evaluation may be needed to confirm a peanut allergy diagnosis, it’s important for parents not to try introducing peanuts at home.

“Instead, they should speak with their pediatrician or allergist about appropriate testing and whether a supervised food challenge is safe and necessary,” she said.

According to Forstie, who was also not part of the study, the goal is to avoid unnecessary food restrictions while also keeping children safe.

“The key message is that not every positive allergy test means a child has a food allergy,” she told Healthline. “Accurate diagnosis helps protect children while allowing those who are not allergic to enjoy a more normal, less restrictive diet.”

How Can We Stop Sexual Violence?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 10, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

I didn’t just start thinking about how to end sexual violence when I sat down to write this. I’ve been thinking about it for most of my life, certainly since adolescence, when people began confiding in me about the assaults that devastated them. One friend described what happened to them as a slow murder of their soul.

I think about everyone who stayed silent out of shame or fear. I think about those who did speak out and were blamed or not believed by their communities, their families, and their friends, the very people who were supposed to support and advocate for them. Many described this secondary assault as more painful than the first.

So when I read about the alleged gang rape at Cornell, I felt deep sorrow for Jane Doe and shock at the response of the men and women who spoke out against her. We must not forget that sexual assault is never the victim’s fault. Responsibility always belongs to the person who assaults them. It does not matter what she wore, what her relationship was with the person or people who assaulted her, or whether she said yes and then changed her mind. And if she was under the influence of substances, even by her own choice, that does not lessen anyone else’s responsibility. I am speaking as a clinician and as a human, not as a lawyer.

So how do we teach our children, especially our sons, not to sexually assault another person?

That question keeps haunting me. I don’t know the men involved in the Cornell case, or the other countless men who have committed sexual assault. I do know that what was taught, or not taught, to them before the assaults contributed to their behavior — for instance, when they ignored a person telling them “No,” either verbally or by their body language; when belonging to a group mattered more than doing what was right; when they disregarded a person’s boundary; when they did not stop when a person was impaired; or when they did not protect a person in danger, and all of this was seen as acceptable.

I couldn’t help but look back at my own children’s childhoods. Some of my proudest moments as a mother happened at parent-teacher conferences when I learned that my son had noticed a classmate sitting alone and invited him to join his lunch table. Another time, I learned that my daughter had stood up for a child who was being bullied, knowing it might cost her friendships.

We begin shaping our children the moment we hold them as infants and respond to their needs with love and compassion. Babies learn empathy by receiving it when we respond to their cries and name their feelings. They learn that their feelings matter, as do the feelings of others. How we treat our children and others becomes how they treat other people as well.

We teach toddlers about boundaries by teaching them that their bodies belong to them. That means not forcing them to hug relatives, and making sure that when a sibling says “stop,” they stop. And we teach them that behavior has consequences: When you hurt someone, you take responsibility for what you did and do what you can to make the situation better.

Children learn respect, compassion, and right from wrong through what their parents (or primary caregivers) say, how they behave, and what they overhear. If they hear the adults around them say a girl “asked for it,” that is where victim-blaming is learned. Instead, we can model kindness, empathy, respect for boundaries, respect for others, self-respect, respect for rules, and the critical thinking it takes to resist groupthink.

We can’t control every influence. Children learn from everything around them: school, friends, their friends’ parents, television, and social media. That is why parents’ influence is so important. Their words and actions matter. Parenting happens through both messaging and modeling. Children notice more than most parents realize: the subtle cues and the conversations they assume their children weren’t paying attention to. And when a parent does realize that they sent the wrong message through words or behavior, they can tell their children they made a mistake and explain what they want them to learn instead.

Through our own actions, we show children how to respect other people’s boundaries and needs. We teach them to read one another’s facial expressions and body language. We teach them that if someone has been drinking or using other substances and is not able to make decisions for themselves, they cannot take advantage of that person. We teach them that wrongdoing has consequences. And we teach them that if they are part of a group that is doing things they are uncomfortable with, or that they know are wrong, they need to separate from the group and even get help if needed. Being different from a group is hard, but doing what’s right matters more.

Teaching empathy, compassion, boundaries, respect, courage, ethics, consequences, and critical thinking is no guarantee against abuse, but it’s certainly a great start. And I hope that raising children with empathy means raising adults who believe survivors and provide support.

Share this post Facebook Bluesky Linkedin Email

Jessica Anne Pressler, LCSW, is a Columbia University–trained psychotherapist, hospice grief counselor, author, and mental health advocate with more than four decades of experience.

49ers trade deadline grade reveals winners and losers from deals

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

All teams serious about making a Super Bowl run made sure to take advantage of the trade deadline, and the San Francisco 49ers addressed their concerns.

The 49ers have a 7-1 record through Week 9 without a fully healthy roster. All throughout the season, they've missed multiple starters and key players. The most impacted positions were defensive line and wide receiver.

There was a clear focus at the trade deadline and the Niners addressed it in two separate trades with the Indianapolis Colts, bringing former 49ers defensive lineman DeForest Buckner and ex-Cal wide receiver Keenan Allen back to the Bay Area.

San Francisco faced a number of injuries to their receiving core: Mike Evans, Deebo Samuel Sr., De'Zhaun Stribling, Ricky Pearsall and Christian Kirk. The good news for the 49ers is Samuel trending in the right direction. Niners head coach Kyle Shanahan told reporters Monday that he expects to have Samuel back at practice on Wednesday.

Injuries also plagued a number of defensive linemen, including rookie Gracen Halton, who dislocated his ankle in a Nov. 8 win against the Las Vegas Raiders. He was placed on IR, the team announced Tuesday.

Other injuries on the D-line included Nick Bosa, Mykel Williams, Alfred Collins, Sam Okuayinonu, CJ West, Mikail Kamara and Andrew Farmer II. Bosa returned against the Raiders after missing time with a knee and calf injury. Shanahan said he also expects West back at practice.

Allen and Buckner come in as immediate plug-ins to help solve San Francisco's health issues as the team as players look to make their eventual return to the gridiron.

The 49ers earn an overall trade grade of a "B" for their moves.

NFL trade grades: NFL trade deadline grades reveal which team won every major deal

The 49ers made only two moves, same team, different days. Their first trade was for Buckner on Oct. 27, then moments before the trade deadline, the circled back to the Colts and dealt for Allen.

The 49ers' trade return has been to their benefit early on. Defensively, San Francisco wanted to get into the backfield more often, fill gaps and pressure quarterbacks. Although only two trades were made, the Niners still made it worth their while.

Bringing back Buckner from the Colts added to the defense's continuity led by Fred Warner, Dre Greenlaw and Bosa. Buckner's presence had an immediate impact in his first game was against the Raiders, although he didn't register a sack and had just one tackle.

"I thought we hit the quarterback a lot. I thought we also rushed really well in some key situations," Shanahan said on Monday. "Buckner got him on the ground a number of times. [DL] Keion [White] got him on the ground a couple times. [Las Vegas Raiders QB] Kirk's [Cousins] pretty good at getting rid of the ball when we do beat someone, but I thought our pass rush and the pressure on the quarterback, I thought we had a good game in that manner."

Allen was brought back to California. He started his NFL career in 2013 with the then-San Diego Chargers, spending the first 10 years of his NFL career with that team and earning six Pro Bowl selections. Allen made a name for himself as a possession receiver who was an exquisite in route runner.

He's added to a wide receiver room that will eventually get back Evans and Samuel. Scary.

The 49ers have been hanging on with the healthy receivers they have such as Jordan Watkins, Demarcus Robinson, Jacob Cowing and recently signed vet Brandin Cooks. They've been holding it down while their stars get back.

"I think [Cooks] has been effective. I think we went to him twice yesterday and [QB] Brock [Purdy] got to him twice and made both of those plays. He's been doing like everyone else," Shanahan said. "I think he doesn't have many busts. I think he's getting used to the offense and being a part of the group and I think all those guys have, there's not really one guy ahead of the other too much."

While Purdy has missed his much-anticipated target in Evans, it's created more anonymity in the 49ers offense.

"You never know where the ball is going to go," Shanahan said. "I think Jordan Watkins has been playing real well and he got more opportunities yesterday. His touchdown pass, he was fourth in the progression on that play. Brock went all the way across the board and got it to him and he made the play. There were other plays he was first in the progression and it's no different for Brandin Cooks and any of the other guys.

"It was no different for [WR Demarcus Robinson] D-Rob in the game," he added. "But I've been real happy with Brandin and how he's been blocking. When the ball's come his way, I think he's done a good job making the plays for the most part."

A week ago when looking at potential trade targets for the 49ers, a heavy suggestion was to revisit the Colts, since they already made a deal for Buckner, and ask for another player. This time a wide receiver.

The 49ers did that, but they didn't get the expected guy. No shade whatsoever to Allen, because it's still a great pickup. However, a 25-year-old slot receiver in Josh Downs went to the Kansas City Chiefs. Featuring similar stats to Allen, Downs piled up 33 receptions for 354 yards and a touchdown through nine games. He averaged 10.7 yards per catch.

When it's all said and done, the Niners still have Brandon Aiyuk following his reinstatement by the NFL. San Francisco was given an additional two weeks to determine how they would proceed with his contract, whether to trade, keep or release Aiyuk. The Niners' two-week exemption ends on Nov. 23.

The trade deadline passed. So, there goes that idea. The 49ers could keep Aiyuk, if they wanted to use him still.

Shanahan has more than blatantly indicated that he does not expect Aiyuk to be a part of the team. Therefore, the next option would be to release him.

Aiyuk's agent Drew Rosenhaus said on "The Pat McAfee Show" that he expects Aiyuk to compete on some team upon his eventual release from the 49ers.

"Brandon will either play for the 49ers when the two weeks is up, or they can decide to release him, by which he will be a free agent, and I expect that there will be interest in him," Rosenhaus said. "I definitely expect him to play this season. Judging the comments from coach Shanahan, it doesn't sound like they plan on bringing Brandon back."

All the NFL news on and off the field. Sign up for USA TODAY's 4th and Monday newsletter.
