Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Remake Water Temple Walkthrough

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Nintendo Switch 2The Legend of ZeldaThe Legend of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time

Well, you’ve made it. You’re finally at the most infamous dungeon in all of Zelda-dom, the Water Temple of Ocarina of Time. Good news for you: In the remake, this dungeon is far, far easier than it’s ever been. A few of the edges have been sanded down, the rooms all look far more distinct and memorable so it’s less of a mental maze, and the iron boots can be equipped and de-equipped simply by pressing down on the gamepad. Compared to the original, it’s a smooth ride. But still, this is one of the most involved dungeons in the game, so buckle on that Zora Tunic and let’s get swimming.

To enter the Water Temple, you need to be wearing (no, I wasn’t joking) the Zora Tunic, which you get by freeing King Zora from his ice block back in Zora Domain. To do that, you need Blue Fire in a bottle, which you obtain in the Ice Cavern as an adult. You’ll also need the Iron Boots from that same cavern. If you haven’t gotten that far yet, back yourself up and come back to this guide later.

Once you’re in the temple, remove your iron boots and float to the top. Run forward, and you’ll be in a big, open room with a bunch of doors on all four sides across four different floors, and water below you. I’m going to be referring to this as the “main hall” going forward. We’re going to be here a lot. I’ll also be referring to doors by floor and cardinal direction, so if you’re ever confused about what I’m talking about, open that map.

The first thing you’re going to do is slay that Blue Tektite in front of you. Sword, hookshot, whatever you like, just get it out of the way.

Next, you’re going to equip those Iron Boots, descend to the first floor, and head through the east door. Once you’re inside, remove the boots to float upward for a cutscene with your fiancee, Ruto. When it’s over, grab the Dungeon Map from the chest in front of you, and then stand on the platform with the Triforce symbol and play Zelda’s Lullaby to lower the water level.

Real quick tip: There are three different spots to mess with the water level in this dungeon, and each one will raise it to a different spot. This one brings it down to the first floor, and is marked by glowy blue pillars. It might be good to make a note somewhere of which color results in which water level, just in case you ever get turned around or confused and the water level is in the wrong place.

Once the water has dropped, fearlessly jump down the hole, landing in the water below so it doesn’t damage you. Now, with the water taken care of, note that one of the torches in here is lit. Stand behind it with your bow equipped and aim an arrow through the fire to each of the two torches on the other end of the room to open the door in front of you, then enter. Defeat the three Spikes by blocking their attacks with your shield and swinging your sword when they’re vulnerable to obtain a Small Key. (Din’s Fire is also usable for this puzzle).

Backtrack the way you came in and head out into the main hall. There are two more aptly named Spikes out here waiting for you; get rid of them. To your left (west side, 1F) is a bombable/breakable wall. Knock that out, and use your Iron Boots to sink, sink, sink. Head down the underwater corridor and take care of the Shell Blade by hitting it with your hookshot when its mouth is all the way open. At the end of the corridor, take off the boots and rise up.

In here, climb the platform jutting out of the water and use the hookshot to grapple onto the pillars ahead of you. Take down the Tektites and the Like Like (Hookshot is great here), and you’ll be rewarded with the Compass. Now, all the way back to the main hall!

To the west (1F) is a block you can push forward until it drops in the water. Do that, then use the boots to sink again. Look out for the Shell Blade in the way, then rise back up at the end of the hall. You’ll find yourself in a room with a whirlpool and some statues underwater, and a chest on the other side.

This looks trickier than it is. Use the iron boots to drop into the whirlpool, aiming for the center platform (the serpent’s body) stretched down the middle of the pool. Stand on the platform and use your hookshot to smack the weird gem thing in the serpent’s mouth. This will unlock the underwater grate, allowing you to enter. You might need to don and doff the iron boots a few times to swim over properly, but with some careful aiming it’s very doable. There are two more Shell Blades to fight inside, after which you’re free to take off the boots, rise to the surface, and grab the small key.

Back across the whirlpool, out the door, into the main hall again. There’s a locked door on the central pillar here, directly in front of you, and you now have a key. Head inside.

Once inside, step forward, turn around, and look up for a grapple point. Use your hookshot to get on the platform above. Right in front of you is a spot to stand and play Zelda’s Lullaby to raise the water back up. (Note: the columns here are green, green brings the water to 2F! Write it down if you need to!) Before you leave this inside pillar, though, jump in the water and look down: a new gap has opened up with the water level rising.

Use iron boots to drop down, where three Spikes are waiting for you. Kill them with the hookshot, then use the hookshot to hit the green switch above your head, releasing a Shell Blade. Kill that too, then remove the iron boots where it was standing to rise up into the now-open area. There’s a red rupee in a chest on the way up, as well as a gold skulltula on the wall opposite. Funny, I feel like something else used to be here in the original game…anyway, grab these, backtrack the way you came in, and take off the boots to rise back up into the middle of the pillar. Then swim out the door into the main hall.

Directly in front of you across the water (2F, east side) is a bombable/hammerable wall in an alcove. You know what to do. Smash the pots for bombs if needed, otherwise use the Megaton Hammer if you’ve cleared the Fire Temple already, and head on through.

Be cautious as you head into the water and equip the Iron Boots. There are four Stingers waiting for you. Your hookshot will take care of them, but it’s easy to get overwhelmed, so take it slow (not that you can do otherwise with those boots on). Once they’re dead, proceed to the end of the underwater hallway and unequip the boots to rise to the surface.

In front of you is a pool of water with a switch in it, and a round platform being lifted by a fountain. Use your hookshot to hit the switch to lower the platform, stand on the platform, then use hookshot again on the same switch to raise yourself up to the floor above. Your reward is a Small Key. Take it, then backtrack all the way to the main hall again.

In the main hall, hop in the water and swim around the central pillar to the locked door directly across from where you just entered. It’s got glowing red columns around itâcan’t miss it. Use the Small Key and head inside. Smack the switch and ready yourself; a blue Tektite will descend and try to fight you. Once it’s dispatched, stand on the lowered platform and use your hookshot to hit the switch again and rise up.

It’s another Zelda’s Lullaby spot, this time with red lights, that will bring the water to 3F! Play that same song! Up goes the water. Jump down from the ledge next to the Triforce symbol and back into the water of the main room.

Mind any Tektites you haven’t dispatched already and swim on over to 3F, west side, for another locked door for which you conveniently have a key.

A couple of Keese will be waiting for you to slash them up. Take stock of your surroundings before you proceed. You’ll want to use the hookshot to grapple your way up these moving platforms to the top, one by one. Start by dropping down to the platform below the one you start on, and it’ll descend until you can grapple across. Then just work your way up, one by one. Don’t panic, it’s very doable, and there’s a stable platform to the side you can jump to if you need a second to collect yourself. Head through the door at the top once you reach it.

In this next room, I started by hitting all the Tektites below with my arrows just to get them out of the way. Then, use your hookshot to hit the switch at the center of the room and make the grapple points raise up. You’ll want to grapple to the first point directly in front of you, then (counterintuitively) hit the switch again to lower the points. Use the grapple point on the next wall to crossâyou’ll be able to continue along the ledge only if the grapple point pillars are lowered. Hit the switch yet again and grapple to the final pillar, hit the switchÂ again to lower the pillars. Climb atop the one next to you, then hit the switch one final time to raise the pillar and lift you within reach of the next floor.

Hit those pots for health, arrows, whatever before you go through the door, it’s time for one of the best bosses in the game!

Shadow Link is tough; he mirrors your moves! Don’t attack him head-on while his shield is up or he’ll do something I hear the kids call “aura farming.” I was able to beat him pretty easily by keeping my guard up and dodging his combos, waiting for an opening. In the first phase of the fight, I would dodge to the left side twice whenever he swung, then would come in for a combo toward his exposed back. In the second phase, this was trickier, but still very doable if I dodged backwards twice and then threw out the hookshot to stun him. Make sure you dodge backwards far enough when he gears up for a spin attack, and don’t let yourself get pushed too close to the arena walls, or he can pin you down for some big damage.

With Shadow Link down, head through the door opposite where you came in (use your map if you got turned around) to finally obtain the Longshot, which will let you hookshot targets even farther away than before! To escape this room, aim for the very far away targets above you, then drop down the waterfall into the cavern below. Let yourself float down the river, but mind the whirlpoolsâyou can use the iron boots to just walk between them underwater. About three-quarters of the way down the river, there’s a gold skulltula on a wall above you. Make sure you’re firmly iron-booted underwater, then use the longshot to smack it once to defeat it, and then again to collect the token.

Climb atop the stone outcropping on the end of the river and use the longshot again, once to hit the eye above the gates and open them, and again to grapple to the grapple point on the other side of the gates before they close on you.

We’re back in familiar territory, so head out to the main hall once again. We’ve still got some work to do, and we’re sorely in want of a boss key right about now. First, we need to get the water level down. Get those boots on and drop down to 1F east, the room where you first ran into Ruto. Swim up to the Triforce and play Zelda’s Lullaby to drop the water, then head back into the main hall. Then, you want to enter the central pillar again from the door on 1F. We’re here to raise the water level, but hold up. Grapple to the Triforce stand, then look at the ceiling for a grapple point you can now reach with the longshot. Fly on up there, then look across to the other side of the ceiling for another gold skulltula. Once you have that, drop back down and play Zelda’s Lullaby.

Everything’s in place! Exit the pillar and run around the central platform to stand on the floating block, looking across at the outer wall. You should see a gate with a grapple point on the other side, and an eye switch above. Once again, use the longshot to hit the eye, then immediately use it again on the grapple to get through. Drop into the pool and equip the iron boots, then walk into the hallway. There’s a movable block wedged into the wall hereâpull it out as far as you can, blocking off the hallway, then take off the boots and swim up and out of the pool. You should be able to see a fence ahead of you with a waterfall on the other side, and a grapple point on the ceiling. Grapple to it with the longshot, use the iron boots to sink into the water, then head down the hallway and push the block out of your way to clear a path to the chest with yet another small key.

Back to the main hall again, equipping the iron boots again and sinking down to 1F, north. Grapple across the gap and use your small key on the door. Inside, there are a lot of tektites in the way. Use your sword, shield, bow, whatever, to get them out of the way (seriously, they will knock you into whirlpools otherwise). Carefully cross the room, avoiding the boulders, using the iron boots as needed to stay out of whirlpools, and go through the door. In here, use the longshot to dispatch all the stingers, then smash the rock walls (with bombs or the megaton hammer) to reveal a block that can be pushed into the water to hit a switch. The switch will raise the water level for you to head out the door in the wall above.

Whew. Almost done. In here, step on the switch to raise platforms you can jump across, and a tektite with them. Get rid of it (and the other tektite on the other side of the room) as you see fit, then cross and go through the opening. On the other side, be careful. Boulders are rolling down this stream and will run you over if you step in at the wrong time. Wait for one to pass, then go under the waterfall and immediately turn to the left to jump into the alcove. There’s a shell blade waiting, so get that out of the way. Before you head through the door, turn back, wait for the boulder to pass, then jump back in the stream, through the waterfall, and look upâthere’s another gold skulltula near the ceiling. Grab it fast, then turn back and head through the door to, at long last, claim the boss key.

Great work, folks! Turn back, follow the boulders down the stream and into the room you were in earlier with the boulders and whirlpools, then back into the main hall. I’m sorry to say we’ve got to mess with the water levels one more time. You’ll need to go back into 2F west, head up the fountain, and play Zelda’s Lullaby one last time to raise the water and open a path up the big stairs to the north on 2F (technically 3F). Drop down, and head that direction. You’ll have to stand on the platform of the central pillar and longshot your way over. Load up on hearts from the pots before you go in, and get ready to fight.

Okay, not yet. Use your iron boots in this room to climb the waterfall and avoid the spiky things spinning back and forth.Â Now get ready to fight!

Morpha isn’t too bad, as bosses go. The boss itself is the weird red Metroid-looking ball thingie that’s hiding in the tentacles. Your main goal is to use the longshot to rip it out of its watery protection and whack it a bunch with your sword. This is pretty easy with Navi’s targeting; the main thing you need to worry about is getting hit by water tentacles. Morpha will manifest these out of the water and try and slam them on the ground near you. Early on, they’ll be pretty spread out, but later in the fight they’ll form a big wall that’s harder to avoid. If you get trapped you can also use the grapple points on the sides of the room to fly out of danger. I tried to stay on the edges as much as possible; you never really want to be in the water with Morpha. But you can use the central platforms to bait Morpha into an easier spot for you to longshot the ball out, or just to cross out of the way of attacks. Try to be aware of where Morpha the Ball is at all times, as this fight can get annoying if it’s always all the way across the room, just out of range. Use those grapple points!

That’s the Water Temple done, folks! Enjoy the cutscenes, and get the heck outta there to see how the Zora are doing.

Oh, psst. Once the cutscenes are over, you’ll be at Lake Hylia, just before sunrise. Check out the stone monument in front of you to make good use of that timing. You’re welcome!

REI vs. Dick’s Sporting Goods Black Friday Deals, Which Is Better?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The two brands are known for wilding out on sales, but here’s how to find the best deals.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

Your cart has been sitting there since October, and I get it. I’ve got a few (hundred) tabs open myself. As someone with many marathons, a few ultras, and more halfs and 5Ks than I can count, I can tell you the real Black Friday question isn’t whether to buy shoes and running gear; it’s where. REI and Dick’s are two of the names that come up the most often, and they handle the holiday very differently. REI is again giving its employees a paid day off on Friday, November 27, so its deals land early. Dick’s has typically stretched its sale across the weekend. Deciding who has the best deals comes down to these two questions: What are you actually shopping for, and how much do you need? Here’s how the deals stack up.

One thing both brands are doing, and doing pretty well, is taking some big-name running shoes and discounting them hard. Think Hoka, Asics, and Brooks top trainers with discounts of $50 or more, with deeper discounts on previous years’ models. The deals don’t match model to model, and each outlet has different shoes at different prices. For example, REI offers steeper discounts on Hoka, and Dick’s has better pricing on brands like Adidas.

The Hoka Bondi 9 is a favorite among distance runners, especially those with flatter feet. It has a supercritical foam midsole that makes it light and responsive, with a snug fit, a generously padded collar, and comfortable, max-stack cushioning. It’s a big improvement over the Bondis of antiquity, and it’s one of their most popular shoes overall. And the price difference between REI and Dick’s is significant, with REI the clear winner.

Can’t go wrong with the ultra-comfortable Adizero Evo SL from Adidas. It’s fun to run in; it’s bouncy, responsive, and keeps you happy on long runs. Dubbed by Jeff Dengate as the Shoe of the Year for 2025, this trainer remains one of our most beloved shoes of the past few years.

The On Cloudmonster 3 is the perfect mix of firm and cushioned, keeping you supported on your longest efforts, whether walking or running. It’s a max-cushioned trainer with On’s CloudTec technology that compresses and rebounds with every strike, making it responsive, a bit bouncy, and ready for high mileage.

REI is also known for its Co-op line of gear. It’s their own, high-quality gear that comes in at terrific prices. For Black Friday and Cyber Monday, many of those items are discounted even further, making it hard to say no to already affordable goodies. REI also offers a variety of smaller brands like Rabbit, which they are able to offer on sale. If you’re into smaller brands, or you’re a big fan of Co-op, REI is the place to hit up.

Rabbit makes great UPF-rated long sleeves that are perfect for layering on cooler days or staying cool in the sun on hotter days. This one has one of my favorite features—a watch panel that lets you wear the shirt over your hands with thumb holes, so you can still see your watch. The fabric is buttery, the seams are sealed, so they’re not itchy or irritating, and they launder like a dream.

REI makes really good hydration vests, often in a more expansive size range than other brands. I’ve tested this one, and I like it a lot. It has space for a bladder and two flasks, plus room for snacks, gear, and whatever you happen to take off during your run because you’ve overheated.

Some of the biggest sales at Dick’s Sporting Goods are on its biggest-ticket items like treadmills, running watches, and other exercise equipment. These are categories that REI doesn’t touch as much, and some not at all (treadmills). This area is where Dick’s really shines. You could get hundreds off this time of year, and you should take full advantage.

This is truly one of my favorite “get-up-and-run” treadmills, and for 40 percent off, it’s tripping into “cheap” treadmill territory. It’s a solid treadmill with a good-size deck, plenty of connectivity, incline, and speed, all at a fabulous price tag.

Suunto has improved so much over the past few years, and this is one of the best examples. It’s a dynamic, multisport running watch with terrific GPS, battery life, connectivity, and a family of apps to help keep you connected. It’s also one of the better-looking running watches available, and while that might not be the most important thing, it’s still worth considering.

Both brands have fantastic Black Friday discounts, but they look pretty different. Dick’s has the upper hand on big-ticket items, but their shoes are a bit more expensive. REI wins on shoe price, and in the smaller labels and private-label brands they’re known for. And both stores carry shoe brands the other doesn't. In the end, it’s about picking your faves and knowing what you’re shopping for.

For over a decade, she has tested parenting, fitness, home, and running gear and written in-depth guides to help readers with their next purchase. Holding multiple advanced degrees and currently studying kinesiology, Cat Bowen brings research-backed insight to all of her guides. Passionate about women’s health and neurodivergent inclusion, she advocates for closing research gaps and helping others—especially AudHD people—find joy in running and fitness.

Bioengineered Liver System Passes Safety Test in Phase I Trial

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

— The miroliverELAP also showed signs of efficacy in patients with syndromes of liver failure

A single-use bioengineered liver paired with an extracorporeal blood circuit that replicates key functions of a human liver was shown to be safe among patients with syndromes of liver failure in a small phase I trial.

There were no serious or unexpected adverse events (AEs) related to the miroliverELAP system, while every patient was still alive after the scheduled 48 hours of treatment, reported Christopher J. Danford, MD, of Intermountain Health in Salt Lake City, at the American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases annual meeting in Denver.

In addition, the system demonstrated signs of efficacy, as there was an improvement in encephalopathy and reductions in vasopressor requirements during the course of treatment.

While none of the patients in the study were transplant candidates, "many of them would have been potentially transplantable by the end of treatment, and based on those results, we're moving forward with our phase II and III clinical trial," Danford noted.

Syndromes of liver failure -- fulminant hepatic failure, acute alcoholic hepatitis (AAH), and acute-on-chronic liver failure (ACLF) -- are devastating conditions for which there's no good treatment other than liver transplant. Due to the lack of transplantable livers and the rapid onset of acute liver failure, a significant number of patients die before getting access to a liver transplant.

"The hope is that an external bioengineered liver may provide some support that either bridges the patient to recovery or transplant," Danford said. "However, there are no previous studies in humans involving a bioengineered liver."

The FDA granted a Regenerative Medicine Advanced Therapy designation to the miroliverELAP system for the treatment of acute liver failure in April. As described by its maker in a press release, the bioengineered liver is manufactured "by seeding a decellularized porcine liver scaffold with allogeneic human endothelial cells and human liver cells isolated from donated human livers." These donated livers are not considered suitable for transplantation and are provided by organ procurement organizations.

This study included seven critically ill patients who initiated therapy, five of whom completed the planned therapy (ranging in age from 27 to 43).

The primary etiology was AAH in four patients, and ACLF in the fifth, with a mean Model for End-Stage Liver Disease (MELD) score of 36.

Inclusion criteria included a MELD score ≥20, an International Normalized Ratio (INR) ≥2.0, and a West Haven hepatic encephalopathy (HE) grade of 1-3, while patients were excluded if eligible for transplantation.

Primary endpoints were serious AEs attributable to the system, and survival for the duration of the therapy. Secondary endpoints included 21-day post-treatment survival.

Three of the five patients who completed therapy were alive 21 days post-treatment.

There were a total of 42 AEs, with 17 occurring during treatment (11 possibly or probably related to treatment). System-related AEs among the full seven patients included grade 2-3 thrombocytopenia (four patients), anemia (three patients), bleeding (two patients), hypotension (two patients), and worsening lactic acidosis (one patient).

Twenty-five AEs occurred after therapy, one of which, a gastrointestinal bleed, was deemed possibly related to treatment. There were six serious AEs after therapy, including five deaths related to liver disease progression.

While this was primarily a safety study, "there were some promising [efficacy] signs," Danford noted.

Of the five patients who completed treatment, four experienced improvement in encephalopathy, with improvement sustained 48 hours post-treatment. In addition, while three of the five patients were on significant vasopressor therapy at baseline, all five were either off vasopressors or had their requirements reduced by 75% at the completion of the trial.

Travel demand signals softened in September 2026 while pricing kept revenue intact. Another Drug Shows Potential for Functional Cures in Chronic Hepatitis B

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

— With highest dose of siRNA therapy, 19% achieved a functional cure 24 weeks after treatment ended

Reductions in hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) were observed with a novel small interfering RNA (siRNA) therapy in patients with chronic hepatitis B virus (HBV) infection, according to a phase II study.

Among patients randomized to monotherapy with one of four doses of the siRNA HRS-5635, all 32 on the highest dose (400 mg) had HBsAg levels less than 100 IU/mL after 48 weeks of treatment, with 88% still at that level 24 weeks after treatment ended.

Among this high-dose group, 16% achieved a functional cure, with an HBsAg clearance level of less than 0.05 IU/mL after 48 weeks -- a percentage that rose to 19% by 24 weeks post-treatment, reported Xieer Liang, PhD, of Southern Medical University in Guangzhou, China, at the American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases annual meeting in Denver.

"A dose-dependent antiviral response was observed with HRS-5635," Liang said. "All HRS-5635 regimens were generally safe and well-tolerated."

An estimated 240 million people worldwide have chronic HBV infection, including about 640,000 U.S. adults. Chronic HBV can cause liver damage, cirrhosis, liver cancer, and death. Five antivirals and two immune modulators are approved in the U.S. to treat chronic HBV, with treatment regimens lasting from 6 months to over 1 year.

In a previous phase II trial, siRNA therapy with an immunomodulator reduced HBsAg in patients with chronic HBV infection. Last month, the FDA approved another nucleic acid-based therapeutic, the antisense oligonucleotide drug bepirovirsen (Hibsago), for patients with chronic HBV taking antiviral therapy based on results from the B-Well 1 and B-Well 2 studies. In those trials, 19% to 20% of patients with HBsAg levels between 100 and 3,000 IU/mL had a functional cure with bepirovirsen at week 72.

The current trial included patients with chronic HBV on stable nucleos(t)ide analogue therapy who had HBsAg levels over 100 and less than 3,000 IU/mL and HBV DNA levels less than 20 IU/mL. Patients could be hepatitis B envelope antigen (HBeAg) positive or negative, but they couldn't have cirrhosis. All patients received tenofovir amibufenamide during 48 weeks of HRS-5635 treatment.

Mean age ranged from 41.5 to 44.6 years in the four treatment groups, 59.4% to 76.7% were men, and mean HBsAg ranged from 1,058.2 IU/mL to 1,200.4 IU/mL. Most patients were HBeAg negative (65.6% to 73.3%).

The investigators defined functional cure as sustained HBV DNA suppression and HBsAg clearance of less than 0.05 IU/mL, with or without seroconversion, sustained for 24 weeks after treatment.

All four HRS-5635 doses achieved the study's primary endpoint of HBsAg reduction from baseline to week 12, with mean reductions of -1.26 log10 IU/mL in the 50-mg group, -1.90 log10 IU/mL in the 100-mg group, -1.99 log10 IU/mL in the 200-mg group, and -2.12 log10 IU/mL in the 400-mg group.

Among patients in the 400-mg group, 45% converted from HBeAg positive to HBeAg negative after 48 weeks of treatment, a percentage that slid to 27% at 24 weeks after treatment.

In patients receiving the 400-mg dose who were HBeAg negative and had HBsAg below 1,000 IU/mL at baseline, 33% reached the functional cure rate of HBsAg less than 0.05 IU/mL.

Across the four groups, 46.9% to 60% had a treatment-related adverse event. There were only two such events that were grade 3 or higher, and no serious adverse events related to treatment and no deaths occurred. Upper respiratory tract infections were the most common adverse event, occurring in 25% to 33.3% of patients in the four groups.

Alanine transaminase elevations occurred in up to 6.7% of patients during and after treatment, Liang said, but levels were generally stable during treatment, and no elevations reached a grade 3 level.

The follow-up period of 24 weeks after treatment ended was relatively short, Liang cautioned, but a longer-duration extension study is ongoing, along with a phase III trial.

Anxiety Is More Complex Than We Might Think

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 10, 2026 | Reviewed by Lybi Ma

Anxiety can be a necessary response to potential threats, but also a serious chronic condition that negatively affects our functioning and well-being. In either case, we feel wary and on edge, scared or irritable, with overactive thoughts. We are tense and easily startled into more powerful fear responses.

The key factor is whether our fear-system, our biological responses to threat, can ‘switch off’ once the threat has passed. If we hear a strange creak alone in bed at night, we might tense up, hold our breath and listen carefully; our heart beating faster. If we hear it again and recognise the hot water pipe noises, we can relax and quickly fall back to sleep.

However, if anxiety is a habitual state, we might still guess that it was the hot water pipes, but we can’t relax—we keep imagining an intruder breaking in—staying awake for hours in a state of fear.

What is anxiety? The biological response is at the core, what I call fear-alert, our instinctive reaction to danger sensed at a distance.[1] Our hearing, eyesight, and sense of smell become more acute to evaluate the threat, and our startle response is readied, and the nerve signal regulating our heart rate is withdrawn. The ‘brake’ that holds our fear responses in check is removed. We are now ready for extreme life-saving action.

This means our nervous system is highly volatile now; even minor or imagined threats can put us in a high-energy state of fear-arousal—fight, flight, or freeze. In this state, the heart's pumping capacity shifts up a gear, and a flood of hormones enriches the bloodstream with glucose and other nutrients, providing sustained energy.

Because fear-alert frequently switches into fear-arousal, our experience of ‘anxiety’ covers fear-arousal symptoms, such as a racing heart, dry mouth, sweaty palms, nausea or numbness, as well as the wariness, irritability and worrying thoughts of fear-alert.

None of this would be a problem if our fear responses deactivated once the threat had passed. However, this can only happen when we feel safe. If we are under threat and don’t feel safe for long periods, the biological processes that switch off our fear responses start to lose effectiveness, and we can get stuck in fear-mode, a chronic state of fear-alert that frequently tips us into fear-arousal or fear-collapse [2] [3]. This is another way of talking about complex trauma, which affects many areas of life, as we can see in the following example:

Adrian was a young man with a responsible managerial job who suffered from constant high levels of anxiety and was unable to make decisions about work and relationships, leading to constant self-criticism. He worked for an aggressive boss who treated him like a slave.

He found all social interactions stressful and exhausting; he felt the need to please everyone and keep them entertained. This burden led him to avoid intimate relationships, and the relationships he had didn’t last long.

He experienced all his pain and confusion as being "in his head," with his mind always overactive but his body “numb from the neck down.”

I worked with Adrian for nearly five years. He had previously tried CBT and found it unhelpful. I felt under huge pressure to alleviate his anxiety quickly, which triggered my own fear responses, but our work was painfully slow.

It emerged that he had suffered from anxiety since he was 3 years old. He had been highly disturbed by his mother’s acute distress at the death of her own mother. This loss of his caregiver was compounded by a sense that he had caused her collapse, and throughout his childhood he constantly tried to make her happy, becoming overburdened by responsibility.

Understanding the impact of his early history interested Adrian; however, it did not alleviate his anxiety, and realising that he didn’t have to take responsibility for the happiness of others also changed nothing. Being stuck for many years in a continuous state of fear activation had created a complex tangle of problems: nervous system volatility, chronic bodily tension, the numbing of sensation, deeply ingrained patterns of thought and behaviour towards others, habitual self-criticism and an inability to seek care.

We explored all these areas with little success at first. What started to shift things, however, was understanding and regulating my own fear-system. I stopped reacting with anxiety to Adrian’s pressing need for a cure and stopped overworking. I became a calmer presence, and he responded, eventually able to engage with ways of working that were not initially helpful. We worked on regulating breathing and using a focus-oriented approach that gradually helped him become reconnected with his bodily sensations. [4]

By the time Adrian finished work, he had become far less of a people-pleaser, had changed his boss and asserted himself more at work. He was in a relationship where he was gradually allowing himself to accept care as well as give it, and his generalised anxiety had greatly diminished.

Anxiety can be highly complex, and treating it at the level of a thought disorder that needs correction can lead to disappointment and frustration. Treatment may take time, and it may consider many aspects and use several different approaches. It also requires the therapist to understand and regulate their own anxiety in the work.

1. Also referred to as our ‘orienting response’.

2. For a more detailed explanation see my book Guilding. M. (2026). Fear in the Therapy Room: A survival guide for working with complex trauma. London; Hammersmith Health Books, pp. 45-50.

3. The metabolic shutdown triggered by the parasympathetic nervous system is the biological state underpinning depression.

4. Gendlin, E.T. (1996). Focusing-Oriented Psychotherapy. A manual of the experiential method. New York: The Guilford Press.

MusiCares’ 2026 Wellness in Music Survey: 5 Statistics That Paint a Sobering Picture

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The situation is even worse for emerging professionals (aged 18 to 24).

MusiCares shared key findings from its 2026 Wellness in Music Survey on Tuesday (Nov. 10), revealing that financial instability, limited access to healthcare and persistent mental health challenges remain closely interconnected across the music industry.

More than 3,300 music professionals responded to this year’s survey in English and Spanish, representing every U.S. state and Puerto Rico and more than 40 professions across the industry. The annual survey examines more than 100 indicators, including financial security, physical and mental health, access to health insurance and preventive care, substance use and confidence in sustaining a career in music.

MusiCares’ takeaway from the study: “Taken together, the 2026 findings point to a need for the industry to look beyond individual wellness interventions and more closely examine the underlying conditions affecting whether music professionals can build healthy, sustainable careers.”

This year’s survey also included dedicated outreach to emerging music professionals aged 18 to 24 with support from Live Free 999, a nonprofit founded by Carmela Wallace in honor of her son, the late rapper Juice WRLD, offering a closer look at the challenges facing the next generation entering the industry.

Since its founding in 1989, MusiCares has distributed more than $146 million in financial assistance to the music community. Findings from the Wellness in Music Survey help MusiCares identify where needs are most urgent.

In recent years, survey findings have helped shape expanded services, including access to women’s and family care through Maven Clinic, increased education around cannabis use, and scaled preventive care services. Following last year’s Wellness in Music data showing persistently high rates of suicidal ideation among music professionals, MusiCares also expanded its mental health resources through Tune Into Mental Health, developed with The Jed Foundation (JED) to provide mental health and suicide prevention resources tailored specifically to the music community.

Between 46% and 50% of music professionals who responded to the survey earn less than $50,000 annually, while only 30% say they can comfortably cover their expenses through work in music. Finances remain the leading single source of stress, anxiety and depression across demographics.

Approximately 84% to 85% of music professionals who responded to the survey report having health insurance compared with 93% of the general U.S. population. Coverage is lower among Black and Latino music professionals who responded at 79% and 80.6%, respectively.

Even for those who are insured, high deductibles, costly out-of-pocket expenses and limited coverage can make it difficult to access the care they need. Among those without insurance, more than 60% cite affordability as the reason they do not have coverage. Preventive care access also differs sharply by insurance status. Nearly 80% of insured respondents reported receiving an annual exam compared with just one-third of those without coverage, with similar disparities across dental, vision, hearing and cancer screenings.

Despite increased attention on mental health, the proportion of music professionals using available care has not substantially changed. Over the last five years, the percentage of music professionals who responded to the survey who report having accessed therapy has remained relatively steady at 57% to 59%. Among those interested in therapy who have not received it, 45% cite an inability to pay as the primary barrier. Suicidal ideation among respondents has also consistently measured two to three times higher than the U.S. general population.

Within the overall survey sample, 203 respondents were emerging music professionals (defined as those aged 18 to 24). With the support provided by Live Free 999, MusiCares was able to analyze this group more closely to compare their experiences with those of the broader respondent pool. Among them, 68.8% earned $25,000 or less in the previous year and 86.4% earned $50,000 or less. Seventy-nine percent said they could not comfortably cover their expenses through work in music, compared with 70% of respondents overall, while 72.7% reported low or very low confidence in their ability to cover future expenses through music work alone.

Health coverage among this younger group presents another important consideration. While 89.7% of emerging professionals who responded to the survey currently have health insurance, 57.4% receive that coverage through a parent. MusiCares’ summary: “As these professionals age out of family coverage, many may enter an industry where employer-sponsored insurance is far from guaranteed, underscoring the need to help early-career music professionals prepare for health care costs and coverage as their careers evolve.”

Want More Out of Therapy? Let Your Therapist Know

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 10, 2026 | Reviewed by Gary Drevitch

"I like my therapist, but sometimes I wish they would just ask me more open-ended questions."

An amazing colleague, who is also professional coach, recently said this to me. This colleague shared how her therapist is brilliant and helpful, but often shifts too quickly into problem-solving mode. I inquired if my colleague had ever given her therapist this feedback and she shook her head. I asked her what stopped her and she replied that she just never thought of doing so, but felt that her therapist would likely respond positively if she did. I encouraged her, in the same way that I encourage you, dear reader, to regularly be sharing feedback in your sessions.

Licensed therapists have years of training, including learning about evidence-based practices and getting supervised training in providing therapy. Your therapist shows up to your sessions with a lot of knowledge and skill that can help you recognize patterns, process significant life events, and so much more. But no matter how amazing your therapist is, one thing that they can never know, unless you tell them, is how helpful you find their therapeutic approach and the information that they’re sharing with you.

Therapists try to build a strong rapport with you, as they know that having a strong therapeutic alliance is important for you to feel safe and fully engage in your session. Research tells us that the stronger a therapist’s interpersonal skills are, the more likely their patients are to return for another session.1 Nevertheless, the therapist cannot know if you do not understand Information that they are trying to share with you, or if it just doesn’t feel aligned with your perspective, unless you tell them. You, your therapist, and your work together will benefit from this information.

It can feel awkward, giving feedback—especially if after reading this article, you’re the one who’s prompting such a discussion in your sessions. (Feel free to share this article as a jumping off point for discussion in your therapy session!)

If part of the reason that you don’t want to give feedback to your therapist is because you are afraid that they will stop liking you, that is also really important information that you need to share with your therapist. There could be many reasons for you feeling this way, and they are all really important to identify and discuss in therapy.

It’s normal for you to want other people to like you. This is especially true of your therapist, with whom you have shared a lot of sensitive information about yourself. Therapists have an ethical obligation to do no harm, to try to benefit you with their care, to build trust, practice fairly and justly, and respect the dignity and privacy of all people. This means that the therapist needs to be making decisions based on what they feel is healthiest for you and the therapeutic relationship. Therefore, even if your feedback is somehow given in such a way that your therapist could feel negatively about it, that is not a reason that you would not share it.

A therapists job is to take in the information that you’re sharing and to consider how the therapy session could be augmented to better meet your needs. They will not stop liking you if you provide constructive feedback, and if you perceived that were somehow to be the case, that would be a really important area for discussion and ultimately may signal that you and your therapist are no longer a good fit.

The feedback you provide can help the therapist engage in shared decision-making with you to see if you need a new treatment approach, if they need to refer you to a colleague so you can meet with someone who specializes in a different areas (e.g., couples counseling or EMDR sessions). This could mean engaging with different clinicians for different treatments at the same time or pausing your current treatment while receiving this care.

As a therapist, avoiding feedback doesn't mean you can assume a client is completely satisfied with the therapy: it more likely means you won't have a good understanding of the reasons why clients discontinue their sessions with you. Asking for feedback routinely normalizes the practice, helps you provide better care, and helps you learn and grow as a clinician so you can build your skills and continue benefiting your clients.

To find a therapist, visit the Psychology Today Therapy Directory.

M., Anderson, T., Wampold, B. E., & Owen, J. (2026). Multimodal Assessments of Therapist Characteristics Are Largely Unrelated to Patient Outcomes: A Preregistered Analysis. Ben White: England legend demands 'lifetime ban' for Arsenal star after squad snub

Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Chris Waddle has called for a “lifetime ban” for Ben White after the Arsenal defender’s previous refusal to represent England.

White ended a self-imposed exile from the England national set-up in March 2026 when he accepted a call-up from boss Thomas Tuchel, featuring in friendlies against Japan and Uruguay.

Before that, White had not represented his country since the 2022 World Cup, quitting the tournament shortly before the end of the group stage for ‘personal reasons’.

It has been suggested that White’s refusal to play for England stemmed from a ‘barbed comment’ made by then-assistant manager Steve Holland, with former Three Lions manager Gareth Southgate reiterating that he would call-up the Arsenal defender if he was available for selection.

Waddle, capped 62 times by his country, believes that White should never have been given the chance to play for England again.

Speaking to iGaming.com, Waddle stated: “Listen, Ben White shouldn’t be in the England squad, that’s my opinion.

“He refused to represent his country. I'm surprised Thomas (Tuchel) brought him back in, and I don't get why he has. There might be a shortage of fullbacks, but when somebody turns down playing for their country, like Ben White did at Euro 2024, to me that's a lifetime ban.

“I'd say, ‘Forget it mate, on my watch you're not getting back in.’”

The former Tottenham midfielder said he would go one step further, permanently marking White’s England record after his refusal to accept a call-up.

Waddle added: “Once you turn your country down, there should be an asterisk next to your name that means you will never get the opportunity to put on that England shirt again. End of story.”

White has been included in the Three Lions’ squad to face Croatia and Spain in the Nations League, prompting discourse about his refusal to play for England under Gareth Southgate.

Alzheimer’s may have early warning signs outside the brain

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Alzheimer's disease is best known for its effects on memory and thinking, but new research suggests that some symptoms may develop in a surprising place: outside the brain.

Scientists at the University of Central Florida (UCF) have discovered that genetic mutations associated with Alzheimer's can interfere with the nerves that control muscle movement, even without involvement from the brain or spinal cord. The findings could help explain why some people with Alzheimer's experience changes in walking, balance, or coordination long before noticeable memory problems emerge.

The discovery raises the possibility that movement difficulties could provide earlier warning signs of the disease and that treatments focused exclusively on the brain may overlook important problems elsewhere in the body.

The research was led by James Hickman, a professor at UCF's Nanoscience Technology Center, and Research Professor Xiufang "Nadine" Guo, working alongside scientists at the healthcare technology company Hesperos. Funded by the National Institutes of Health's National Institute on Aging, the study used laboratory systems built from human cells to investigate how inherited Alzheimer's mutations affect movement. The findings were published in Alzheimer's & Dementia: The Journal of the Alzheimer's Association.

"Motor deficits may be an earlier indication [of Alzheimer's]," Guo says. "If we can detect those changes and intervene earlier, that could help delay the onset of central nervous system symptoms."

Why Alzheimer's Symptoms May Appear Before Memory Loss

Alzheimer's is generally associated with progressive memory loss, confusion, and declining cognitive abilities. However, doctors have also observed that some patients develop physical symptoms, including changes in balance, coordination, and gait (manner of walking), years before clear cognitive difficulties become apparent.

These observations have raised an important question: Are such movement problems simply a consequence of damage occurring in the brain, or could Alzheimer's also interfere directly with nerves elsewhere in the body?

To investigate, the UCF team focused on familial Alzheimer's, a rare inherited form of the disease that typically develops earlier in life (from 40 to 65 years of age) than the more common form.

Their experiments revealed that motor neurons carrying mutations linked to familial Alzheimer's could disrupt the connections responsible for controlling muscles, even when the brain and spinal cord were completely absent from the experimental system.

Motor neurons are specialized nerve cells that transmit signals to muscles, telling them when to contract and produce movement. The new findings suggest that Alzheimer's mutations can interfere with this process directly, rather than affecting movement only through damage to the brain.

"This is the first time it's been demonstrated that deficits in the peripheral nervous system can arise directly from these mutations," Hickman says. "It means drugs that target the brain may not fix problems in the rest of the body."

The peripheral nervous system includes the nerves located outside the brain and spinal cord. These nerves carry signals between the central nervous system and the rest of the body, helping control movement, sensation, and other essential functions.

Guo also points out that preserving the ability to move could have benefits beyond physical health. Regular physical activity is associated with better cognitive well-being, so protecting motor function may also help support brain health.

How a Miniature Human Cell System Revealed the Discovery

To examine the relationship between Alzheimer's mutations and muscle movement, the scientists used an innovative laboratory platform known as "human-on-a-chip" technology.

Developed and manufactured through Hesperos, a company co-founded by Hickman, these miniature systems bring different types of human cells together under controlled conditions. They allow researchers to recreate specific biological functions and observe how diseases interfere with them.

Unlike conventional laboratory experiments that examine isolated cells, these systems can reproduce important interactions between different tissues. They also provide an alternative to animal models, which do not always accurately reflect how human diseases behave.

For this study, the team created a miniature model of the neuromuscular junction, the connection where a motor neuron communicates with a muscle cell.

The model was deliberately designed without a brain or spinal cord. This was a crucial part of the experiment because it allowed the researchers to investigate whether Alzheimer's-related changes could cause movement problems independently of the central nervous system.

The scientists combined healthy muscle cells with motor neurons derived from stem cells that carried genetic mutations associated with familial Alzheimer's disease. Stem cells are cells that can develop into specialized types of cells, making them valuable tools for studying human diseases in the laboratory.

The researchers then examined how effectively the altered nerve cells communicated with otherwise healthy muscle tissue.

The results showed that the Alzheimer's-associated mutations could interfere with normal nerve-to-muscle function. This suggests that at least some movement difficulties linked to the disease may arise directly in the peripheral nervous system rather than being caused entirely by degeneration in the brain.

Why Damaged Nerve Connections Can Affect Walking and Balance

Every voluntary movement depends on successful communication between the nervous system and the muscles.

At the neuromuscular junction, an electrical signal traveling along a motor neuron triggers chemical communication that causes a muscle to contract. This process makes everyday activities possible, from walking and maintaining posture to lifting objects and performing precise movements.

When the connection becomes impaired, muscles may respond less effectively to nerve signals. Depending on the severity and location of the problem, this can contribute to weakness, reduced coordination, and difficulty sustaining physical activity.

To evaluate these effects, the research team measured how consistently nerve signals produced muscle contractions and how long the muscles could continue contracting before becoming fatigued.

These measurements provided a direct assessment of how well the nerve and muscle cells worked together. They also resemble aspects of the functional testing physicians use when examining patients with movement disorders.

"You can't move unless the motor circuit works," Hickman says. "When a doctor taps your knee to check your reflex, they're testing that exact connection."

By demonstrating impaired nerve-to-muscle communication in a system containing no brain or spinal cord, the study provides evidence that mutations linked to familial Alzheimer's can produce effects beyond the central nervous system.

However, the experiments do not establish that these changes necessarily occur before memory loss in patients. Further research will be needed to determine how closely the laboratory findings reflect the progression of Alzheimer's in people, particularly those with the more common forms of the disease.

Could This Discovery Lead to Earlier Alzheimer's Detection?

The findings suggest a potentially important new direction for Alzheimer's research: looking beyond memory and other cognitive symptoms when investigating the disease.

If future studies confirm that changes in motor function are detectable early in certain patients, tests of walking, coordination, or nerve-to-muscle activity might eventually help identify disease-related changes before more obvious neurological symptoms develop.

The results also raise questions about treatment. Many Alzheimer's therapies are designed to address processes occurring in the brain, but medications that target the central nervous system may not necessarily correct abnormalities affecting peripheral nerves and muscles.

Understanding these separate effects could help researchers investigate treatments that protect physical function alongside cognitive health.

Human-on-a-Chip Technology Could Transform Disease Research

Beyond the implications for Alzheimer's, the researchers see considerable potential in using human-on-a-chip systems to investigate other diseases and evaluate possible treatments.

Because these platforms contain human cells and reproduce measurable biological activities, they can help scientists observe disease-related changes in conditions that more closely resemble human physiology than some conventional experimental models.

This approach may be particularly useful in drug development, where researchers need to understand not only how a potential treatment affects individual cells but also how it influences interactions between different tissues.

Such systems can also uncover biological effects that may be difficult to detect in animal experiments, potentially helping researchers identify promising therapies and recognize limitations earlier in development.

For Hickman, the progress represents the culmination of three decades of work devoted to understanding disease through more realistic laboratory models.

"These systems let us study disease in a way that's closer to what actually happens in the human body, and that's what we need to develop better treatments," he says.

Materials provided by University of Central Florida. Original written by Emily Dougherty. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/advocate-health-increase-total-employee-compensation-846m-2027" hreflang="en">Advocate Health to increase total employee compensation by $846M for 2027</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Advocate Health to increase total employee compensation by $846M for 2027" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Nonprofit health system Advocate Health will invest an additional $1.24 billion in its workforce beginning in 2027, with approximately $846 million dedicated towards compensation and $390 million towards incremental benefits.

Advocate will increase its minimum wage to $20 an hour, building on its $18.85 per hour minimum wage established this year.

For a 40-hour workweek, that wage would pay over $26,520 more per year than the federal minimum wage, it said.

Compensation investments will also include merit increases, incentives, pay-range updates and other investments in pay, executives said in the Nov. 5 announcement.

“The most important investment we make isn’t in buildings, technology or equipment. It’s in our people,” said Eugene A. Woods, Advocate Health CEO, in a statement. “Healthcare is a 24/7 calling and every single day our 170,000 teammates bring extraordinary skill, compassion and commitment to the patients and communities we serve. Their dedication is what brings our purpose to life for the millions of people who count on us each year.”

The country’s third-largest nonprofit health system currently covers approximately 83% of employee medical costs, and plans to invest an additional $138 million for what it says will absorb “more than 90% of the projected $149 million increase in medical plan costs.”

Moreover, the health system’s 2027 benefits package extends fully paid parental bonding leave to four weeks enterprise-wide; expands paid bereavement leave; continues coverage for GLP-1 medications for weight loss; provides no-cost mental health support for employees and family members; continues sustained retirement contributions of up to 6% of eligible pay and offers education assistance across more than 1,300 eligible programs.

The package also offers student loan repayment assistance, with lifetime support of up to $21,000 for eligible full-time employees and $10,500 for eligible part-time employees.

“The benefits that matter most are the ones that make a real difference in people’s lives,” said Nakesha Lopez, Advocate Health senior executive vice president and chief people and culture officer, in a statement. “We want our benefits to reflect what teammates need at different stages of their lives, while keeping affordability at the forefront and providing meaningful support for their health, families and financial futures.”

Plant-Based Milk Alternatives May Not Give You Enough Iodine. Here’s Why That Matters

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Replacing dairy with plant-based alternatives (PBAs), even partially, could substantially lower iodine intake, especially if those alternatives aren’t fortified, according to a recent study published in the Lancet Planetary Health.

The United Kingdom-based researchers found that only 12% of UK plant-based milk, yogurt, and cheese products analyzed were fortified with iodine. This compares with 61% for calcium and 46% for vitamin B12.

In the United States, the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) provides guidance on the nutritional content of PBAs, but fortification with iodine is voluntary.

The study found that replacing as little as 20% of dairy intake with plant-based alternatives could increase the risk of inadequate iodine intake, particularly among adolescent girls and women of reproductive age.

“People often worry about a decline in calcium intake when they replace some of their dairy with plant-based alternatives. As a result, iodine is frequently overlooked. This study serves as a valuable reminder of iodine’s significance as a nutrient,” said Victoria Finn, MD, board certified endocrinologist at Medical Offices of Manhattan, who wasn’t involved in the study.

The study included analysis of UK plant-based milk, cheese, and yogurt products, as well as dietary data from 3,500 people.

The researchers used statistical modeling to predict how swapping dairy for PBAs might affect iodine and other nutrients.

The people who are most vulnerable to iodine inadequacy are adolescent females and people at reproductive age, especially those who are pregnant or who plan to become pregnant.

The researchers estimated that replacing 20% to 35% of dairy intake with PBAs would increase the proportion of people with inadequate iodine intake:

When the researchers modeled the replacement of all dairy products with PBAs, the proportion of adolescent females with inadequate iodine intake rose to 80%. Among females ages 19 to 49, that proportion remained relatively stable as replacement rates increased.

It is important to keep in mind that the study used dietary modeling rather than direct measurement of iodine status.

Iodine is important for thyroid hormone production, metabolism, and both fetal and child brain development.

“Your thyroid gland requires iodine to produce thyroid hormones, which play a crucial role in regulating metabolism and supporting normal growth and development,” said Finn.

She added that if you consistently consume too little iodine over an extended period, the thyroid gland may enlarge and eventually struggle to produce enough hormones. This condition is known as hypothyroidism, which can lead to various symptoms such as lethargy, sleepiness, weight gain, dry skin, and more.

“Hypothyroidism can be particularly dangerous during pregnancy, as the baby’s brain, nervous system, and organs rely heavily on the mother’s thyroid hormone during the early stages of development,” Finn added.

In the United Kingdom, dairy is a major iodine source due to cattle feed supplementation, not because dairy is naturally high in iodine.

In the United States, dairy is also an important source of iodine for the same reasons.

However, the risk profile for iodine deficiency in the U.S. may differ from the U.K., as iodized salt is also a significant source of iodine and is widely available in the U.S., but not in the U.K.

According to the National Institutes of Health (NIH), the recommended daily intake of iodine depends on your age.

Plant-based or plant-forward diets have grown in popularity, in part due to the health benefits they offer. For example, consuming less meat can reduce your risk of heart disease, type 2 diabetes, obesity, and high cholesterol.

However, as this study suggests, it may be possible to reduce your iodine intake when transitioning to a plant-based diet. There are ways you can protect your iodine intake when choosing a more plant-forward diet, though.

Check the labels on plant-based dairy alternatives to find those fortified with iodine. You can also read the nutrition label. Iodine may be listed as potassium iodide or potassium iodate.

Seafood, eggs, and iodized salt are all good sources of iodine.

You can also discuss iodine supplements with your healthcare professional.

“Iodine supplements should be taken with caution, especially without checking your thyroid levels,” said Finn. “Additionally, be cautious with kelp supplements, as their iodine content can vary, and excessive iodine intake can disrupt thyroid function.”

Mammograms May Offer Early Insight Into Cardiovascular Disease

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Mammograms are widely used to help detect breast cancer, but they may also offer insight into heart health.

The routine X-ray screening may identify breast arterial calcification (BAC), which is the presence of calcium deposits within the walls of the small arteries in the breast.

On a mammogram, BAC is commonly identified as thin, white, parallel lines that give a “tram-track” appearance.

While BAC is harmless to the breasts, it is associated with a significantly higher risk of cardiovascular disease. A 2022 study found women with BAC were 51% more likely to develop heart disease or experience a stroke than those without it.

Laxmi Mehta, MD, a cardiologist at The Ohio State University Wexner Medical Center, said BAC is associated with cardiovascular risk factors, such as diabetes, high blood pressure, and high cholesterol. Additionally, BAC has been connected to:

“The reasons for BAC resulting in higher risks for cardiovascular disease are not definitely determined; however, there are some potential mechanisms, one being that they share similar risk factors that result in vascular injury or metabolic stress that result in vascular calcification and atherosclerosis,” Mehta told Healthline.

Cheng-Han Chen, MD, interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center, added that because BAC and heart disease share many of the same risk factors, “calcium in breast arteries therefore suggests disease in the heart (coronary) and brain arteries as well,” he told Healthline.

While 1 in 39 females in the United States dies from breast cancer each year, cardiovascular disease causes one out of every three deaths, resulting in 1 death per minute.

“Cardiovascular disease is still the most common cause of death in premenopausal women,” Richard Reitherman, MD, PhD, a radiologist and medical director of breast imaging at MemorialCare Breast Center at Orange Coast Medical Center, told Healthline.

“The presentation of cardiovascular disease in premenopausal women is notoriously different than in postmenopausal women, primarily [because] there are no typical symptoms such as chest pain.”

Reitherman said that a prevalent and common health screening procedure such as a mammogram can lead to the early identification of women with silent cardiovascular disease.

“Cardiovascular outcome studies that have included breast arterial calcifications as a risk factor are increasingly reported in the medical literature,” he said.

“The hope is that identifying younger women with silent cardiovascular risks [like BAC along with] appropriate integrated risk identification, analysis, and interventions can significantly reduce mortality in this age group.”

Mehta said there are several reasons BAC has been overlooked as a risk factor for heart disease.

“Mammograms are completed for assessment of breast cancer, and BAC has been historically an ‘incidental’ finding, which is not clinically relevant from an oncologic standpoint,” she said.

Additionally, there is no standardization on quantification of BAC severity, nor is it a required component of mammogram reports, said Mehta.

However, routine reporting of BAC on mammograms is an urgent goal of the American College of Radiology, Society of Breast Imaging, and Medicare, said Reitherman.

In fact, it is gaining traction in the United States. A new Maryland law, passed on October 1, requires mammography centers to notify women when a BAC is detected on their mammogram.

“Automated identification and reporting of these calcifications detected on screening mammograms will become increasingly prevalent in the coming months [and] years,” said Reitherman.

The next step is to identify this group of women to primary care physicians so that appropriate risk evaluation can take place, Reitherman added.

In addition to doctors routinely screening for conditions related to heart disease, such as diabetes, high blood pressure, and high cholesterol, he hopes BAC will also be routinely considered.

“Under current development are protocols to identify patients needing a cardiologist’s training and expertise to appropriately stratify this group of patients,” said Reitherman.

“The common healthcare behavior [of] having regular mammograms not only reduces risk of morbidity and mortality from breast cancer but will hopefully contribute to the reduction of morbidity and mortality from cardiovascular disease in younger women.”

He said this type of integrated healthcare approach demonstrates cross-specialty integration and coordination among women, radiologists, and their healthcare providers to improve women’s lives.

“This is an amazing game-changing opportunity,” Reitherman said.

Mehta said it’s reasonable for women to ask if BAC is present on their mammogram report.

“But if it is not uniformly reported by the radiologist, the individual clinician may not have the answer,” she said.

More research is needed before BAC on mammograms can be used routinely to predict heart disease.

Finding out your BAC could provide additional information to help you and your doctors decide how aggressively to address your cardiovascular risk factors, Chen said.

Reitherman agreed. “The goal of reducing morbidity and mortality from any cause such as cardiovascular disease is based on recognition of risk factors, followed by appropriate lifestyle and pharmacologic interventions,” he said.

10 Deadliest Weapons in Transformers History

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Across its many continuities, the Transformers franchise features an extensive and varied arsenal of weaponry shaped by the conflicts between its two major warring factions. These weapons serve purposes ranging from strengthening individual warriors in battle to threatening entire civilizations, while some carry historical significance through their origins and connections to Cybertronian history.

Their reputations across the series’ many interpretations are shaped not only by destructive force, but also by the circumstances surrounding their creation, ownership, and use in battle. Some are tied to ancient Transformers lore, while others emerge from later technological advancements and the demands of continuing warfare across competing forces.

The Underbase is an immense repository containing the accumulated knowledge of the entire Cybertronian race, preserved as patterns of light within Marvel’s Generation One continuity. Its contents also possess considerable destructive power, and as a result, long before the main events, Optimus Prime sent it into space to deny Megatron access to its dangerous capabilities.

That threat, however, resurfaced millions of years later when the Underbase approached Earth, drawing rival Decepticon groups into a race to secure it. Starscream secretly pursued the archive for himself, absorbing part of its power and massacring most of the unbonded Autobots and Decepticons before its full force ultimately obliterated him.

Among Megatron’s most recognizable pieces of equipment is the Fusion Cannon, a powerful arm-mounted weapon that has accompanied the Decepticon leader across numerous Transformers continuities. In Generation One, its technical profile describes a particle-beam system capable of converting small quantities of matter into enormous amounts of explosive nuclear energy to power its attacks.

The weapon can discharge energy across distances reaching twelve miles, with enough force to level a small town in a single blast. His specifications also describe an interdimensional link to a black hole, allowing the cannon to draw antimatter at considerable cost to Megatron’s own systems during even one discharge.

The Spark Extractor is a fearsome Decepticon weapon featured in Transformers Prime, designed to remove a Cybertronian’s spark from its mechanical body. It can attack every Transformer within its operational radius simultaneously, draining the life force that sustains them and leaving their mechanical bodies lifeless within moments, without even requiring direct contact.

After the Autobots recovered it, Smokescreen carried the relic into their battle for the Omega Lock on Cybertron. He dropped it among Megatron’s Vehicons before phasing away, killing the surrounding troops and forcing Megatron to destroy the weapon with his Dark Star Saber to prevent its field from reaching him.

The Cybonic Plague is a Decepticon-engineered virus from Transformers Prime, developed under Megatron’s biological warfare program and responsible for millions of Cybertronian deaths during the war. Spread through contact with infected Energon, it gradually contaminates a Cybertronian’s fuel supply and can cause fatal system failure if a cure is not administered.

Its danger resurfaced when Optimus Prime contracted the dormant infection while investigating a centuries-old Autobot vessel that had crashed on Earth. With Ratchet unable to find a remedy, Bumblebee infiltrated Megatron’s subconscious through a Cortical Psychic Patch and recovered the formula from the Decepticon leader’s memories before Optimus’s condition became irreversible.

The Staff of Merlin is an ancient Cybertronian artifact that originated with Quintessa, who intended to use its power to restore her homeworld. Its ability to drain Unicron’s life energy made it central to her plan for restoring Cybertron, placing Earth in danger once its identity as Unicron became known.

After Quintessa brainwashed Optimus Prime, she ordered him to recover the artifact and bring it to Cybertron, where its power could extract Earth’s life energy. Megatron eventually seized the staff and delivered it to her, allowing the process to begin through the Knights’ submerged ship while the Autobots fought to stop her.

The Requiem Blaster is one of the formidable Mini-Con weapons in Transformers Armada, assembled from three members of the Space Mini-Con Team. Astroscope, Payload, and Sky Blast combine to form its cannon configuration, with Astroscope’s transdimensional research enabling it to draw energy from cosmic phenomena, including supernovas, quasars, and black holes.

Sky Blast summoned Astroscope and Payload with a song, awakening the trio’s combined form as the Autobots struggled against Tidal Wave. Optimus Prime then wielded the completed weapon, using its immense output to drive the Decepticon giant back and protect his allies during the desperate coastal battle for the Autobots’ survival.

The Star Saber is another powerful Mini-Con weapon featured in Transformers Armada, formed when Jetstorm, Runway, and Sonar combine their bodies into a single sword. Its power enhances whoever wields it with immense strength and destructive energy, making its three components coveted prizes throughout the war between the Autobots and Decepticons on Earth.

The sword’s reputation predates the modern conflict, as an ancient civilization collapsed after its people fought over possession of the assembled weapon. On Earth, Hot Shot became its first prominent wielder, using its enhanced power to overwhelm Decepticon opponents before the weapon passed into enemy hands through Sideways’s calculated betrayal.

Also in the Armada continuity, the Hydra Cannon is a colossal Mini-Con superweapon developed by the Decepticons during their campaign to leave Earth. Its firing system combines the Star Saber, Skyboom Shield, and Requiem Blaster, three legendary weapons formed by nine individual Mini-Cons, channeling their collective energy through one devastating discharge.

The Decepticons installed the weapon aboard a lunar warship, constructed by enslaved Mini-Cons under Megatron’s command and intended to transport his forces toward Cybertron after their time on Earth. During its first test, the cannon obliterated an approaching comet in seconds, prompting Megatron to order Starscream to target the Autobot base.

The Planet Buster is a moon-sized heat-ray cannon deployed by the Vok in Beast Wars, concealed within Earth’s second moon until the aliens judged their experiment compromised. Designed to ignite the Energon deposits scattered beneath the surface, its beam would trigger a chain reaction capable of tearing the entire planet apart.

The Vok channelled power from an alien installation on Earth, and the firing beam began disturbing the orbiting stasis pods as it charged. Optimus Primal commandeered a modified stasis pod, rigged its transwarp cell with explosives, and piloted it into the Planet Buster's core, sacrificing himself when Megatron’s sabotage prevented his escape.

The Star Harvester is an enormous machine featured in Revenge of the Fallen. It was built by the original Primes to convert a star’s energy into Energon for the AllSpark and their people. Its use was forbidden near inhabited worlds, since draining their suns would also endanger every civilization dependent upon those solar systems and nearby planets.

After the machine was constructed beneath ancient Egypt, the Fallen attempted to violate the Primes’ agreement by activating it to harvest Earth’s sun. His six fellow Primes confronted him, reclaimed the Matrix of Leadership, and sacrificed themselves to hide it inside a tomb built from their own bodies for millennia.
